\displaystyle\leq c\bigg(\Big\lvert\int_{-1}^{1}x\,\mathrm{d}(\mu_{n}-\mu)(x)\Big\rvert+2\E\Pi_{n}^{\prime\prime}+\E\Pi_{n}^{\prime}\int_{[-1,1]^{2}}\lvert x-y\rvert\,\mathrm{d}\nu(x,y)\rvert\bigg).

Taking the infimum over all couplings
\smash{\nu\in\Pr\big([-1,1]^{2}\big)}
with marginals
\smash{\overline{\mu}_{n}}
and
\smash{\overline{\mu}}
reveals that for
n
large enough,

\big\lvert\psi^{1}(\mu_{n})-\psi^{1}(\mu)\big\rvert\leq c\bigg(\Big\lvert\int_{-1}^{1}x\,\mathrm{d}(\mu_{n}-\mu)(x)\Big\rvert+2\big\lvert\mu_{n}[-1,1]-\mu[-1,1]\big\rvert+3\mu[-1,1]W\big(\overline{\mu}_{n},\overline{\mu}\big)\bigg),

where we have used that
\E\Pi_{n}^{\prime}\leq\E\Pi+\E\Pi_{n}\leq 3\mu[-1,1]
for
n
large enough as
\mu_{n}
converges weakly to
\mu
. Letting
n
tend to infinity and recalling that the Wasserstein distance (
3.10
) metrizes the weak convergence of probability measures completes the proof.
∎

Proposition 3.6
.

For any measure
\mu\in\mathcal{M}_{+}
, the sequence
(\psi_{N}(\mu))
converges to (
3.1
).

Proof.

Consider a sequence
(\mu_{n})_{n\geq 1}
of discrete measures such that
\mu_{n}[-1,1]=\mu[-1,1]
for all
n\geq 1
and
\overline{\mu}_{n}\to\overline{\mu}
with respect to the Wasserstein distance (
3.9
). By the triangle inequality and
Lemma

3.4
,

\displaystyle\big\lvert\psi(\mu)-\psi_{N}(\mu)\big\rvert

\displaystyle\leq\big\lvert\psi(\mu)-\psi(\mu_{n})\big\rvert+\big\lvert\psi(\mu_{n})-\psi_{N}(\mu_{n})\big\rvert+\big\lvert\psi_{N}(\mu_{n})-\psi_{N}(\mu)\big\rvert

\displaystyle\leq\big\lvert\psi(\mu)-\psi(\mu_{n})\big\rvert+\big\lvert\psi(\mu_{n})-\psi_{N}(\mu_{n})\big\rvert

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad\quad\,\,\,\,\,\,\,+c\big(1+\lvert\log(2c)\rvert+\lvert\log(c-\lvert\Delta\rvert)\rvert\big)W\big(\overline{\mu}_{n},\overline{\mu}\big)

where we have used the fact that
\mu_{n}[-1,1]=\mu[-1,1]
. Combining
Lemma

3.5
with
Lemma

3.1
to let
N\to\infty
and then
n\to\infty
completes the proof.
∎

This result identifies the initial condition for the infinite-dimensional Hamilton-Jacobi equation (
1.41
).
We may now leverage the main result in
[
31
]
to establish the well-posedness of this infinite-dimensional Hamilton-Jacobi equation. Before we do this, let us introduce the notation used in
[
31
]
; for more details and motivation regarding this notation, we encourage the reader to consult
[
31
]
.
Given an integer
K\geq 1
, we write

\mathcal{D}_{K}=\Big\{k=\frac{i}{2^{K}}\mid-2^{K}\leq i<2^{K}\Big\}

(3.16)

for the set of dyadic rationals on
[-1,1]
at scale
K
. We index vectors using the set of dyadic rationals, writing

\smash{x=(x_{k})_{k\in\mathcal{D}_{K}}\in\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}^{\mathcal{D}_{K}}}
for a non-negative sequence with values in
\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
indexed by the dyadic rationals
\mathcal{D}_{K}
. More generally, given two sets
\mathbb{A}
and
\mathcal{B}
, we write
\mathbb{A}^{\mathcal{B}}
for the set of functions from
\mathcal{B}
to
\mathbb{A}
.
We denote the set of discrete measures supported on the dyadic rationals at scale
K
in the interval
[-1,1]
by

\mathcal{M}^{(K)}_{+}=\Big\{\mu\in\mathcal{M}_{+}\mid\mu=\frac{1}{\lvert\mathcal{D}_{K}\rvert}\sum_{k\in\mathcal{D}_{K}}x_{k}\delta_{k}\text{ for some }x=(x_{k})_{k\in\mathcal{D}_{K}}\in\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}^{\mathcal{D}_{K}}\Big\},

(3.17)

and we project a general measure
\mu\in\mathcal{M}_{+}
onto
\mathcal{M}_{+}^{(K)}
via the mapping

x^{(K)}(\mu)=\big(\lvert\mathcal{D}_{K}\rvert\mu\big[k,k+2^{-K}\big)\big)_{k\in\mathcal{D}_{K}}\in\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}^{\mathcal{D}_{K}},

(3.18)

whose inverse assigns to each
\smash{x\in\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}^{\mathcal{D}_{K}}}
the measure

\mu^{(K)}_{x}=\frac{1}{\lvert\mathcal{D}_{K}\rvert}\sum_{k\in\mathcal{D}_{K}}x_{k}\delta_{k}\in\mathcal{M}_{+}^{(K)}.

(3.19)

We identify any real-valued function
\smash{f:\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}\times\mathcal{M}_{+}^{(K)}\to\mathbb{R}}
with the function

f^{(K)}(t,x)=f\big(t,\mu_{x}^{(K)}\big)

(3.20)

defined on
\smash{\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}\times\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}^{\mathcal{D}_{K}}}
, and we identify the projection of the initial condition with the function

\psi^{(K)}(x)=\psi\big(\mu_{x}^{(K)}\big)

(3.21)

defined on
\smash{\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}^{\mathcal{D}_{K}}}
. The Gateaux derivative of a real-valued function
\smash{f:\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}\times\mathcal{M}_{+}^{(K)}\to\mathbb{R}}
at the measure
\smash{\mu\in\mathcal{M}_{+}^{(K)}}
becomes the gradient
\smash{\lvert\mathcal{D}_{K}\rvert\nabla f^{(K)}(t,x^{(K)}(\mu))}
by duality. Indeed,

D_{\mu}f(t,\mu;\nu)=\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}\epsilon}\Big|_{\epsilon=0}f^{(K)}(t,x^{(K)}(\mu)+\epsilon x^{(K)}(\nu))=\nabla f^{(K)}(t,x^{(K)}(\mu))\cdot x^{(K)}(\nu)

(3.22)

for any direction
\smash{\nu\in\mathcal{M}_{+}^{(K)}}
. We fix
b>0
large enough so the modified kernel

\widetilde{g}_{b}(z)=g(z)+b

(3.23)

is strictly positive, and we introduce the symmetric matrices

G^{(K)}=\frac{1}{\lvert\mathcal{D}_{K}\rvert^{2}}\big(g(kk^{\prime})\big)_{k,k^{\prime}\in\mathcal{D}_{K}}\quad\text{and}\quad\widetilde{G}_{b}^{(K)}=\frac{1}{\lvert\mathcal{D}_{K}\rvert^{2}}\big(\widetilde{g}_{b}(kk^{\prime})\big)_{k,k^{\prime}\in\mathcal{D}_{K}}

(3.24)

in
\smash{\mathbb{R}^{\mathcal{D}_{K}\times\mathcal{D}_{K}}}
. We also define the projected cone

\widetilde{\mathcal{C}}_{b,K}=\Big\{\widetilde{G}_{b}^{(K)}x\in\mathbb{R}^{\mathcal{D}_{K}}\mid x\in\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}^{\mathcal{D}_{K}}\Big\},

(3.25)

the projected non-linearity
\widetilde{\mathsf{C}}_{b,K}:\widetilde{\mathcal{C}}_{b,K}\to\mathbb{R}
given by

\widetilde{\mathsf{C}}_{b,K}\big(\widetilde{G}^{(K)}_{b}x\big)=\frac{1}{2}\widetilde{G}_{b}^{(K)}x\cdot x=\frac{1}{2\lvert\mathcal{D}_{K}\rvert^{2}}\sum_{k,k^{\prime}\in\mathcal{D}_{K}}\widetilde{g}_{b}(kk^{\prime})x_{k}x_{k^{\prime}},

(3.26)

and the closed convex set

\mathcal{K}_{=1,K}=\Big\{G^{(K)}x\in\mathbb{R}^{\mathcal{D}_{K}}\mid x\in\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}^{\mathcal{D}_{K}}\text{ and }{|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|x|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|}_{1}=a\Big\}.

(3.27)

We will measure quantities in the normalized
\ell^{1}
and
\ell^{1,*}
norms,

{|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|x|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|}_{1}=\frac{1}{\lvert\mathcal{D}_{K}\rvert}\sum_{k\in\mathcal{D}_{K}}\lvert x_{k}\rvert\quad\text{and}\quad{|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|y|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|}_{1,*}=\max_{k\in\mathcal{D}_{K}}\lvert\mathcal{D}_{K}\rvert\lvert y_{k}\rvert,

(3.28)

and it will be convenient to write

B_{K,R}=\big\{y\in\mathbb{R}^{\mathcal{D}_{K}}\mid{|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|y|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|}_{1,*}\leq R\big\}

(3.29)

for the ball of radius
R>0
centered at the origin with respect to the normalized-
\ell^{1,*}
norm in
\smash{\mathbb{R}^{\mathcal{D}_{K}}}
. Recall that a function
\smash{h:\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}}
is said to be non-decreasing if
h(y)\leq h(y^{\prime})
for all
\smash{y,y^{\prime}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}}
with
\smash{y^{\prime}-y\in\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}^{d}}
. Proposition 2.3 in
[
31
]
will give the existence of a uniformly Lipschitz continuous and non-decreasing non-linearity
\smash{\widetilde{\mathsf{H}}_{b,K,R}}
which agrees with
\smash{\widetilde{\mathsf{C}}_{b,K}}
on the ball
\smash{\widetilde{\mathcal{C}}_{b,K}\cap B_{K,R}}
, and we will define the solution to the infinite-dimensional Hamilton-Jacobi equation (
1.41
) by

f(t,\mu)=\lim_{K\to\infty}\bigg(\widetilde{f}_{b,R}^{(K)}\big(t,x^{(K)}(\mu)\big)-b{|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|x^{(K)}(\mu)|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|}_{1}-\frac{bt}{2}\bigg),

(3.30)

where
\widetilde{f}^{(K)}_{b,R}:[0,\infty)\times\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}^{\mathcal{D}_{K}}\to\mathbb{R}
is the unique solution to the Hamilton-Jacobi equation

\partial_{t}\widetilde{f}^{(K)}(t,x)=\widetilde{\mathsf{H}}_{b,K,R}\big(\nabla\widetilde{f}^{(K)}(t,x)\big)\quad\text{on}\quad\mathbb{R}_{>0}\times\mathbb{R}_{>0}^{\mathcal{D}_{K}}

(3.31)

subject to the projection of the initial condition
\widetilde{\psi}_{b}:\mathcal{M}_{+}\to\mathbb{R}
defined by

\widetilde{\psi}_{b}(\mu)=\psi(\mu)+b\int_{-1}^{1}\,\mathrm{d}\mu.

(3.32)

The fact that the function
f
defined by (
3.30
) does not depend on the choice of the constants
b\in\mathbb{R}
and
R>0
sufficiently large is established in Proposition
3.14
, while the existence and uniqueness of the appropriate notion of solution to the Hamilton-Jacobi equation (
3.31
) is established in Proposition
3.12
. In order to prove these propositions, we will rely on some results from
[
31
]
. To appeal to these results, we must start by verifying that the initial condition
\psi
satisfies a certain number of hypotheses introduced there. To state these hypotheses, given a closed convex set
\smash{\mathcal{K}\subset\mathbb{R}^{\mathcal{D}_{K}}}
, write

\mathcal{K}^{\prime}=\mathcal{K}+B_{K,2^{-K/2}}

(3.33)

for the neighbourhood of radius
\smash{2^{-K/2}}
around
\mathcal{K}
in the normalized-
\ell^{1,*}
norm, and denote by

\mathrm{TV}(\mu,\nu)=\sup\big\{\lvert\mu(A)-\nu(A)\rvert\mid A\text{ is a measurable subset of }[-1,1]\big\}

(3.34)

the total variation distance on
\mathcal{M}_{+}
. A non-differential criterion for the gradient of a Lipschitz continuous function to lie in a closed convex set is given in Proposition B.2 of
[
31
]
.

H1

The initial condition
\psi:\mathcal{M}_{+}\to\mathbb{R}
is Lipschitz continuous with respect to the total variation distance (
3.34
),

\lvert\psi(\mu)-\psi(\nu)\rvert\leq\lVert\psi\rVert_{\mathrm{Lip},\mathrm{TV}}\mathrm{TV}(\mu,\nu)

(3.35)

for all measures
\nu,\mu\in\mathcal{M}_{+}
.

H2

The initial condition
\smash{\psi:\mathcal{M}_{+}\to\mathbb{R}}
has the property that each of the projected initial conditions has its gradient in the set
\mathcal{K}_{=1,K}^{\prime}
,

\nabla\psi^{(K)}\in L^{\infty}\big(\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}^{d};\mathcal{K}_{=1,K}^{\prime}\big).

(3.36)

H3

The initial condition
\psi:\Pr[-1,1]\to\mathbb{R}
is Lipschitz continuous with respect to the Wasserstein distance (
3.9
),

\lvert\psi(\mathbb{P})-\psi(\mathbb{Q})\rvert\leq\lVert\psi\rVert_{\mathrm{Lip},W}W(\mathbb{P},\mathbb{Q})

(3.37)

for all probability measures
\mathbb{P},\mathbb{Q}\in\Pr[-1,1]
.

Lemma 3.7
.

The initial condition
\psi
in (
3.1
) satisfies (
H1
)-(
H3
).

Proof.

Recall that
Lemma

3.3
implies the existence of a constant
C>0
which depends only on
c
and
\Delta
such that for every integer
N\geq 1
,
\mu\in\mathcal{M}_{+}
and
t\geq 0
,

\big\lvert D_{\mu}\overline{F}_{N}(t,\mu,x)\big\rvert\leq C\quad\text{and}\quad\big\lvert\partial_{x}D_{\mu}\overline{F}_{N}(t,\mu,x)\big\rvert\leq C.

(3.38)

To establish (
H1
) notice that for every integer
N\geq 1
and
\mu,\nu\in\mathcal{M}_{+}
,

\psi_{N}(\mu)-\psi_{N}(\nu)=\int_{0}^{1}D_{\mu}\psi_{N}\big(\nu+t(\mu-\nu);\mu-\nu\big)\,\mathrm{d}t=\int_{0}^{1}\int_{-1}^{1}f_{t}(x)\,\mathrm{d}\big(\mu-\nu\big)(x)\,\mathrm{d}t

for the continuously differentiable function
\smash{f_{t}(x)=D_{\mu}\psi_{N}\big(\nu+t(\mu-\nu),x\big)}
. To bound this integral by the total variation distance, let
\eta=\mu-\nu\in\mathcal{M}_{s}
, and use the Hahn-Jordan decomposition to write
\smash{\eta=\eta^{+}-\eta^{-}}
for measures
\smash{\eta^{+},\eta^{-}\in\mathcal{M}_{+}}
with the property that for some measurable set
D\subset[-1,1]
and all measurable sets
E\subset[-1,1]
,

\eta^{+}(E)=\eta(E\cap D)\geq 0\quad\text{and}\quad\eta^{-}(E)=-\eta(E\cap D^{c})\geq 0.

The triangle inequality and the first bound in (
3.38
) imply that

\displaystyle\lvert\psi_{N}(\mu)-\psi_{N}(\nu)\rvert

\displaystyle\leq\Big\lvert\int_{0}^{1}\int_{-1}^{1}f_{t}(x)\,\mathrm{d}\eta^{+}(x)\Big\rvert+\Big\lvert\int_{0}^{1}\int_{-1}^{1}f_{t}(x)\,\mathrm{d}\eta^{-}(x)\Big\rvert

\displaystyle\leq C\big(\eta^{+}[-1,1]+\eta^{-}[-1,1]\big)

\displaystyle\leq 2C\mathrm{TV}(\mu,\nu).

Using
Proposition

3.6
to let
N
tend to infinity establishes (
H1
). To prove (
H2
) notice that by (
3.22
) and (
2.34
), for every
\smash{y\in\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}^{\mathcal{D}_{K}}}
, there exists some probability measure
\mu^{*}\in\Pr[-1,1]
with

\partial_{x_{k}}\psi_{N}^{(K)}(y)=\frac{1}{\lvert\mathcal{D}_{K}\rvert}D_{\mu}\psi_{N}\big(\mu_{y}^{(K)},k\big)=\frac{1}{\lvert\mathcal{D}_{K}\rvert}G_{\mu^{*}}(k)+\BigO\big(N^{-1}\big).

If
\mu^{*}_{K}=\mu^{(K)}_{x^{(K)}(\mu^{*})}\in\mathcal{M}_{+}^{(K)}
denotes the projection of
\mu^{*}
onto
\mathcal{M}_{+}^{(K)}
, then the mean value theorem implies that

\lvert G_{\mu^{*}}(k)-G_{\mu^{*}_{K}}(k)\rvert\leq\sum_{k^{\prime}\in\mathcal{D}_{K}}\int_{k^{\prime}}^{k^{\prime}+2^{-K}}\lvert g(ky)-g(kk^{\prime})\rvert\,\mathrm{d}\mu^{*}(y)\leq\frac{\lVert g^{\prime}\rVert_{\infty}}{2^{K}},

where we have used that
\mu^{*}_{K}(k^{\prime})=\mu^{*}[k^{\prime},k^{\prime}+2^{-K})
for every dyadic
k^{\prime}\in\mathcal{D}_{K}
. This means that

\partial_{x_{k}}\psi_{N}^{(K)}(y)=\frac{1}{\lvert\mathcal{D}_{K}\rvert}G_{\mu^{*}_{K}}(k)+\BigO\big(2^{-2K}\big)+\BigO\big(N^{-1}\big)=G^{(K)}x^{(K)}(\mu^{*}_{K})_{k}+\BigO\big(2^{-2K}\big)+\BigO\big(N^{-1}\big)

so, for
K
large enough, we have
\smash{\nabla\psi^{(K)}_{N}(y)=w+\BigO\big(N^{-1}\big)}
for some
\smash{w\in\mathcal{K}^{\prime}_{=1,K}}
. At this point fix
c\in\mathbb{R}
and
\smash{x,x^{\prime}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}}
with
(x^{\prime}-x)\cdot z\geq c
for every
\smash{z\in\mathcal{K}_{=1,K}^{\prime}}
. The fundamental theorem of calculus reveals that

\psi_{N}^{(K)}(x^{\prime})-\psi_{N}^{(K)}(x)=\int_{0}^{1}\nabla\psi_{N}^{(K)}\big(tx^{\prime}+(1-t)x\big)\cdot(x^{\prime}-x)\,\mathrm{d}t\geq c+\BigO\big(N^{-1}\big).

Using
Proposition

3.6
to let
N
tend to infinity shows that
\smash{\psi^{(K)}(x^{\prime})-\psi^{(K)}(x)\geq c}
and Proposition B.2 in
[
31
]
gives (
H2
). Finally, (
H3
) is a consequence of
Lemma

3.4
and
Proposition

3.6
. This completes the proof.
∎

This result allows us to invoke Proposition 2.3 in
[
31
]
to extend the non-linearity
\smash{\widetilde{\mathsf{C}}_{b,K}}
in (
3.26
). It will be convenient to write
M_{b}=\max_{[-1,1]}\widetilde{g}_{b}
and
m_{b}=\min_{[-1,1]}\widetilde{g}_{b}>0
.

Proposition 3.8
.

For every
R>0
, there exists a non-decreasing non-linearity
\smash{\widetilde{\mathsf{H}}_{b,K,R}:\mathbb{R}^{\mathcal{D}_{K}}\to\mathbb{R}}
which agrees with
\smash{\widetilde{\mathsf{C}}_{b,K}}
on
\smash{\widetilde{\mathcal{C}}_{b,K}\cap B_{K,R}}
and satisfies the Lipschitz continuity property

\big\lvert\widetilde{\mathsf{H}}_{b,K,R}(y)-\widetilde{\mathsf{H}}_{b,K,R}(y^{\prime})\big\rvert\leq\frac{8RM_{b}}{m_{b}^{2}}{|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|y-y^{\prime}|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|}_{1,*}

(3.39)

for all
y,y^{\prime}\in\mathbb{R}^{\mathcal{D}_{K}}
.

The well-posedness of the Hamilton-Jacobi equation (
3.31
) is the content of Theorem 1.1 in
[
31
]
. Before stating this result, let us remind the reader of the notion of a viscosity solution and introduce some more notation.

Definition 3.9
.

An upper semi-continuous function
\smash{u:[0,\infty)\times\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}^{\mathcal{D}_{K}}\to\mathbb{R}}
is said to be a viscosity subsolution to (
3.31
) if, given any
\smash{\phi\in C^{\infty}\big((0,\infty)\times\mathbb{R}_{>0}^{\mathcal{D}_{K}}\big)}
with the property that
u-\phi
has a local maximum at
\smash{(t^{*},x^{*})\in(0,\infty)\times\mathbb{R}_{>0}^{\mathcal{D}_{K}}}
,

\big(\partial_{t}\phi-\widetilde{\mathsf{H}}_{b,K,R}(\nabla\phi)\big)(t^{*},x^{*})\leq 0.

(3.40)

Definition 3.10
.

A lower semi-continuous function
\smash{v:[0,\infty)\times\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}^{\mathcal{D}_{K}}\to\mathbb{R}}
is said to be a viscosity supersolution to (
3.31
) if, given any
\smash{\phi\in C^{\infty}\big((0,\infty)\times\mathbb{R}_{>0}^{\mathcal{D}_{K}}\big)}
with the property that
v-\phi
has a local minimum at
\smash{(t^{*},x^{*})\in(0,\infty)\times\mathbb{R}_{>0}^{\mathcal{D}_{K}}}
,

\big(\partial_{t}\phi-\widetilde{\mathsf{H}}_{b,K,R}(\nabla\phi)\big)(t^{*},x^{*})\geq 0.

(3.41)

Definition 3.11
.

A continuous function
\smash{f\in C\big([0,\infty)\times\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}^{\mathcal{D}_{K}}\big)}
is said to be a viscosity solution to (
3.31
) if it is both a viscosity subsolution and a viscosity supersolution to (
3.31
).

Given functions
h:\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}^{\mathcal{D}_{K}}\to\mathbb{R}
and
\smash{u:[0,\infty)\times\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}^{\mathcal{D}_{K}}\to\mathbb{R}}
, define the semi-norms

{|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|h|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|}_{\mathrm{Lip},1}=\sup_{x\neq x^{\prime}\in\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}^{\mathcal{D}_{K}}}\frac{\lvert h(x)-h(x^{\prime})\rvert}{{|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|x-x^{\prime}|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|}_{1}}\quad\text{and}\quad[u]_{0}=\sup_{\begin{subarray}{c}t>0\\
x\in\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}^{\mathcal{D}_{K}}\end{subarray}}\frac{\lvert u(t,x)-u(0,x)\rvert}{t}.

(3.42)

Introduce the space of functions with Lipschitz initial condition that grow at most linearly in time,

\mathfrak{L}=\big\{u:[0,\infty)\times\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}^{\mathcal{D}_{K}}\to\mathbb{R}\mid u(0,\cdot)\text{ is Lipschitz continuous and }[u]_{0}<\infty\big\},

(3.43)

and its subset of uniformly Lipschitz functions,

\mathfrak{L}_{\mathrm{unif}}=\Big\{u\in\mathfrak{L}\mid\sup_{t\geq 0}{|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|u(t,\cdot)|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|}_{\mathrm{Lip},1}<\infty\Big\}.

(3.44)

Combining Theorem 1.1 with the arguments leading to the second conclusion of Lemma 6.1 in
[
31
]
gives the following well-posedness result for the Hamilton-Jacobi equation (
3.31
).

Proposition 3.12
.

For every
R>0
, the Hamilton-Jacobi equation (
3.31
) admits a unique viscosity solution
\smash{\widetilde{f}^{(K)}_{b,R}\in\mathfrak{L}_{\mathrm{unif}}}
subject to the initial condition
\smash{\widetilde{\psi}_{b}^{(K)}}
. Moreover, the solution has its gradient in the closed convex set
\smash{\widetilde{\mathcal{K}}_{=1,K}^{\prime}}
,

\nabla\widetilde{f}^{(K)}_{b,R}\in L^{\infty}\big([0,\infty)\times\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}^{\mathcal{D}_{K}};\widetilde{\mathcal{K}}_{=1,K}^{\prime}\big),

(3.45)

and it satisfies the Lipschitz bound

\sup_{t>0}{|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|\widetilde{f}_{b,R}^{(K)}(t,\cdot)|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|}_{\mathrm{Lip},1}={|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|\widetilde{\psi}_{b}^{(K)}|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|}_{\mathrm{Lip},1}\leq\lVert\widetilde{\psi}_{b}\rVert_{\mathrm{Lip},\mathrm{TV}}.

(3.46)

In addition to this existence and uniqueness result, it will be important to record the following comparison principle for the projected Hamilton-Jacobi equation (
3.31
). This comparison principle is a consequence of Corollary A.12 in
[
31
]
.

Lemma 3.13
.

If
\smash{u,v\in\mathfrak{L}_{\mathrm{unif}}}
are respectively a continuous subsolution and a continuous supersolution to (
3.31
), then

\sup_{\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}\times\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}^{\mathcal{D}_{K}}}\big(u(t,x)-v(t,x)\big)=\sup_{\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}^{\mathcal{D}_{K}}}\big(u(0,x)-v(0,x)\big).

(3.47)

The existence of the limit (
3.30
) defining
f(t,\mu)
is a consequence of Theorems 1.2 and 1.4 in
[
31
]
.

Proposition 3.14
.

Let
b\in\mathbb{R}
be such that the kernel
\smash{\widetilde{g}_{b}}
defined in (
3.23
) is positive on
[-1,1]
, let
\smash{\widetilde{\psi}_{b}}
be defined by (
3.32
), and for each integer
K\geq 1
and
\smash{R>\lVert\widetilde{\psi}_{b}\rVert_{\mathrm{Lip},\mathrm{TV}}}
, denote by
\smash{\widetilde{f}_{b,R}^{(K)}}
the unique viscosity solution to the Hamilton-Jacobi equation (
3.31
) subject to the initial condition
\smash{\widetilde{\psi}_{b}^{(K)}}
. The limit (
3.30
) exists, is finite and is independent of
R
and
b
. This limit is defined to be the solution to the infinite-dimensional Hamilton-Jacobi equation (
1.41
).

To establish
Theorem

1.1
, the idea will be to show that the enriched free energy (
1.35
) is essentially a viscosity subsolution to the infinite-dimensional Hamilton-Jacobi equation (
1.41
).

4
The free energy upper bound

In this section, we combine the computations in
Section

2
with the arguments in
[
57
]
to essentially show that the enriched free energy (
1.35
) is a viscosity subsolution to the infinite-dimensional Hamilton-Jacobi equation (
1.41
). This equation is given a precise meaning by
Proposition

3.14
. As alluded to in
Section

2
, we will perturb the enriched Hamiltonian (
1.34
) to enforce the concentration of all the multi-overlaps (
2.11
) without changing the limit of the associated free energy. The perturbation Hamiltonian we will now describe was introduced in
[
14
]
to prove a general multi-overlap concentration result whose finitary version we establish in
Appendix

C
.

Fix an integer
K_{+}
which will be chosen sufficiently large in the course of this section, and write
\lambda=(\lambda_{0},\lambda_{1},\ldots,\lambda_{K_{+}})
for a perturbation parameter with
\lambda_{0}\in[1/2,1]
and
\lambda_{k}\in[2^{-k-1},2^{-k}]
for
1\leq k\leq K_{+}
. Given a sequence
(\epsilon_{N})
with
\epsilon_{N}=N^{\gamma}
for some
-1/8<\gamma<0
and a standard Gaussian vector
Z_{0}=(Z_{0,1},\ldots,Z_{0,N})
in
\mathbb{R}^{N}
, introduce the Gaussian perturbation Hamiltonian

H_{N}^{\mathrm{gauss}}(\sigma,\lambda_{0})=\mathcal{H}_{0}=\sum_{i\leq N}\big(\lambda_{0}\epsilon_{N}\sigma^{*}_{i}\sigma_{i}+\sqrt{\lambda_{0}\epsilon_{N}}Z_{0,i}\sigma_{i}\big)

(4.1)

associated with the task of recovering the signal
\sigma^{*}
from the data

Y^{\mathrm{gauss}}=\sqrt{\lambda_{0}\epsilon_{N}}\sigma^{*}+Z_{0}.

(4.2)

Notice that

1\geq\epsilon_{N}\to 0\quad\text{and}\quad N\epsilon_{N}\to\infty.

(4.3)

Similarly, consider a sequence
(s_{N})
with
s_{N}=N^{\eta}
for
4/5<\eta<1
in such a way that

\frac{s_{N}}{N}\to 0\quad\text{and}\quad\frac{s_{N}}{\sqrt{N}}\to\infty.

(4.4)

Fix a sequence of i.i.d. random variables
(\pi_{k})
with
\Poi(s_{N})
distribution as well as a sequence
e=(e_{jk})
of random variables with
\Exp(1)
distribution.
For every
j\leq\pi_{k}
, sample i.i.d. random indices
i_{jk}
uniformly from the set
\{1,\ldots,N\}
, and define the exponential perturbation Hamiltonian by

\mathcal{H}_{k}=\sum_{j\leq\pi_{k}}\Big(\log(1+\lambda_{k}\sigma_{i_{jk}}\big)-\frac{\lambda_{k}e_{jk}\sigma_{i_{jk}}}{1+\lambda_{k}\sigma_{i_{jk}}^{*}}\Big)\quad\text{and}\quad H_{N}^{\mathrm{exp}}(\sigma)=\sum_{1\leq k\leq K_{+}}\mathcal{H}_{k}.

(4.5)

Observe that this is the Hamiltonian associated with the task of recovering the signal
\sigma^{*}
from the independently generated data

Y_{jk}^{\mathrm{exp}}=\frac{e_{jk}}{1+\lambda_{k}\sigma^{*}_{i_{jk}}}

(4.6)

for
j\leq\pi_{k}
and
k\geq 1
. Introduce the perturbed Hamiltonian

H_{N}(\sigma,\lambda)=H_{N}^{t,\mu}(\sigma)+H_{N}^{\mathrm{gauss}}(\sigma,\lambda_{0})+H_{N}^{\mathrm{exp}}(\sigma,\lambda)

(4.7)

as well as its associated free energy

\overline{F}_{N}^{\text{pert}}(t,\mu,\lambda)=\frac{1}{N}\E\log\int_{\Sigma_{N}}\exp H_{N}(\sigma,\lambda)\,\mathrm{d}P_{N}^{*}(\sigma).

(4.8)

Since the Gibbs measure associated with the Hamiltonian in (
4.7
) is still a conditional expectation as in (
2.2
), it will still satisfy the Nishimori identity (
2.3
). An essential property of the perturbation Hamiltonians (
4.1
) and (
4.5
) is that they do not affect the asymptotic behavior of the enriched free energy (
1.35
).

Lemma 4.1
.

For every
t>0
,
\mu\in\mathcal{M}_{+}
and
\lambda
, the enriched free energy (
1.35
) and the perturbed free energy (
4.8
) are asymptotically equivalent,

\lim_{N\to\infty}\big\lvert\overline{F}_{N}^{\mathrm{pert}}(t,\mu,\lambda)-\overline{F}_{N}(t,\mu)\big\rvert=0.

(4.9)

Proof.

A direct computation reveals that

\big\lvert\overline{F}_{N}^{\mathrm{pert}}(t,\mu,\lambda)-\overline{F}_{N}(t,\mu)\big\rvert\leq\frac{1}{N}\E\max_{\sigma\in\Sigma_{N}}\big\lvert H_{N}^{\mathrm{gauss}}(\sigma,\lambda_{0})\big\rvert+\frac{1}{N}\E\max_{\sigma\in\Sigma_{N}}\big\lvert H_{N}^{\mathrm{exp}}(\sigma,\lambda)\big\rvert.

For any spin configuration
\sigma\in\Sigma_{N}
,

\big\lvert H_{N}^{\mathrm{gauss}}(\sigma,\lambda_{0})\big\rvert\leq N\epsilon_{N}+\sqrt{\epsilon_{N}}\sum_{i\leq N}\lvert Z_{0,i}\rvert

while

\big\lvert H_{N}^{\mathrm{exp}}(\sigma,\lambda)\big\rvert\leq\sum_{1\leq k\leq K^{\prime}}\sum_{j\leq\pi_{k}}\Big(\log(1+\lambda_{k})+\frac{\lambda_{k}e_{jk}}{1-\lambda_{k}}\Big).

Since these bounds are uniform in
\sigma
, it follows that

\big\lvert\overline{F}_{N}^{\mathrm{pert}}(t,\mu,\lambda)-\overline{F}_{N}(t,\mu)\big\rvert\leq\epsilon_{N}+\sqrt{\epsilon_{N}}\E Z_{0,1}+\frac{s_{N}}{N}\sum_{k\geq 1}\Big(\log(1+\lambda_{k})+\frac{\lambda_{k}}{1-\lambda_{k}}\Big).

The third term was obtained by taking the expectation with respect to the randomness of
e
first and then with respect to the randomness of
(\pi_{k})
. Leveraging (
4.3
) and (
4.4
) to let
N
tend to infinity completes the proof.
∎

With this result in mind, we abuse notation and redefine the perturbed free energy (
4.8
),

F_{N}(t,\mu,\lambda)=\frac{1}{N}\log\int_{\Sigma_{N}}\exp H_{N}(\sigma,\lambda)\,\mathrm{d}P_{N}^{*}(\sigma)\quad\text{and}\quad\overline{F}_{N}(t,\mu,\lambda)=\E F_{N}(t,\mu,\lambda).

(4.10)

For every integer
K\geq 1
and
x\in\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}^{\mathcal{D}_{K}}
, we denote by

F_{N}^{(K)}(t,x,\lambda)=F_{N}\big(t,\mu_{x}^{(K)},\lambda\big)\quad\text{and}\quad\overline{F}_{N}^{(K)}(t,x,\lambda)=\E F_{N}^{(K)}(t,x,\lambda)

(4.11)

the finite-dimensional projections of these perturbed free energy functionals. In the same spirit as (
3.30
), given
b\in\mathbb{R}
such that the kernel
\widetilde{g}_{b}
defined in (
3.23
) is positive on
[-1,1]
, introduce translated versions of these free energy functionals,

F_{N}^{\prime}(t,\mu,\lambda)=F_{N}(t,\mu,\lambda)+b\int_{-1}^{1}\,\mathrm{d}\mu+\frac{bt}{2}\quad\text{and}\quad\overline{F}_{N}^{\prime}(t,\mu,\lambda)=\E F_{N}^{\prime}(t,\mu,\lambda).

(4.12)

For every integer
K\geq 1
and
x\in\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}^{\mathcal{D}_{K}}
, we denote by

F_{N}^{\prime(K)}(t,x,\lambda)=F_{N}^{\prime}\big(t,\mu_{x}^{(K)},\lambda\big)\quad\text{and}\quad\overline{F}_{N}^{\prime(K)}(t,x,\lambda)=\E F_{N}^{\prime(K)}(t,x,\lambda)

(4.13)

the finite-dimensional projections of these modified free energy functionals. Similarly, we write

\overline{F}_{N}^{(K)}(t,x)=\overline{F}_{N}\big(t,\mu_{x}^{(K)}\big)\quad\text{and}\quad\overline{F}_{N}^{\prime(K)}(t,x)=\overline{F}_{N}^{(K)}(t,x)+b{|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|x|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|}_{1}+\frac{bt}{2}

(4.14)

for the finite-dimensional projections of the enriched free energy (
4.12
) and its translation according to (
3.30
). Combining Lemmas
2.1
,
2.4
and
3.1
with the Arzela-Ascoli theorem, it is possible to extract a subsequential limit
\smash{\widetilde{F}^{(K)}}
from the sequence defined by (
4.14
) for varying
N
. Passing to a further subsequence and using a diagonalization argument, it is also possible to ensure that

\widetilde{F}^{(K)}(t,x)=\limsup_{N\to\infty}\overline{F}_{N}^{\prime(K)}(t,x)

(4.15)

for all
\smash{(t,x)\in(0,\infty)\times\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}^{\mathcal{D}_{K}}}
. The key to establishing
Theorem

1.1
will be to show that, in some sense, the subsequential limit
\smash{\widetilde{F}^{(K)}}
is an approximate subsolution to the Hamilton-Jacobi equation (
3.31
) for some
\smash{R>\lVert\widetilde{\psi}_{b}\rVert_{\mathrm{Lip},\mathrm{TV}}+\lVert\widetilde{g}_{b}\rVert_{\infty}+\lVert\widetilde{g}_{b}^{\prime}\rVert_{\infty}+1}
which will remain fixed throughout this section.

We fix a smooth function
\smash{\phi\in C^{\infty}\big((0,\infty)\times\mathbb{R}_{>0}^{\mathcal{D}_{K}}\big)}
with the property that the difference
\smash{\widetilde{F}^{(K)}-\phi}
achieves a local maximum at some point
\smash{(t_{\infty},x_{\infty})\in(0,\infty)\times\mathbb{R}_{>0}^{\mathcal{D}_{K}}}
. Recalling that the index
K_{+}
controls the number of terms in the perturbation Hamiltonian (
4.5
), we introduce the parameter

\lambda_{\infty}=\frac{\big(1,2^{-1},2^{-2},\ldots,2^{-K_{+}}\big)+\big(2^{-1},2^{-2},2^{-3},\ldots,2^{-K_{+}-1}\big)}{2}

(4.16)

as well as the smooth function

\widetilde{\phi}(t,x,\lambda)=\phi(t,x)+(t-t_{\infty})^{2}+\lVert x-x_{\infty}\rVert_{2}^{2}+\lVert\lambda-\lambda_{\infty}\rVert_{2}^{2}.

(4.17)

It is clear that
\smash{(t,x,\lambda)\mapsto\widetilde{F}^{(K)}(t,x)-\widetilde{\phi}(t,x,\lambda)}
has a strict local maximum at
(t_{\infty},x_{\infty},\lambda_{\infty})
. Arguing as in the proof of
Lemma

4.1
shows that
\smash{(t,x,\lambda)\mapsto\overline{F}_{N}^{\prime(K)}(t,x,\lambda)}
converges to
\smash{(t,x,\lambda)\mapsto\widetilde{F}^{(K)}(t,x)}
locally uniformly. It is therefore possible to find a sequence
(t_{N},x_{N},\lambda_{N})
which converges to the point
(t_{\infty},x_{\infty},\lambda_{\infty})
and has the property that
\smash{(t,x,\lambda)\mapsto\overline{F}_{N}^{\prime(K)}(t,x,\lambda)-\widetilde{\phi}(t,x,\lambda)}
attains a local maximum at
(t_{N},x_{N},\lambda_{N})
. More precisely, it is possible to find a constant
C<\infty
which is allowed to depend on
K
,
K_{+}
,
t_{\infty}
,
x_{\infty}
and the function
\phi
such that

\displaystyle\Big(\overline{F}_{N}^{\prime(K)}-\widetilde{\phi}\Big)

\displaystyle(t_{N},x_{N},\lambda_{N})

\displaystyle=\sup\Big\{\Big(\overline{F}_{N}^{\prime(K)}-\widetilde{\phi}\Big)(t,x,\lambda)\mid\lvert t-t_{N}\rvert+\lVert x-x_{N}\rVert_{2}+\lVert\lambda-\lambda_{N}\rVert_{2}\leq C^{-1}\Big\}.

(4.18)

We will use such a constant
C<\infty
at various places in this proof, and we understand that its value may need to be increased as we proceed; the important point is that it does not depend on
N
.
The choices of
\lambda_{\infty}
in (
4.16
) and
\smash{x_{\infty}\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}^{\mathcal{D}_{K}}}
ensure that when
N
is large enough
(\lambda_{N})_{k}\in(2^{-k-1},2^{-k})
for
0\leq k\leq K_{+}
and
\smash{x_{N}\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}^{\mathcal{D}_{K}}}
. Increasing
C<\infty
if necessary, it is therefore possible to guarantee that for
N
large enough the supremum on the right-hand side of (
4
) is taken over triples
(t,x,\lambda)
with
t>0
,
\smash{x\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}^{\mathcal{D}_{K}}}
and
\lambda_{k}\in[2^{-k-1},2^{-k}]
for
1\leq k\leq K_{+}
. It follows that

\partial_{t}\Big(\overline{F}_{N}^{\prime(K)}-\widetilde{\phi}\Big)(t_{N},x_{N},\lambda_{N})=0,\qquad\nabla_{x}\Big(\overline{F}_{N}^{\prime(K)}-\widetilde{\phi}\Big)(t_{N},x_{N},\lambda_{N})=0

(4.19)

and

\nabla_{\lambda}\Big(\overline{F}_{N}^{(K)}-\widetilde{\phi}\Big)(t_{N},x_{N},\lambda_{N})=\nabla_{\lambda}\Big(\overline{F}_{N}^{\prime(K)}-\widetilde{\phi}\Big)(t_{N},x_{N},\lambda_{N})=0.

(4.20)

The majority of this section will be devoted to using the second equality in (
4.20
) in conjunction with the main result in
Appendix

C
to show the concentration of a finite but very large number of the multi-overlaps (
2.11
). We will then combine this finitary multi-overlap concentration result with the computations in
Section

2
to establish the following crucial result.

Lemma 4.2
.

Fix
\smash{R>\lVert\widetilde{\psi}_{b}\rVert_{\mathrm{Lip},\mathrm{TV}}+\lVert\widetilde{g}_{b}\rVert_{\infty}+\lVert\widetilde{g}_{b}^{\prime}\rVert_{\infty}+1}
. For every
\epsilon>0
, there exists a choice of integer
K_{+}\geq 1
in the perturbed Hamiltonian (
4.7
) with the property that for any integer
K\geq 1
, it is possible to find a constant
\smash{\mathcal{E}_{\epsilon,K}}
with

\limsup_{N\to\infty}\Big\lvert\Big(\partial_{t}\overline{F}_{N}^{\prime(K)}-\widetilde{\mathsf{H}}_{b,K,R}\Big(\nabla_{x}\overline{F}_{N}^{\prime(K)}\Big)\Big)(t_{N},x_{N},\lambda_{N})\Big\rvert\leq\mathcal{E}_{\epsilon,K}

(4.21)

and
\lim_{\epsilon\to 0}\lim_{K\to\infty}\mathcal{E}_{\epsilon,K}=0
.

For the time being, let us prove
Theorem

1.1
assuming
Lemma

4.2
.

Proof of
Theorem

1.1
assuming
Lemma

4.2
.

Given
\epsilon>0
, invoke
Lemma

4.2
to find an integer
K_{+}\geq 1
in the perturbed Hamiltonian (
4.7
) with the property that for any integer
K\geq 1
, it is possible to find a constant
\smash{\mathcal{E}_{\epsilon,K}}
with

\limsup_{N\to\infty}\Big\lvert\Big(\partial_{t}\overline{F}_{N}^{\prime(K)}-\widetilde{\mathsf{H}}_{b,K,R}\Big(\nabla_{x}\overline{F}_{N}^{\prime(K)}\Big)\Big)(t_{N},x_{N},\lambda_{N})\Big\rvert\leq\mathcal{E}_{\epsilon,K}

(4.22)

and
\lim_{\epsilon\to 0}\lim_{K\to\infty}\mathcal{E}_{\epsilon,K}=0
. Given an integer
K\geq 1
, the idea will be to show that the test function
\smash{\phi\in C^{\infty}\big((0,\infty)\times\mathbb{R}_{>0}^{\mathcal{D}_{K}}\big)}
introduced above satisfies the subsolution condition in (
3.40
) for the non-linearity
\smash{\widetilde{\mathsf{H}}_{b,K,R}}
at the point of contact
(t_{\infty},x_{\infty})
up to the small error
\mathcal{E}_{\epsilon,K}
. This will mean that the subsequential limit
\smash{\widetilde{F}^{(K)}}
of the modified free energy (
4.14
) is a viscosity subsolution to the Hamilton-Jacobi equation (
3.31
) up to a small error. More precisely, the function

\widetilde{F}^{(K)}_{\epsilon}=\widetilde{F}^{(K)}-t\mathcal{E}_{\epsilon,K}

(4.23)

will be a viscosity subsolution to (
3.31
). This observation will allow us to leverage the comparison principle in
Lemma

3.13
to bound the limit superior of the enriched free energy (
1.35
) by the solution
f
to the infinite-dimensional Hamilton-Jacobi equation (
1.41
) constructed in
Proposition

3.14
. We proceed in two steps.

Step 1:
\widetilde{F}_{\epsilon}^{(K)}
subsolution.

Since
\smash{x_{N}\to x_{\infty}}
assume without loss of generality that
\smash{(x_{N})\subset\mathbb{R}_{>0}^{\mathcal{D}_{K}}}
. It follows by (
4.19
) that

\big(\partial_{t}\widetilde{\phi}-\widetilde{\mathsf{H}}_{b,K,R}\big(\nabla_{x}\widetilde{\phi}\big)\big)(t_{N},x_{N},\lambda_{N})=\Big(\partial_{t}\overline{F}_{N}^{\prime(K)}-\widetilde{\mathsf{H}}_{b,K,R}\Big(\nabla_{x}\overline{F}_{N}^{\prime(K)}\Big)\Big)(t_{N},x_{N},\lambda_{N}),

so letting
N
tend to infinity and combining the definition of
\widetilde{\phi}
with (
4.22
) yields

\big(\partial_{t}\widetilde{\phi}-\widetilde{\mathsf{H}}_{b,K,R}\big(\nabla_{x}\widetilde{\phi}\big)\big)(t_{\infty},x_{\infty})\leq\mathcal{E}_{\epsilon,K}.

This shows that the function (
4.23
) satisfies the subsolution condition in (
3.40
).

Step 2: comparison principle.

The comparison principle in
Lemma

3.13
gives the upper bound

\widetilde{F}^{(K)}(t,x)\leq\widetilde{f}^{(K)}_{b,R}(t,x)+t\mathcal{E}_{\epsilon,K}.

(4.24)

We have implicitly used that
\smash{\widetilde{F}^{(K)}}
and
\smash{\widetilde{f}_{b,R}^{(K)}}
are continuous and have the same initial condition by
Proposition

3.6
. We have also used that they both belong to the solution space
\smash{\mathfrak{L}_{\mathrm{unif}}}
by
Proposition

3.12
, (
2.1
), (
2.4
) and a simple application of the mean value theorem. With (
4.24
) in mind, fix a finite measure
\mu\in\mathcal{M}_{+}
, and introduce the discrete measure

\mu^{(K)}=\mu^{(K)}_{x^{(K)}(\mu)}

defined in (
3.19
).
It is readily verified that
\smash{\overline{\mu}^{(K)}\to\overline{\mu}}
with respect to the Wasserstein distance (
3.9
). Moreover, an identical argument to that in
Lemma

3.4
leveraging the second bound in (
3.13
) reveals that

{\overline{F}_{N}}(t,\mu)\leq C^{\prime}\mu[-1,1]W\big(\overline{\mu},\overline{\mu}^{(K)}\big)+\overline{F}_{N}^{(K)}\big(t,x^{(K)}(\mu)\big)

for some constant
C^{\prime}
that depends only on
c
. We use the letter
C^{\prime}
instead of
C
to emphasize that the constant
C^{\prime}
does not depend on
K
.
Letting
N
tend to infinity, recalling (
4.14
), (
4.15
) and leveraging (
4.24
) yields

\limsup_{N\to\infty}\overline{F}_{N}(t,\mu)\leq C^{\prime}\mu[-1,1]W\big(\overline{\mu},\overline{\mu}^{(K)}\big)+\widetilde{f}_{b,R}^{(K)}\big(t,x^{(K)}(\mu)\big)-b{|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|x^{(K)}(\mu)|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|}_{1}-\frac{bt}{2}+t\mathcal{E}_{\epsilon,K}.

Invoking
Lemma

4.2
and
Proposition

3.14
to let
K
tend to infinity and then
\epsilon
tend to zero completes the proof.
∎

The rest of this section is devoted to the proof of
Lemma

4.2
which will be obtained by combining the computations of
Section

2
with the main result of
Appendix

C
to show the concentration of a finite but very large number of the multi-overlaps (
2.11
). In the notation of
[
14
]
, for any perturbation parameter
\lambda
, let

\lambda_{0,N}=\epsilon_{N}\lambda_{0},

(4.25)

and introduce the quantities

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{0}

\displaystyle=\frac{\mathcal{H}_{0}^{\prime}}{N\epsilon_{N}},\quad\text{ where }\quad\mathcal{H}_{0}^{\prime}=\partial_{\lambda_{0}}H_{N}^{\mathrm{gauss}}(\sigma,\lambda_{0})=\epsilon_{N}\bigg(\sigma\cdot\sigma^{*}+\frac{\sigma\cdot Z_{0}}{2\sqrt{\lambda_{0,N}}}\bigg),

(4.26)

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{k}

\displaystyle=\frac{\mathcal{H}_{k}^{\prime}}{s_{N}},\quad\text{ where }\quad\mathcal{H}_{k}^{\prime}=\partial_{\lambda_{k}}H_{N}^{\mathrm{exp}}(\sigma,\lambda)=\sum_{j\leq\pi_{k}}\sigma_{i_{jk}}\bigg(\frac{1}{1+\lambda_{k}\sigma_{i_{jk}}}-\frac{e_{jk}}{(1+\lambda_{k}\sigma^{*}_{i_{jk}})^{2}}\bigg)

(4.27)

for
1\leq k\leq K_{+}
. The importance of these quantities stems from the fact that

\displaystyle\partial_{\lambda_{0}}F_{N}^{(K)}(t,x,\lambda)=\frac{1}{N}\big\langle\mathcal{H}_{0}^{\prime}\big\rangle,

\displaystyle\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\partial^{2}_{\lambda_{0}}F_{N}^{(K)}(t,x,\lambda)=\frac{1}{N}\Big(\big\langle(\mathcal{H}_{0}^{\prime}-\langle\mathcal{H}_{0}^{\prime}\rangle)^{2}\big\rangle-\frac{\epsilon_{N}^{2}}{4\lambda_{0,N}^{3/2}}\langle\sigma\rangle\cdot Z_{0}\Big),

(4.28)

\displaystyle\partial_{\lambda_{k}}F_{N}^{(K)}(t,x,\lambda)=\frac{1}{N}\big\langle\mathcal{H}_{k}^{\prime}\big\rangle,

\displaystyle\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\partial^{2}_{\lambda_{k}}F_{N}^{(K)}(t,x,\lambda)=\frac{1}{N}\big(\big\langle(\mathcal{H}_{k}^{\prime}-\langle\mathcal{H}_{k}^{\prime}\rangle)^{2}\big\rangle+\big\langle\mathcal{H}_{k}^{\prime\prime}\big\rangle\big)

(4.29)

for
1\leq k\leq K_{+}
while

\partial_{\lambda_{k}\lambda_{j}}F_{N}^{(K)}(t,x,\lambda)=\frac{1}{N}\big(\langle\mathcal{H}^{\prime}_{j}\mathcal{H}^{\prime}_{k}\rangle-\langle\mathcal{H}^{\prime}_{j}\rangle\langle\mathcal{H}^{\prime}_{k}\rangle\big)

(4.30)

for
0\leq j\neq k\leq K_{+}
.
Here, and for the remainder of this section, the Gibbs average
\langle\cdot\rangle
will always be associated with the perturbed Hamiltonian (
4.7
) evaluated at a triple
(t,x,\lambda)
which will be clear from the context. It will also be convenient to record that for
1\leq k\leq K_{+}
,

\mathcal{H}_{k}^{\prime\prime}=\partial_{\lambda_{k}}^{2}H_{N}^{\mathrm{exp}}(\sigma,\lambda)=\sum_{j\leq\pi_{k}}\bigg(-\frac{1}{(1+\lambda_{k}\sigma_{i_{jk}})^{2}}+2\frac{\sigma_{i_{jk}}\sigma^{*}_{i_{jk}}e_{jk}}{(1+\lambda_{k}\sigma^{*}_{i_{jk}})^{3}}\bigg)\text{ and }\big\lvert\E\big\langle\mathcal{H}_{k}^{\prime\prime}\big\rangle\big\rvert\leq Cs_{N}.

(4.31)

To obtain the concentration of the multi-overlaps (
2.11
) we will show the concentration (
C.8
) of the quantities
\mathcal{L}_{k}
for the Gibbs measure with parameters given by the contact point
(t_{N},x_{N},\lambda_{N})
. This concentration will be deduced from the fact that the averaged free energy is being “touched from above” by a smooth function at the contact point, thereby constraining its Hessian at this point, together with the concentration of the free energy
\smash{F_{N}^{(K)}}
about its average
\smash{\overline{F}_{N}^{(K)}}
. Due to the constraint on the Hessian at the contact point, we will be able to extend the concentration result on the free energy into an estimate on the concentration of its gradient. We decompose this argument into a series of four lemmas: the first two essentially bound the Hessian of the perturbed free energy (
4.11
) from above and from below; the third leverages the free energy concentration result in
Appendix

B
to estimate the uniform
L^{p}
-distance between the quenched and averaged free energies (
4.11
); while the fourth extends this to a control on the gradient of the free energy.

Lemma 4.3
.

For any perturbation parameter
\lambda
with
\lVert\lambda\rVert_{2}\leq C^{-1}
,

\overline{F}_{N}^{(K)}(t_{N},x_{N},\lambda_{N}+\lambda)-\overline{F}_{N}^{(K)}(t_{N},x_{N},\lambda_{N})-\lambda\cdot\nabla_{\lambda}\overline{F}_{N}^{(K)}(t_{N},x_{N},\lambda_{N})\leq C\lVert\lambda\rVert_{2}^{2}.

(4.32)

Proof.

Fix a perturbation parameter
\lambda
with
\lVert\lambda\rVert_{2}\leq C^{-1}
, and notice that (
4
) gives

\overline{F}_{N}^{\prime(K)}(t_{N},x_{N},\lambda_{N}+\lambda)-\overline{F}_{N}^{\prime(K)}(t_{N},x_{N},\lambda_{N})\leq\widetilde{\phi}(t_{N},x_{N},\lambda_{N}+\lambda)-\widetilde{\phi}(t_{N},x_{N},\lambda_{N}).

On the other hand, Taylor’s formula with integral remainder implies that

\displaystyle\overline{F}_{N}^{\prime(K)}(t_{N},

\displaystyle x_{N},\lambda_{N}+\lambda)-\overline{F}_{N}^{\prime(K)}(t_{N},x_{N},\lambda_{N})

\displaystyle=\lambda\cdot\nabla_{\lambda}\overline{F}_{N}^{\prime(K)}(t_{N},x_{N},\lambda_{N})+\int_{0}^{1}(1-s)\lambda\cdot\nabla_{\lambda}^{2}\overline{F}_{N}^{\prime(K)}(t_{N},x_{N},\lambda_{N}+s\lambda)\lambda\,\mathrm{d}s,

(4.33)

and similarly,

\displaystyle\widetilde{\phi}(t_{N},x_{N},\lambda_{N}

\displaystyle+\lambda)-\widetilde{\phi}(t_{N},x_{N},\lambda_{N})

\displaystyle=\lambda\cdot\nabla_{\lambda}\widetilde{\phi}(t_{N},x_{N},\lambda_{N})+\int_{0}^{1}(1-s)\lambda\cdot\nabla_{\lambda}^{2}\widetilde{\phi}(t_{N},x_{N},\lambda_{N}+s\lambda)\lambda\,\mathrm{d}s.

Combining (
4.19
) with the chain rule shows that
\lambda\cdot\nabla_{\lambda}\widetilde{\phi}(t_{N},x_{N},\lambda_{N})=\lambda\cdot\nabla_{\lambda}\overline{F}_{N}^{\prime(K)}(t_{N},x_{N},\lambda_{N})
, and therefore

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{1}(1-s)\lambda\cdot\nabla_{\lambda}^{2}\overline{F}_{N}^{\prime(K)}(t_{N},x_{N},\lambda_{N}+s\lambda)\lambda\,\mathrm{d}s

\displaystyle\leq\int_{0}^{1}(1-s)\lambda\cdot\nabla_{\lambda}^{2}\widetilde{\phi}(t_{N},x_{N},\lambda_{N}+s\lambda)\lambda\,\mathrm{d}s

\displaystyle\leq C\lVert\lambda\rVert_{2}^{2}.

Substituting this into (
4
) gives

\displaystyle\overline{F}_{N}^{(K)}(t_{N},x_{N},\lambda_{N}+\lambda)-\overline{F}_{N}^{(K)}(t_{N},x_{N},\lambda_{N})

\displaystyle=\overline{F}_{N}^{\prime(K)}(t_{N},x_{N},\lambda_{N}+\lambda)-\overline{F}_{N}^{\prime(K)}(t_{N},x_{N},\lambda_{N})

\displaystyle\leq\lambda\cdot\nabla_{\lambda}\overline{F}_{N}^{\prime(K)}(t_{N},x_{N},\lambda_{N})+C\lVert\lambda\rVert_{2}^{2}

\displaystyle=\lambda\cdot\nabla_{\lambda}\overline{F}_{N}^{(K)}(t_{N},x_{N},\lambda_{N})+C\lVert\lambda\rVert_{2}^{2}.

This completes the proof.
∎

Lemma 4.4
.

There exists a random variable
X
with
\E X^{2}\leq C
such that, for all perturbation parameters
\lambda
with
\lVert\lambda\rVert_{2}\leq C^{-1}
,

F_{N}^{(K)}(t_{N},x_{N},\lambda_{N}+\lambda)-F_{N}^{(K)}(t_{N},x_{N},\lambda_{N})-\lambda\cdot\nabla_{\lambda}F_{N}^{(K)}(t_{N},x_{N},\lambda_{N})\geq-X\lVert\lambda\rVert_{2}^{2}.

(4.34)

Proof.

Since
t_{N}
and
x_{N}
remain fixed throughout, write
\smash{F_{N}^{(K)}(\lambda)}
for
\smash{F_{N}^{(K)}(t_{N},x_{N},\lambda)}
. Introduce the function

h(\lambda)=F_{N}^{(K)}(\lambda)-\frac{\sqrt{\lambda_{0,N}}}{N}\sum_{i\leq N}\lvert Z_{0,i}\rvert+\frac{1}{N}\sum_{1\leq k\leq K_{+}}\sum_{j\leq\pi_{k}}\big(8\lambda_{k}^{2}e_{jk}-\log(1-\lambda_{k})\big).

(4.35)

Leveraging (
4.28
) and Hölder’s inequality, one can see that

\partial_{\lambda_{0}}^{2}h(\lambda)=\frac{1}{N}\big\langle(\mathcal{H}_{0}^{\prime}-\langle\mathcal{H}_{0}^{\prime}\rangle)^{2}\big\rangle-\frac{\epsilon_{N}^{2}}{4N\lambda_{0,N}^{3/2}}\langle\sigma\rangle\cdot Z_{0}+\frac{\epsilon_{N}^{2}}{4N\lambda_{0,N}^{3/2}}\sum_{i\leq N}\lvert Z_{0,i}\rvert\geq\frac{1}{N}\big\langle(\mathcal{H}_{0}^{\prime}-\langle\mathcal{H}_{0}^{\prime}\rangle)^{2}\big\rangle.

Using (
4.29
) and (
4.31
) reveals that for
1\leq k\leq K_{+}
,

\displaystyle\partial_{\lambda_{k}}^{2}h(\lambda)=\frac{1}{N}\big\langle

\displaystyle(\mathcal{H}_{k}^{\prime}-\langle\mathcal{H}_{k}^{\prime}\rangle)^{2}\big\rangle

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{N}\bigg\langle\sum_{j\leq\pi_{k}}\Big(-\frac{1}{(1+\lambda_{k}\sigma_{i_{jk}})^{2}}+2\frac{\sigma_{i_{jk}}\sigma^{*}_{i_{jk}}e_{jk}}{(1+\lambda_{k}\sigma^{*}_{i_{jk}})^{3}}+16e_{jk}+\frac{1}{(1-\lambda_{k})^{2}}\Big)\bigg\rangle.

Since
\lambda_{k}\leq 1/2
and all spin configuration coordinates are bounded by one, it is actually the case that

\partial_{\lambda_{k}}^{2}h(\lambda)\geq\frac{1}{N}\big\langle(\mathcal{H}_{k}^{\prime}-\langle\mathcal{H}_{k}^{\prime}\rangle)^{2}\big\rangle.

Together with (
4.30
), this shows that
\nabla_{\lambda}^{2}h
is positive definite and therefore
h
is convex. It follows that for any perturbation parameter
\lambda
with
\lVert\lambda\rVert_{2}\leq C^{-1}
,

h(\lambda_{N}+\lambda)\geq h(\lambda_{N})+\lambda\cdot\nabla_{\lambda}h(\lambda_{N}).

Remembering the definition of
h
in (
4.35
), this shows that the left-hand side of (
4.34
) is bounded from below by

\displaystyle\frac{1}{N}

\displaystyle\bigg(\sqrt{(\lambda_{N})_{0,N}+\lambda_{0,N}}-\sqrt{(\lambda_{N})_{0,N}}-\frac{\lambda_{0,N}}{2\sqrt{(\lambda_{N})_{0,N}}}\bigg)\sum_{i\leq N}\lvert Z_{0,i}\rvert

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad+\frac{1}{N}\sum_{1\leq k\leq K_{+}}\sum_{j\leq\pi_{k}}8e_{jk}\Big((\lambda_{N})_{k}^{2}+2\lambda_{k}(\lambda_{N})_{k}-\big((\lambda_{N})_{k}+\lambda_{k}\big)^{2}\Big)

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad+\frac{1}{N}\sum_{1\leq k\leq K_{+}}\sum_{j\leq\pi_{k}}\bigg(\log\bigg(\frac{1-(\lambda_{N})_{k}-\lambda_{k}}{1-(\lambda_{N})_{k}}\bigg)+\frac{\lambda_{k}}{1-(\lambda_{N})_{k}}\bigg).

Increasing
C
if necessary, Taylor’s theorem with differential remainder gives a perturbation parameter
\tilde{\lambda}
with
\tilde{\lambda}_{k}\in[2^{-k-1},2^{-k}]
for
0\leq k\leq K_{+}
whose value might not be the same at each occurrence such that

\displaystyle\sqrt{(\lambda_{N})_{0,N}+\lambda_{0,N}}-\sqrt{(\lambda_{N})_{0,N}}-\frac{\lambda_{0,N}}{2\sqrt{(\lambda_{N})_{0,N}}}

\displaystyle=-\frac{(\lambda_{0,N})^{2}}{8\tilde{\lambda}_{0,N}^{3/2}}\geq-\sqrt{\epsilon_{N}}\lambda_{0}^{2}\geq-\lambda_{0}^{2}

\displaystyle(\lambda_{N})_{k}^{2}+2\lambda_{k}(\lambda_{N})_{k}-\big((\lambda_{N})_{k}+\lambda_{k}\big)^{2}

\displaystyle=-\lambda_{k}^{2}

\displaystyle\log\bigg(\frac{1-(\lambda_{N})_{k}-\lambda_{k}}{1-(\lambda_{N})_{k}}\bigg)+\frac{\lambda_{k}}{1-(\lambda_{N})_{k}}

\displaystyle=-\frac{\lambda_{k}^{2}}{2(1-\tilde{\lambda}_{k})^{2}}\geq-2\lambda_{k}^{2}.

It follows that the left-hand side of (
4.34
) is bounded from below by

-\frac{\lambda_{0}^{2}}{N}\sum_{i\leq N}\lvert Z_{0,i}\rvert-\frac{1}{N}\sum_{1\leq k\leq K_{+}}\lambda_{k}^{2}\sum_{j\leq\pi_{k}}(8e_{jk}+2)\geq-X\lVert\lambda\rVert_{2}^{2}

for the random variable

X=\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i\leq N}\lvert Z_{0,i}\rvert+\frac{1}{N}\sum_{1\leq k\leq K_{+}}\sum_{j\leq\pi_{k}}(8e_{jk}+2).

Using the Cauchy-Schwarz inequality, taking the average with respect to the randomness of
(e_{jk})
before the average with respect to the randomness of
(\pi_{k})
and remembering (
4.4
) shows that

\displaystyle\E X^{2}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{C}{N^{2}}\bigg(\E\Big(\sum_{i\leq N}\lvert Z_{0,i}\rvert\Big)^{2}+\sum_{1\leq k\leq K_{+}}\E\Big(\sum_{j\leq\pi_{k}}(8e_{jk}+2)\Big)^{2}\bigg)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{C}{N^{2}}\Big(N\E\lvert Z_{0,1}\rvert+(N^{2}-N)\E\lvert Z_{0,1}Z_{0,2}\rvert+\sum_{1\leq k\leq K_{+}}\E\pi_{k}\sum_{j\leq\pi_{k}}(8e_{jk}+2)^{2}\Big)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{C}{N^{2}}\big(N^{2}+s_{N}^{2}+s_{N}\big)\leq C.

This completes the proof.
∎

Lemma 4.5
.

For every
M>0
small enough,
p\in[1,\infty)
and
\epsilon>0
, there exists
C<\infty
not depending on
N
such that

\bigg(\E\sup_{\lVert\lambda\rVert_{\infty}\leq M}\Big\lvert\Big(F_{N}^{(K)}-\overline{F}_{N}^{(K)}\Big)(t_{N},x_{N},\lambda_{N}+\lambda)\Big\rvert^{p}\bigg)^{\frac{1}{p}}\leq CN^{-\frac{1}{2}+\epsilon}.

(4.36)

Proof.

Let
0<M<1/2
be small enough so that
(\lambda_{N}+\lambda)_{k}\in[2^{-k-1},2^{-k}]
for
0\leq k\leq K_{+}
whenever
\lVert\lambda\rVert_{\infty}\leq M
, and for each perturbation parameter
\lambda
introduce the random variable

Y(\lambda)=\frac{1}{N}\sum_{0\leq k\leq K_{+}}\lvert\langle\mathcal{H}_{k}^{\prime}\rangle\rvert,

where the Gibbs average is associated with the perturbed Hamiltonian (
4.7
) evaluated at the triple
(t_{N},x_{N},\lambda_{N}+\lambda)
. The relevance of these random variables stems from the fact that by the mean value theorem, (
4.28
) and (
4.29
), for every
\lambda,\lambda^{\prime}
in the
\ell^{\infty}
-ball of radius
M
,

\big\lvert F_{N}^{(K)}(t_{N},x_{N},\lambda_{N}+\lambda)-F_{N}^{(K)}(t_{N},x_{N},\lambda_{N}+\lambda^{\prime})\big\rvert\leq C\sup_{\lVert\eta\rVert_{\infty}\leq M}Y(\eta)\lVert\lambda-\lambda^{\prime}\rVert_{1}.

Averaging this inequality also shows that for every
\lambda,\lambda^{\prime}
in the
\ell^{\infty}
-ball of radius
M
,

\big\lvert\overline{F}_{N}^{(K)}(t_{N},x_{N},\lambda_{N}+\lambda)-\overline{F}_{N}^{(K)}(t_{N},x_{N},\lambda_{N}+\lambda^{\prime})\big\rvert\leq\E\sup_{\lVert\eta\rVert_{\infty}\leq M}Y(\eta)\lVert\lambda-\lambda^{\prime}\rVert_{1}

These two bounds imply that for any even integer
q\geq 2
,

\displaystyle\E\sup_{\lVert\lambda\rVert_{\infty}\leq M}\Big\lvert\Big(F_{N}^{(K)}-\overline{F}_{N}^{(K)}\Big)(t_{N},x_{N},\lambda_{N}+\lambda)\Big\rvert^{q}\leq\E\sup_{\lambda\in A_{\epsilon}}\Big\lvert\Big(F_{N}^{(K)}-\overline{F}_{N}^{(K)}

\displaystyle\Big)(t_{N},x_{N},\lambda_{N}+\lambda)\Big\rvert^{q}

\displaystyle\quad+C\epsilon^{q}\E\sup_{\lVert\lambda\rVert_{\infty}\leq M}Y(\lambda)^{q}

for
A_{\epsilon}=\epsilon\mathbb{Z}^{1+K_{+}}\cap\{\lVert\lambda\rVert_{\infty}\leq M\}
. Indeed, every
\lambda
is at most at distance
\epsilon(K_{+}+1)
from an element in
A_{\epsilon}
with respect to the
\ell^{1}
-norm. Bounding the supremum over
A_{\epsilon}
by the sum over
A_{\epsilon}
and invoking the free energy concentration result in
Proposition

B.4
shows that

\E\sup_{\lVert\lambda\rVert_{\infty}\leq M}\Big\lvert\Big(F_{N}^{(K)}-\overline{F}_{N}^{(K)}\Big)(t_{N},x_{N},\lambda_{N}+\lambda)\Big\rvert^{q}\leq C\lvert A_{\epsilon}\rvert N^{-\frac{q}{2}}+C\epsilon^{q}\E\sup_{\lVert\lambda\rVert_{\infty}\leq M}\lvert Y(\lambda)\rvert^{q}.

(4.37)

To bound the moments of
\smash{\sup_{\lVert\lambda\rVert_{\infty}\leq M}\lvert Y(\lambda)\rvert}
fix
1\leq k\leq K_{+}
. Hölder’s inequality and (
4.27
) reveal that

\displaystyle\E\sup_{\lVert\lambda\rVert_{\infty}\leq M}\lvert\langle\mathcal{H}_{k}^{\prime}\rangle\rvert^{q}

\displaystyle\leq\E\sup_{\lVert\lambda\rVert_{\infty}\leq M}\bigg\lvert\sum_{j\leq\pi_{k}}\frac{1}{1-(\lambda_{N}+\lambda)_{k}}+\frac{e_{jk}}{(1-(\lambda_{N}+\lambda)_{k})^{2}}\bigg\rvert^{q}

\displaystyle\leq\E\bigg\lvert\sum_{j\leq\pi_{k}}\frac{4(1+e_{jk})}{(1-2M)^{2}}\bigg\rvert^{q}\leq\E\pi_{k}^{q-1}\sum_{j\leq\pi_{k}}\sum_{j\leq\pi_{k}}\bigg\lvert\frac{4(1+e_{jk})}{(1-2M)^{2}}\bigg\rvert^{q}

\displaystyle\leq C\E\pi_{k}^{q},

where the last inequality is found by averaging over the randomness of
(e_{jk})
. Similarly (
4.26
) and Hölder’s inequality give

\displaystyle\E\sup_{\lVert\lambda\rVert_{\infty}\leq M}\lvert\langle\mathcal{H}_{0}^{\prime}\rangle\rvert^{q}

\displaystyle\leq\epsilon_{N}^{q}\E\sup_{\lVert\lambda\rVert_{\infty}\leq M}\bigg\lvert\lvert\sigma\cdot\sigma^{*}\rvert+\frac{\lvert\sigma\cdot Z_{0}\rvert}{2\epsilon_{N}^{q/2}((\lambda_{N})_{0}+\lambda_{0})^{q/2}}\bigg\rvert^{q}

\displaystyle\leq C\epsilon_{N}^{q}\bigg(N^{q}+\frac{2^{q}\E\lvert\sigma\cdot Z_{0}\rvert^{q}}{2\epsilon_{N}^{q/2}(1-2M)^{q}}\bigg)\leq C\epsilon_{N}^{\frac{q}{2}}N^{q}.

Combining these two inequalities with
Lemma

B.2
and recalling the choices (
4.3
) and (
4.4
) of
\epsilon_{N}
and
s_{N}
shows that

\E\sup_{\lVert\lambda\rVert_{\infty}\leq M}\lvert Y(\lambda)\rvert^{q}\leq C.

(4.38)

Substituting this into (
4.37
) and noticing that
\lvert A_{\epsilon}\rvert
is of order
\epsilon^{-(K_{+}+1)}
yields

\E\sup_{\lVert\lambda\rVert_{\infty}\leq M}\Big\lvert\Big(F_{N}^{(K)}-\overline{F}_{N}^{(K)}\Big)(t_{N},x_{N},\lambda_{N}+\lambda)\Big\rvert^{q}\leq C\big(\epsilon^{-(K_{+}+1)}N^{-\frac{q}{2}}+\epsilon^{q}\big).

Taking
1/q
’th powers and choosing
\epsilon=N^{-\frac{q}{2(q+K_{+}+1)}}
gives

\bigg(\E\sup_{\lVert\lambda\rVert_{\infty}\leq M}\Big\lvert\Big(F_{N}^{(K)}-\overline{F}_{N}^{(K)}\Big)(t_{N},x_{N},\lambda_{N}+\lambda)\Big\rvert^{q}\bigg)^{\frac{1}{q}}\leq CN^{-\frac{q}{2(q+K_{+}+1)}}.

Notice that the power on the right-hand side can be made arbitrarily close to
-\frac{1}{2}
by taking
q
large enough. Invoking Jensen’s inequality completes the proof.
∎

Lemma 4.6
.

For every
\epsilon>0
, there exists a constant
C<\infty
not depending on
N
such that

\E\Big\lVert\nabla_{\lambda}\Big(F_{N}^{(K)}-\overline{F}_{N}^{(K)}\Big)(t_{N},x_{N},\lambda_{N})\Big\rVert_{2}^{2}\leq CN^{-\frac{1}{2}+\epsilon}.

(4.39)

Proof.

Given
\mu\in[0,C^{-1}]
, consider the random perturbation parameter

\lambda=\mu\cdot\frac{\nabla_{\lambda}\Big(F_{N}^{(K)}-\overline{F}_{N}^{(K)}\Big)(t_{N},x_{N},\lambda_{N})}{\Big\lVert\nabla_{\lambda}\Big(F_{N}^{(K)}-\overline{F}_{N}^{(K)}\Big)(t_{N},x_{N},\lambda_{N})\Big\rVert_{2}}.

Combining
Lemma

4.3
with
Lemma

4.4
shows that

\displaystyle\Big(F_{N}^{(K)}-\overline{F}_{N}^{(K)}\Big)(t_{N},x_{N},\lambda_{N}+\lambda)-\Big(

\displaystyle F_{N}^{(K)}-\overline{F}_{N}^{(K)}\Big)(t_{N},x_{N},\lambda_{N})

\displaystyle\geq\mu\Big\lVert\nabla_{\lambda}\Big(F_{N}^{(K)}-\overline{F}_{N}^{(K)}\Big)(t_{N},x_{N},\lambda_{N})\Big\rVert_{2}-(C+X)\lVert\lambda\rVert_{2}^{2}.

Rearranging, squaring and taking expectations yields

\displaystyle\mu^{2}\E\Big\lVert\nabla_{\lambda}\Big(F_{N}^{(K)}-\overline{F}_{N}^{(K)}\Big)(t_{N},x_{N},\lambda_{N})\Big\rVert_{2}^{2}\leq C\bigg(\E\sup_{\lVert\lambda\rVert_{\infty}\leq C^{-1}}\Big\rvert\Big(F_{N}^{(K)}-\overline{F}_{N}^{(K)}\Big)(t_{N},x_{N},\lambda_{N}

\displaystyle+\lambda)\Big\rvert^{2}+\mu^{4}\bigg),

where we have used the fact that
\E X^{2}\leq C
and
\lVert\lambda\rVert_{2}=\mu
. Invoking
Lemma

4.5
gives

\E\Big\lVert\nabla_{\lambda}\Big(F_{N}^{(K)}-\overline{F}_{N}^{(K)}\Big)(t_{N},x_{N},\lambda_{N})\Big\rVert_{2}^{2}\leq C\bigg(\frac{1}{N^{1-2\epsilon}\mu^{2}}+\mu^{2}\bigg).

Optimizing over
\mu
leads to the choice
\mu=N^{-\frac{1}{4}+\frac{\epsilon}{2}}
and completes the proof.
∎

Lemma 4.7
.

For any
1\leq k\leq K_{+}
, there exists a constant
C<\infty
not depending on
N
such that

\E\big\langle(\mathcal{L}_{0}-\E\langle\mathcal{L}_{0}\rangle)^{2}\big\rangle\leq CN^{-\frac{1}{4}}\quad\text{and}\quad\E\big\langle(\mathcal{L}_{k}-\E\langle\mathcal{L}_{k}\rangle)^{2}\big\rangle\leq CN^{-\frac{1}{20}}.

(4.40)

Here, the Gibbs average
\langle\cdot\rangle
is associated with the perturbed Hamiltonian (
4.7
) evaluated at the contact point
(t_{N},x_{N},\lambda_{N})
.

Proof.

A direct computation using (
4.28
) shows that

\displaystyle N^{2}\epsilon_{N}^{2}\E\big\langle(\mathcal{L}_{0}-\E\langle\mathcal{L}_{0}\rangle)^{2}\big\rangle

\displaystyle=\E\big\langle(\mathcal{H}_{0}^{\prime}-\langle\mathcal{H}_{0}^{\prime}\rangle)^{2}\big\rangle+\E\big(\langle\mathcal{H}_{0}^{\prime}\rangle-\E\langle\mathcal{H}^{\prime}_{0}\rangle\big)^{2}

\displaystyle=N\partial_{\lambda_{0}}^{2}\overline{F}_{N}^{(K)}(t_{N},x_{N},\lambda_{N})+\frac{\epsilon_{N}^{2}}{4\lambda_{0,N}^{3/2}}\E\langle\sigma\rangle\cdot Z_{0}

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\quad+N^{2}\E\Big(\partial_{\lambda_{0}}\Big(F_{N}^{(K)}-\overline{F}_{N}^{(K)}\Big)(t_{N},x_{N},\lambda_{N})\Big)^{2}.

It follows by
Lemma

4.3
and
Lemma

4.6
that for any
\epsilon>0
,

\E\big\langle(\mathcal{L}_{0}-\E\langle\mathcal{L}_{0}\rangle)^{2}\big\rangle\leq\frac{C}{N^{2}\epsilon_{N}^{2}}\Big(N+N\epsilon_{N}^{2-\frac{3}{2}}+N^{2-\frac{1}{2}+\epsilon}\Big)=C\Big(N^{2\lvert\gamma\rvert-1}+N^{\frac{3}{2}\lvert\gamma\rvert-1}+N^{2\lvert\gamma\rvert+\epsilon-\frac{1}{2}}\Big).

Remembering that
-1/8<\gamma<0
gives the first bound in (
4.40
). To establish the second bound, fix
1\leq k\leq K_{+}
. A direct computation using (
4.29
) yields

\displaystyle s_{N}^{2}\E\big\langle(\mathcal{L}_{k}-\E\langle\mathcal{L}_{k}\rangle)^{2}\big\rangle

\displaystyle=\E\big\langle(\mathcal{H}_{k}^{\prime}-\langle\mathcal{H}_{k}^{\prime}\rangle)^{2}\big\rangle+\E\big(\langle\mathcal{H}_{k}^{\prime}\rangle-\E\langle\mathcal{H}^{\prime}_{k}\rangle\big)^{2}

\displaystyle=N\partial_{\lambda_{k}}^{2}\overline{F}_{N}^{(K)}(t_{N},x_{N},\lambda_{N})-\E\langle\mathcal{H}_{k}^{\prime\prime}\rangle

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\quad+N^{2}\E\Big(\partial_{\lambda_{k}}\Big(F_{N}^{(K)}-\overline{F}_{N}^{(K)}\Big)(t_{N},x_{N},\lambda_{N})\Big)^{2}.

Invoking (
4.31
),
Lemma

4.3
and
Lemma

4.6
reveals that for any
\epsilon>0
,

\E\big\langle(\mathcal{L}_{k}-\E\langle\mathcal{L}_{k}\rangle)^{2}\big\rangle\leq\frac{C}{s_{N}^{2}}\Big(N+s_{N}+N^{\frac{3}{2}+\epsilon}\Big)=C\Big(N^{1-2\eta}+N^{-\eta}+N^{\frac{3}{2}+\epsilon-2\eta}\Big).

Choosing
\epsilon=1/20
, and recalling that
-1/8<\gamma<0
and
4/5<\eta<1
completes the proof.
∎

This result implies the fundamental assumption (
C.8
) in
Appendix

C
. Combining this with
Lemma

C.1
,
Lemma

C.2
and
Proposition

C.3
and fixing
\epsilon>0
, it is possible to find
\delta>0
so the statement of
Proposition

C.6
holds. In particular, setting
K_{+}=\lfloor\delta^{-1}\rfloor
in the perturbed Hamiltonian (
4.7
) ensures that

\E\big\langle(R_{[m]}-\E\langle R_{[m]}\rangle)^{2}\big\rangle\leq\epsilon

(4.41)

for
1\leq m\leq\lfloor\epsilon^{-1}\rfloor
. Together with the computations in
Section

2
, this multi-overlap concentration allows us to finally give a proof of
Lemma

4.2
.

Proof of
Lemma

4.2
.

To alleviate notation, we always implicitly assume that
\overline{F}_{N}^{(K)}
and its derivatives are evaluated at the contact point
(t_{N},x_{N},\lambda_{N})
. The definition of the modified free energy in (
4.12
) and
Corollary

2.2
reveal that

\partial_{t}\overline{F}_{N}^{\prime(K)}=\frac{1}{2}\big(c+\Delta\overline{m}^{2}\big)\log(c)+\frac{c}{2}\sum_{n\geq 2}\frac{(-\Delta/c)^{n}}{n(n-1)}\E\big\langle R_{[n]}^{2}\big\rangle-\frac{c}{2}+\frac{b}{2}+\BigO(N^{-1}).

(4.42)

On the other hand, the duality relation (
3.22
), the definition of the modified free energy in (
4.12
) and
Corollary

2.5
imply that for any
k\in\mathcal{D}_{K}
,

\displaystyle\partial_{x_{k}}\overline{F}_{N}^{\prime(K)}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\lvert\mathcal{D}_{K}\rvert}\bigg(\big(c+\Delta\overline{m}k\big)\log(c)+c\sum_{n\geq 2}\frac{(-\Delta/c)^{n}}{n(n-1)}\E\langle R_{[n]}\rangle k^{n}-c+b\bigg)+\BigO(N^{-1}).

If we denote by
\mu^{*}=\mathcal{L}(\langle\sigma_{i}\rangle)
the law of the Gibbs average of a uniformly sampled spin coordinate, then the Nishimori identity (
2.3
) and the definition of
\widetilde{g}_{b}
in (
3.23
) allow us to rewrite this as

\partial_{x_{k}}\overline{F}_{N}^{\prime(K)}=\frac{1}{\lvert\mathcal{D}_{K}\rvert}\int_{-1}^{1}\widetilde{g}_{b}(ky)\,\mathrm{d}\mu^{*}(y)+\BigO\big(N^{-1}\big).

The mean value theorem shows that

\displaystyle\lvert\mathcal{D}_{K}\rvert\Big\lvert\frac{1}{\lvert\mathcal{D}_{K}\rvert}\int_{-1}^{1}\widetilde{g}_{b}(ky)\,\mathrm{d}\mu^{*}(y)-\widetilde{G}_{b}^{(K)}x^{(K)}(\mu^{*})_{k}\Big\rvert

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{k^{\prime}\in\mathcal{D}_{K}}\int_{k^{\prime}}^{k^{\prime}+2^{-K}}\lvert\widetilde{g}_{b}(ky)-\widetilde{g}_{b}(kk^{\prime})\rvert\,\mathrm{d}\mu^{*}(y)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{\lVert\widetilde{g}_{b}^{\prime}\rVert_{\infty}}{2^{K}},

which means that

{|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|\nabla_{x}\overline{F}_{N}^{\prime(K)}-\widetilde{G}_{b}^{(K)}x^{(K)}(\mu^{*})|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|}_{1,*}\leq\frac{\lVert\widetilde{g}^{\prime}_{b}\rVert_{\infty}}{2^{K}}+\BigO\big(N^{-1}\big).

In particular,

{|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|\widetilde{G}_{b}^{(K)}x^{(K)}(\mu^{*})|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|}_{1,*}\leq{|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|\nabla_{x}\overline{F}_{N}^{\prime(K)}|\kern-0.43057pt|\kern-0.43057pt|}_{1,*}+\frac{\lVert\widetilde{g}_{b}^{\prime}\rVert_{\infty}}{2^{K}}+\BigO\big(N^{-1}\big)\leq\lVert\widetilde{g}_{b}\rVert_{\infty}+\lVert\widetilde{g}_{b}^{\prime}\rVert_{\infty}+\BigO\big(N^{-1}\big)\leq R

for
N
large enough.
Remembering that
\smash{\widetilde{\mathsf{H}}_{b,K,R}}
coincides with
\smash{\widetilde{\mathsf{C}}_{b,K}}
on
\smash{\widetilde{\mathcal{C}}_{b,K}\cap B_{K,R}}
and leveraging the Lipschitz continuity of
\smash{\widetilde{\mathsf{H}}_{b,K,R}}
in
Proposition

3.8
gives

\Big\lvert\widetilde{\mathsf{H}}_{b,K,R}\Big(\nabla_{x}\overline{F}_{N}^{\prime(K)}\Big)-\widetilde{\mathsf{C}}_{b,K}\big(\widetilde{G}_{b}^{(K)}x^{(K)}(\mu^{*})\big)\Big\rvert\leq\frac{8RM_{b}\lVert\widetilde{g}_{b}^{\prime}\rVert_{\infty}}{2^{K}m_{b}^{2}}+\BigO\big(N^{-1}\big).

Another application of the mean value theorem shows that

\Big\lvert\widetilde{\mathsf{C}}_{b,K}\big(\widetilde{G}_{b}^{(K)}x^{(K)}(\mu^{*})\big)-\frac{1}{2}\int_{-1}^{1}\int_{-1}^{1}\widetilde{g}_{b}(xy)\,\mathrm{d}\mu^{*}(y)\,\mathrm{d}\mu^{*}(x)\Big\rvert\leq\frac{\lVert\widetilde{g}_{b}^{\prime}\rVert_{\infty}}{2^{K}},

while a direct computation using the Nishimori identity (
2.3
) reveals that

\frac{1}{2}\int_{-1}^{1}\widetilde{g}_{b}(xy)\,\mathrm{d}\mu^{*}(y)\,\mathrm{d}\mu^{*}(x)=\frac{1}{2}\big(c+\Delta\overline{m}^{2}\big)\log(c)+\frac{c}{2}\sum_{n\geq 2}\frac{(-\Delta/c)^{n}}{n(n-1)}\big(\E\langle R_{[n]}\rangle\big)^{2}-\frac{c}{2}+\frac{b}{2}.

It follows by (
4.42
) that up to an error vanishing with
N
,

\Big\lvert\partial_{t}\overline{F}_{N}^{\prime(K)}-\widetilde{\mathsf{H}}_{K,R}\Big(\nabla_{x}\overline{F}_{N}^{\prime(K)}\Big)\Big\rvert\leq\Big\lvert\frac{c}{2}\sum_{n\geq 2}\frac{(-\Delta/c)^{n}}{n(n-1)}\E\big\langle(R_{[n]}-\E\langle R_{[n]}\rangle)^{2}\big\rangle\Big\rvert+\frac{8RM_{b}\lVert\widetilde{g}_{b}^{\prime}\rVert_{\infty}}{2^{K}m_{b}^{2}}+\frac{\lVert\widetilde{g}^{\prime}\rVert_{\infty}}{2^{K}}.

Invoking the multi-overlap concentration (
4.41
), noticing that the multi-overlaps are bounded by one and using the formula for the sum of a geometric series implies that, up to an error vanishing with
N
,

\Big\lvert\partial_{t}\overline{F}_{N}^{\prime(K)}-\widetilde{\mathsf{H}}_{K,R}\Big(\nabla_{x}\overline{F}_{N}^{\prime(K)}\Big)\Big\rvert\leq\frac{\epsilon c^{2}}{2(c-\lvert\Delta\rvert)}+\frac{c}{2}\sum_{n\geq\lfloor\epsilon^{-1}\rfloor}\big(\lvert\Delta\rvert/c\big)^{n}+\frac{8RM_{b}\lVert\widetilde{g}_{b}^{\prime}\rVert_{\infty}}{2^{K}m_{b}^{2}}+\frac{\lVert\widetilde{g}^{\prime}\rVert_{\infty}}{2^{K}}.

Defining
\mathcal{E}_{\epsilon,K}
to be the right-hand side of this expression completes the proof.
∎

5
The disassortative sparse stochastic block model

In this section, we leverage
Theorem

1.1
to recover the known variational formula for the sparse stochastic block model in the disassortative regime,
\Delta\leq 0
. The idea will be to apply Theorem 1.5 in
[
31
]
to the infinite-dimensional Hamilton-Jacobi equation (
1.41
) and obtain an infinite-dimensional Hopf-Lax formula. This Hopf-Lax formula will coincide with the standard variational formula obtained in
[
13
,
28
]
and stated in
Theorem

1.1
. The key insight will be that in the disassortative setting, for
b
large enough, the function
\widetilde{g}_{b}
in (
3.23
) is such that

\int_{-1}^{1}\int_{-1}^{1}\widetilde{g}_{b}(xy)\,\mathrm{d}\mu(x)\,\mathrm{d}\mu(y)\geq 0

(5.1)

for every signed measures
\mu\in\mathcal{M}_{s}
. This assumption is equivalent to the non-negative definiteness of each of the matrices
\smash{\widetilde{G}_{b}^{(K)}}
and to the convexity of each of the projected non-linearities (
3.26
).

Lemma 5.1
.

If
\Delta\leq 0
and
b
is large enough, then the function
\widetilde{g}_{b}:[-1,1]\to\mathbb{R}
defined in (
3.23
) satisfies (
5.1
).

Proof.

By a simple approximation argument, it suffices to establish (
5.1
) for a discrete signed measure of the form

\mu=\frac{1}{\lvert\mathcal{D}_{K}\rvert}\sum_{k\in\mathcal{D}_{K}}x_{k}\delta_{k}

for some
\smash{x\in\mathbb{R}^{\mathcal{D}_{K}}}
. For such a measure,

\int_{-1}^{1}\int_{-1}^{1}\widetilde{g}_{b}(xy)\,\mathrm{d}\mu(x)\,\mathrm{d}\mu(y)=\frac{1}{\lvert\mathcal{D}_{K}\rvert^{2}}\sum_{k,k^{\prime}\in\mathcal{D}_{K}}\widetilde{g}_{b}(kk^{\prime})x_{k}x_{k^{\prime}}=x^{T}\widetilde{G}_{b}^{(K)}x,

so (
5.1
) is equivalent to the non-negative definiteness of each of the matrices
\smash{\widetilde{G}_{b}^{(K)}}
. Observe that for any
k,k^{\prime}\in\mathcal{D}_{K}
,

\big(\widetilde{G}_{b}^{(K)}\big)_{kk^{\prime}}=\frac{1}{\lvert\mathcal{D}_{K}\rvert^{2}}\widetilde{g}_{b}(kk^{\prime})=\frac{1}{\lvert\mathcal{D}_{K}\rvert^{2}}\bigg(b+c\log(c)-c+\Delta kk^{\prime}\log(c)+c\sum_{n\geq 2}\frac{(-\Delta/c)^{n}}{n(n-1)}(kk^{\prime})^{n}\bigg).

If we introduce the vectors
\mathbf{k}=(k)_{k\in\mathcal{D}_{K}}
and
\mathbf{\iota}=(1)_{k\in\mathcal{D}_{K}}
, and write
\odot n
for the
n
-fold Hadamard product on the space of
\mathcal{D}_{K}\times\mathcal{D}_{K}
matrices, this implies that

\widetilde{G}_{b}^{(K)}=\frac{1}{\lvert\mathcal{D}_{K}\rvert^{2}}\bigg((b+c\log(c)-c)\mathbf{\iota}\mathbf{\iota}^{T}+\Delta\log(c)\mathbf{k}\mathbf{k}^{T}+\sum_{n\geq 2}\frac{(-\Delta/c)^{n}}{n(n-1)}\big(\mathbf{k}\mathbf{k}^{T}\big)^{\odot n}\bigg)=\lim_{M\to\infty}\widetilde{G}^{(K)}_{b,M}

for the matrix

\widetilde{G}^{(K)}_{b,M}=\frac{1}{\lvert\mathcal{D}_{K}\rvert^{2}}\bigg((b+c\log(c)-c)\mathbf{\iota}\mathbf{\iota}^{T}+\Delta\log(c)\mathbf{k}\mathbf{k}^{T}+\sum_{2\leq n\leq M}\frac{(-\Delta/c)^{n}}{n(n-1)}\big(\mathbf{k}\mathbf{k}^{T}\big)^{\odot n}\bigg).

Choosing
b>2c\lvert\log(c)\rvert+c
ensures that the first two terms in this sum define a non-negative definite matrix. Using that
\Delta\leq 0
and the Schur product theorem, we see that the matrix
\smash{\widetilde{G}^{(K)}_{b,M}}
is a positive linear combination of non-negative definite matrices, and is therefore non-negative definite. Noticing that the limit of non-negative definite matrices is again non-negative definite completes the proof.
∎

This result allows us to apply Theorem 1.5 in
[
31
]
to the infinite-dimensional Hamilton-Jacobi equation (
1.41
) and obtain a variational formula for its solution. To state this formula concisely, through a slight abuse of notation,
introduce the functional
\smash{\Par:\mathbb{R}_{>0}\times\mathcal{M}_{+}\times\mathcal{M}_{+}\to\mathbb{R}}
defined by

\Par(t,\mu,\nu)=\psi(\mu+t\nu)-\frac{t}{2}\int_{-1}^{1}G_{\nu}(y)\,\mathrm{d}\nu(y).

(5.2)

Theorem 1.5 in
[
31
]
and (
H2
) imply that the unique solution to the infinite-dimensional Hamilton-Jacobi equation (
1.41
) is given by the Hopf-Lax formula

f(t,\mu)=\sup_{\nu\in\Pr[-1,1]}\Par(t,\mu,\nu)

(5.3)

for every
t>0
and
\mu\in\mathcal{M}_{+}
. Combining this representation formula with
Theorem

1.1
and a simple interpolation argument taken from
[
13
]
, we now prove
Theorem

1.2
.

Proof of
Theorem

1.2
.

By
Theorem

1.1
and the Hopf-Lax formula (
5.3
), the limit of the free energy (
1.20
) satisfies the upper bound

\limsup_{N\to\infty}\overline{F}_{N}\leq\sup_{\nu\in\Pr[-1,1]}\Par(1,0,\nu),

(5.4)

where
0
denotes the zero measure. To show that the right-hand side of this expression may be bounded by the supremum of the functional (
1.46
) over measures
\mu\in\mathcal{M}_{p}
, for
b>1
large enough so the function
\widetilde{g}_{b}
defined in (
3.23
) is strictly positive on
[-1,1]
and each integer
N\geq 1
, introduce the functional
\smash{\widetilde{\Par}_{b,N}:\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}\times\mathcal{M}_{+}\times\mathcal{M}_{+}\to\mathbb{R}}
defined by

\widetilde{\Par}_{b,N}(t,\mu,\nu)=\widetilde{\psi}_{b,N}(\mu+t\nu)-\frac{t}{2}\int_{-1}^{1}\widetilde{G}_{b,\nu}(y)\,\mathrm{d}\nu(y).

Here
\widetilde{\psi}_{b,N}:\mathcal{M}_{+}\to\mathbb{R}
denotes the initial condition

\widetilde{\psi}_{b,N}(\mu)=\psi_{N}(\mu)+b\int_{-1}^{1}\,\mathrm{d}\mu

and
\widetilde{G}_{b,\nu}:[-1,1]\to\mathbb{R}
denotes the function

\widetilde{G}_{b,\nu}(x)=\int_{-1}^{1}\widetilde{g}_{b}(xy)\,\mathrm{d}\nu(y).

By Theorem 1.5 in
[
31
]
, there exists a probability measure
\widetilde{\nu}\in\Pr[-1,1]
which maximizes the right-hand side of (
5.4
). It follows by
Proposition

3.6
that

\sup_{\nu\in\Pr[-1,1]}\Par(1,0,\nu)=\lim_{N\to\infty}\widetilde{\Par}_{b,N}(1,0,\widetilde{\nu})-\frac{b}{2}\leq\limsup_{N\to\infty}\sup_{\nu\in\mathcal{M}_{+}}\widetilde{\Par}_{b,N}(1,0,\nu)-\frac{b}{2}.

(5.5)

An identical argument to that in Lemma 5.1 of
[
31
]
gives a sequence of maximizing measures
(\nu_{N})\subset\mathcal{M}_{+}
with

\sup_{\nu\in\mathcal{M}_{+}}\widetilde{\Par}_{b,N}(1,0,\nu)=\widetilde{\Par}_{b,N}(1,0,\nu_{N}).

(5.6)

By
Corollary

2.5
and (
2.37
), the Gateaux derivative density of the initial condition
\smash{\widetilde{\psi}_{b,N}}
at the measure
\nu_{N}
is given by

\smash{D_{\mu}\widetilde{\psi}_{b,N}(\nu_{N},\cdot)=\widetilde{G}_{b,\nu_{N}^{*}}+\BigO\big(N^{-1}\big)}
for some measure
\nu_{N}^{*}\in\mathcal{M}_{p}
. Up to adding errors of
\BigO(N^{-1})
throughout, the proof of Theorem 1.3 in
[
31
]
applies and reveals that each maximizer
\nu_{N}\in\mathcal{M}_{+}
satisfies the approximate first order condition

D_{\mu}\widetilde{\psi}_{b,N}(\nu_{N},\cdot)=\widetilde{G}_{b,\nu_{N}}+\BigO\big(N^{-1}\big).

This means that

\widetilde{G}_{b,\nu_{N}^{*}}=D_{\mu}\widetilde{\psi}_{b,N}(\nu_{N},\cdot)+\BigO\big(N^{-1}\big)=\widetilde{G}_{b,\nu_{N}}+\BigO\big(N^{-1}\big).

Together with the definition of
\smash{\widetilde{g}_{b}}
in (
3.23
), this implies that

\displaystyle(b+c\log(c)-c)

\displaystyle\int_{-1}^{1}\,\mathrm{d}\nu_{N}(y)+\Delta\log(c)\int_{-1}^{1}y\,\mathrm{d}\nu_{N}(y)x+c\sum_{n\geq 2}\frac{(-\Delta/c)^{n}}{n(n-1)}\int_{-1}^{1}y^{n}\,\mathrm{d}\nu_{N}(y)x^{n}

\displaystyle=(b+c\log(c)-c)+\Delta\log(c)\overline{m}x+c\sum_{n\geq 2}\frac{(-\Delta/c)^{n}}{n(n-1)}\int_{-1}^{1}y^{n}\,\mathrm{d}\nu_{N}^{*}(y)x^{n}+\BigO\big(N^{-1}\big).

Since
b>1
and
c\log(c)-c\geq-1
, we must have

\int_{-1}^{1}\,\mathrm{d}\nu_{N}(y)=1+\BigO\big(N^{-1}\big)\quad\text{and}\quad\int_{-1}^{1}y^{n}\,\mathrm{d}\nu_{N}(y)=\int_{-1}^{1}y^{n}\,\mathrm{d}\nu_{N}^{*}(y)+\BigO\big(N^{-1}\big)

for all
n\geq 2
.
We have used the fact that
\Delta\neq 0
and accounted for the fact that
c
could be equal to one. Applying the Prokhorov theorem and passing to a subsequence if necessary, it is therefore possible to ensure that the sequences
\smash{(\nu_{N})}
and
\smash{(\nu_{N}^{*})}
converge weakly to probability measures
\smash{\nu\in\Pr[-1,1]}
and
\smash{\nu^{*}\in\mathcal{M}_{p}}
with

\int_{-1}^{1}y^{n}\,\mathrm{d}\nu(y)=\int_{-1}^{1}y^{n}\,\mathrm{d}\nu^{*}(y)

for all
n\neq 1
. Since the set of polynomials with degree one coefficient equal to zero form of a sub-algebra of the space of continuous functions on the compact set
[-1,1]
, the Stone-Weierstrass theorem implies that
\smash{\nu=\nu^{*}\in\mathcal{M}_{p}}
. Recalling that we denote
\overline{\nu}_{N}
for the probability measure induced by
\nu_{N}
, and arguing as in the proof of
Lemma

3.7
, we have

\displaystyle\big\lvert\widetilde{\psi}_{b,N}(\nu_{N})-\widetilde{\psi}_{b}(\nu^{*})\big\rvert

\displaystyle\leq\big\lvert\widetilde{\psi}_{b,N}(\nu_{N})-\widetilde{\psi}_{b,N}(\overline{\nu}_{N})\big\rvert+\big\lvert\widetilde{\psi}_{b,N}(\overline{\nu}_{N})-\widetilde{\psi}_{b,N}(\nu^{*})\big\rvert+\big\lvert\widetilde{\psi}_{b,N}(\nu^{*})-\widetilde{\psi}_{b}(\nu^{*})\big\rvert

\displaystyle\leq C\mathrm{TV}(\nu_{N},\overline{\nu}_{N})+CW(\overline{\nu}_{N},\nu^{*})+\big\lvert\widetilde{\psi}_{b,N}(\nu^{*})-\widetilde{\psi}_{b}(\nu^{*})\big\rvert,

for some constant
C>0
that depends only on
c
.
Recalling that the Wasserstein distance (
3.9
) metrizes the weak convergence of probability measures on
[-1,1]
, observing that

\mathrm{TV}(\nu_{N},\overline{\nu}_{N})=\big|1-{\nu_{N}[-1,1]}\big|,

and using
Proposition

3.6
and (
5.6
) to let
N
tend to infinity in (
5.5
) shows that

\displaystyle\sup_{\nu\in\Pr[-1,1]}\Par(1,0,\nu)

\displaystyle\leq\limsup_{N\to\infty}\widetilde{\Par}_{b,N}(1,0,\nu_{N})-\frac{b}{2}=\widetilde{\psi}_{b}(\nu^{*})-\frac{1}{2}\int_{-1}^{1}\widetilde{G}_{b,\nu^{*}}(y)\,\mathrm{d}\nu^{*}(y)-\frac{b}{2}

\displaystyle\leq\sup_{\nu\in\mathcal{M}_{p}}\Par(1,0,\nu).

Substituting this upper bound into (
5.4
) yields

\limsup_{N\to\infty}\overline{F}_{N}\leq\sup_{\nu\in\mathcal{M}_{p}}\Par(1,0,\nu).

To express this upper bound in terms of the functional (
1.46
), fix
\nu\in\mathcal{M}_{p}
and denote by
x_{1}
and
x_{2}
two independent samples from the probability measure
\nu
. The definition of
g
in (
1.36
) implies that

\Par(1,0,\nu)=\psi(\nu)+\frac{c}{2}-\frac{1}{2}\big(c+\Delta\overline{m}^{2}\big)\log(c)-\frac{c}{2}\sum_{n\geq 2}\frac{(-\Delta/c)^{n}}{n(n-1)}\big(\E x_{1}^{n}\big)^{2}.

A Taylor expansion of the logarithm shows that

c\sum_{n\geq 2}\frac{(-\Delta/c)^{n}}{n(n-1)}\big(\E x^{n}\big)^{2}=\E(c+\Delta x_{1}x_{2})\log(c+\Delta x_{1}x_{2})-\big(c+\Delta\overline{m}^{2}\big)\log(c)-\Delta\overline{m}^{2}

(5.7)

from which it follows that

\Par(1,0,\nu)=\psi(\nu)+\frac{c}{2}-\frac{1}{2}\E(c+\Delta x_{1}x_{2})\log(c+\Delta x_{1}x_{2})+\frac{\Delta\overline{m}^{2}}{2}=\Par(\nu),

and therefore

\limsup_{N\to\infty}\overline{F}_{N}\leq\sup_{\nu\in\mathcal{M}_{p}}\Par(\nu).

This establishes the upper bound in (
1.47
). To prove the corresponding lower bound, we follow
[
13
]
and proceed by interpolation. Given a measure
\nu\in\mathcal{M}_{p}
, introduce the interpolating free energy

\varphi(t)=\widetilde{F}_{N}(t,1-t,\nu)

(5.8)

for the free energy
\widetilde{F}_{N}
defined in (
1.29
). The derivative computations in
Corollary

2.2
and
Lemma

2.3
together with a computation identical to that in
Corollary

2.5
imply that

\displaystyle\varphi^{\prime}(t)

\displaystyle=\partial_{t}\widetilde{F}_{N}(t,1-t,\nu)-\partial_{s}\widetilde{F}_{N}(t,1-t,\nu)

\displaystyle=\frac{c}{2}-\frac{1}{2}\big(c+\Delta\overline{m}^{2}\big)\log(c)+\frac{c}{2}\sum_{n\geq 2}\frac{(-\Delta/c)^{n}}{n(n-1)}\E\big\langle R_{[n]}^{2}\big\rangle-c\sum_{n\geq 2}\frac{(-\Delta/c)^{n}}{n(n-1)}\E\langle R_{[n]}\rangle\E x_{1}^{n}.

\displaystyle=\frac{c}{2}-\frac{1}{2}\big(c+\Delta\overline{m}^{2}\big)\log(c)-\frac{c}{2}\sum_{n\geq 2}\frac{(-\Delta/c)^{n}}{n(n-1)}\big(\E x_{1}^{n}\big)^{2}+\frac{c}{2}\sum_{n\geq 2}\frac{(-\Delta/c)^{n}}{n(n-1)}\E\big\langle(R_{[n]}-\E x_{1}^{n})^{2}\big\rangle.

It follows by (
5.7
) that

\varphi^{\prime}(t)=\frac{c}{2}+\frac{\Delta\overline{m}^{2}}{2}-\frac{1}{2}\E(c+\Delta x_{1}x_{2})\log(c+\Delta x_{1}x_{2})+\frac{c}{2}\sum_{n\geq 2}\frac{(-\Delta/c)^{n}}{n(n-1)}\E\big\langle(R_{[n]}-\E x_{1}^{n})^{2}\big\rangle.

Since the final term in this equality is non-negative, the fundamental theorem of calculus reveals that

\overline{F}_{N}\geq\psi_{N}(\nu)+\frac{c}{2}+\frac{\Delta\overline{m}^{2}}{2}-\frac{1}{2}\E(c+\Delta x_{1}x_{2})\log(c+\Delta x_{1}x_{2}),

where we have used that
\varphi(1)=\overline{F}_{N}
and
\varphi(0)=\psi_{N}(\nu)
. Using
Proposition

3.6
to let
N
tend to infinity gives the lower bound

\liminf_{N\to\infty}\overline{F}_{N}\geq\psi(\nu)+\frac{c}{2}+\frac{\Delta\overline{m}^{2}}{2}-\frac{1}{2}\E(c+\Delta x_{1}x_{2})\log(c+\Delta x_{1}x_{2})=\Par(\nu).

Taking the supremum over all measures
\nu\in\mathcal{M}_{p}
completes the proof.
∎

Appendix A
Asymptotic equivalence of free energy functionals

In this appendix we show that the free energy functionals
\overline{F}_{N}^{\circ}
and
\overline{F}_{N}
defined in (
1.15
) and (
1.20
), respectively, are asymptotically equivalent. This will be a consequence of the binomial-Poisson approximation. To state this result concisely, given a separable metric space
S
, recall the definition of the total variation distance

\mathrm{TV}(\mathbb{P},\mathbb{Q})=\sup\big\{\lvert\mathbb{P}(A)-\mathbb{Q}(A)\rvert\mid A\text{ is a measurable subset of }S\big\}

(A.1)

between probability measures
\mathbb{P},\mathbb{Q}\in\Pr(S)
. Approximating any measurable function with values in
S
by a sequence of simple functions, one can check that the total variation distance admits the dual representation

\mathrm{TV}(\mathbb{P},\mathbb{Q})=\sup\bigg\{\Big\lvert\int_{-1}^{1}f(x)\,\mathrm{d}\mathbb{P}(x)-\int_{-1}^{1}f(x)\,\mathrm{d}\mathbb{Q}(x)\Big\rvert\mid f:S\to[0,1]\text{ measurable}\bigg\}.

(A.2)

Using the Hahn-Jordan decomposition, it is also possible to show that

\mathrm{TV}(\mathbb{P},\mathbb{Q})=\inf\big\{\mathbb{P}\{X\neq Y\}\mid X\sim\mathbb{P}\text{ and }Y\sim\mathbb{Q}\big\}.

(A.3)

We will use this result for discrete probability measures supported on the set of natural numbers in which case this representation follows from the Kantorovich-Rubinstein theorem (see Theorem 4.15 in
[
65
]
). The binomial-Poisson approximation is an upper bound on the total variation distance between the convolution of Bernoulli distributions and an appropriate Poisson distribution.

Lemma A.1
.

Consider independent Bernoulli random variables
X_{i}\sim\Ber(p_{i})
for
i\leq n
, and let
\smash{\lambda_{n}=\sum_{i\leq n}p_{i}}
. If
\smash{S_{n}=\sum_{i\leq n}X_{i}}
and
\smash{\Pi_{n}\sim\Poi(\lambda_{n})}
, then

\mathrm{TV}(S_{n},\Pi_{n})\leq\sum_{i\leq n}p_{i}^{2}.

(A.4)

Proof.

See Theorem 2.4 in
[
65
]
.
∎

Proposition A.2
.

The free energies (
1.15
) and (
1.20
) are asymptotically equivalent,

\lim_{N\to\infty}\big\lvert\overline{F}_{N}-\overline{F}_{N}^{\circ}\big\rvert=0.

(A.5)

Proof.

Introduce the Hamiltonians

\displaystyle\widetilde{H}_{N}^{\circ}(\sigma)

\displaystyle=\sum_{i<j}\bigg(G_{ij}\log(c+\Delta\sigma_{i}\sigma_{j})-\frac{c+\Delta\sigma_{i}\sigma_{j}}{N}\bigg)

\displaystyle\widetilde{H}_{N}(\sigma)

\displaystyle=\sum_{k\leq\Pi_{1}}\bigg(G_{i_{k},j_{k}}^{k}\log(c+\Delta\sigma_{i_{k}}\sigma_{j_{k}})-\frac{c+\Delta\sigma_{i_{k}}\sigma_{j_{k}}}{N}\bigg)

on
\Sigma_{N}
, and denote by

\widetilde{F}_{N}^{\circ}=\frac{1}{N}\E\log\int_{\Sigma_{N}}\exp\widetilde{H}_{N}^{\circ}(\sigma)\,\mathrm{d}P_{N}^{*}(\sigma)\quad\text{and}\quad\widetilde{F}_{N}=\frac{1}{N}\E\log\int_{\Sigma_{N}}\exp\widetilde{H}_{N}(\sigma)\,\mathrm{d}P_{N}^{*}(\sigma)

their associated free energy functionals. A Taylor expansion of the logarithm shows that for any
\sigma\in\Sigma_{N}
,

\displaystyle\big\lvert H_{N}^{\circ}(\sigma)-\widetilde{H}_{N}^{\circ}(\sigma)\big\rvert

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{i<j}\Big\lvert\frac{c+\Delta\sigma_{i}\sigma_{j}}{N}-(1-G_{ij})\Big(\frac{c+\Delta\sigma_{i}\sigma_{j}}{N}\Big)\Big\rvert+\BigO(1)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{c+\lvert\Delta\rvert}{N}\sum_{i<j}G_{ij}+\BigO(1)

and

\displaystyle\big\lvert H_{N}(\sigma)-\widetilde{H}_{N}(\sigma)\big\rvert

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{k\leq\Pi_{1}}\Big\lvert\frac{c+\Delta\sigma_{i_{k}}\sigma_{j_{k}}}{N}-(1-G_{i_{k},j_{k}}^{k})\Big(\frac{c+\Delta\sigma_{i_{k}}\sigma_{j_{k}}}{N}\Big)\Big\rvert+\BigO\big(\Pi_{1}/N^{2}\big)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{c+\lvert\Delta\rvert}{N}\sum_{k\leq\Pi_{1}}G_{i_{k},j_{k}}^{k}+\BigO\big(\Pi_{1}/N^{2}\big).

Since these bounds are uniform in
\sigma\in\Sigma_{N}
and
\E\Pi_{1}=\binom{N}{2}
,

\displaystyle\big\lvert\overline{F}_{N}^{\circ}-\widetilde{F}_{N}^{\circ}\big\rvert

\displaystyle\leq\frac{c+\lvert\Delta\rvert}{N^{2}}\sum_{i<j}\E G_{ij}+\BigO\big(N^{-1}\big)\leq\frac{(c+\lvert\Delta\rvert)^{2}}{N}+\BigO\big(N^{-1}\big)=\BigO\big(N^{-1}\big),

\displaystyle\big\lvert\overline{F}_{N}-\widetilde{F}_{N}\big\rvert

\displaystyle\leq\frac{c+\lvert\Delta\rvert}{N^{2}}\E\sum_{k\leq\Pi_{1}}G_{i_{k},j_{k}}^{k}+\BigO\big(\E\Pi_{1}/N^{3}\big)\leq\frac{(c+\lvert\Delta\rvert)^{2}}{N^{3}}\E\Pi_{1}+\BigO\big(N^{-1}\big)=\BigO\big(N^{-1}\big).

By the triangle inequality, it therefore suffices to show that

\lim_{N\to\infty}\big\lvert\widetilde{F}_{N}-\widetilde{F}_{N}^{\circ}\big\rvert=0.

(A.6)

We now rewrite
\smash{\widetilde{F}_{N}}
in a way that more closely resembles
\smash{\widetilde{F}_{N}^{\circ}}
. For each pair
i\leq j
introduce the random index set

\mathcal{I}_{i,j}=\big\{k\leq\Pi_{1}\mid(i_{k},j_{k})=(i,j)\text{ or }(i_{k},j_{k})=(j,i)\big\},

and observe that

\displaystyle\widetilde{H}_{N}(\sigma)

\displaystyle=\sum_{i\leq j}\sum_{k\in\mathcal{I}_{i,j}}\bigg(G_{i_{k},j_{k}}^{k}\log(c+\Delta\sigma_{i_{k}}\sigma_{j_{k}})-\frac{c+\Delta\sigma_{i_{k}}\sigma_{j_{k}}}{N}\bigg)

\displaystyle=\sum_{i<j}\bigg(\widetilde{G}_{i,j}\log(c+\Delta\sigma_{i}\sigma_{j})-\frac{c+\Delta\sigma_{i}\sigma_{j}}{N}\bigg)-\sum_{i\leq N}\bigg(\widetilde{G}_{i,i}\log(c+\Delta)-\frac{c+\Delta}{N}\bigg)

for the random variables
