\widetilde{G}_{i,j}=\sum_{k\in\mathcal{I}_{i,j}}G_{i,j}^{k}.

The Poisson coloring theorem (see Chapter 5 in
[
40
]
) implies that
\smash{\widetilde{G}_{i,j}}
is a Poisson random variable with mean

\widetilde{\lambda}_{i,j}=\E\Pi_{1}\cdot\mathbb{P}\{(i_{1},j_{1})=(i,j)\text{ or }(i_{1},j_{1})=(j,i)\}\cdot\mathbb{P}\{G^{1}_{i,j}=1\}=\begin{cases}\frac{N-1}{N}\cdot\frac{c+\Delta\sigma_{i}^{*}\sigma_{j}^{*}}{N}&\text{if }i<j,\\
\frac{N-1}{2N}\cdot\frac{c+\Delta}{N}&\text{if }i=j.\end{cases}

If we introduce the Hamiltonian

\widetilde{H}_{N}^{\prime}(\sigma)=\sum_{i<j}\bigg(\widetilde{G}_{i,j}\log(c+\Delta\sigma_{i}\sigma_{j})-\frac{c+\Delta\sigma_{i}\sigma_{j}}{N}\bigg)

and its associated free energy

\widetilde{F}_{N}^{\prime}=\frac{1}{N}\E\log\int_{\Sigma_{N}}\exp\widetilde{H}_{N}^{\prime}(\sigma)\,\mathrm{d}P_{N}^{*}(\sigma),

then

\big\lvert\widetilde{F}_{N}^{\prime}-\widetilde{F}_{N}\big\rvert\leq\frac{\lvert\log(c+\Delta)\rvert}{N}\sum_{i\leq N}\E\widetilde{G}_{i,i}+\frac{c+\lvert\Delta\rvert}{N}\leq\frac{\lvert\log(c+\Delta)\rvert}{N}\sum_{i\leq N}\widetilde{\lambda}_{i,i}+\frac{2c}{N}=\BigO\big(N^{-1}\big).

Together with (
A.6
) and the triangle inequality, this means that it suffices to show that

\lim_{N\to\infty}\big\lvert\widetilde{F}_{N}^{\prime}-\widetilde{F}_{N}^{\circ}\big\rvert=0.

(A.7)

At this point, for any random vector
Y=(Y_{i,j})_{i<j}
introduce the Hamiltonian

\widetilde{H}_{N}(\sigma,Y)=\sum_{i<j}Y_{i,j}\log(c+\Delta\sigma_{i}\sigma_{j})

and the measure

\widetilde{P}_{N}^{*}(\sigma)=\exp\Big(-\sum_{i<j}\frac{c+\Delta\sigma_{i}\sigma_{j}}{N}\Big)P_{N}^{*}(\sigma).

Write

F_{N}(Y)=\frac{1}{N}\log\int_{\Sigma_{N}}\exp\widetilde{H}_{N}(\sigma,Y)\,\mathrm{d}\widetilde{P}_{N}^{*}(\sigma)\quad\text{and}\quad\widetilde{F}_{N}(Y)=\E F_{N}(Y)

for the associated free energy functionals, and denote by
\Pi_{i,j}
a Poisson random variable with distribution

\Pi_{i,j}\sim\Poi\Big(\frac{c+\Delta\sigma_{i}^{*}\sigma_{j}^{*}}{N}\Big)

conditionally on
\sigma^{*}
. Observe that

\partial_{Y_{i,j}}F_{N}(Y)=\frac{1}{N}\big\langle\partial_{Y_{i,j}}\widetilde{H}_{N}(\sigma,Y)\big\rangle=\frac{1}{N}\langle\log(c+\Delta\sigma_{i}\sigma_{j})\rangle\leq\frac{C}{N}

for some deterministic constant
C>0
that depends only on
c
. Here, the bracket
\langle\cdot\rangle
denotes the Gibbs measure associated with the Hamiltonian
\smash{\widetilde{H}_{N}(\sigma,Y)}
. It follows by the mean value theorem that

\displaystyle\big\lvert\widetilde{F}_{N}^{\prime}-\widetilde{F}_{N}(\Pi)\big\rvert

\displaystyle=\big\lvert\widetilde{F}_{N}(\widetilde{G})-\widetilde{F}_{N}(\Pi)\big\rvert\leq\frac{C}{N}\sum_{i<j}\E\big\lvert\widetilde{G}_{i,j}-\Pi_{i,j}\big\rvert

\displaystyle\big\lvert\widetilde{F}_{N}(\Pi)-\widetilde{F}_{N}^{\circ}\big\rvert

\displaystyle=\big\lvert\widetilde{F}_{N}(\Pi)-\widetilde{F}_{N}(G)\big\rvert\leq\frac{C}{N}\sum_{i<j}\E\big\lvert\Pi_{i,j}-G_{ij}\big\rvert.

To bound the first of the sums observe that
\smash{\Pi_{i,j}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{=}}\widetilde{G}_{i,j}+\Pi^{\prime}_{i,j}}
for a Poisson random variable with distribution

\Pi^{\prime}_{i,j}\sim\Poi\Big(\frac{c+\Delta\sigma_{i}^{*}\sigma_{j}^{*}}{N^{2}}\Big)

conditionally on
\sigma^{*}
. This means that

\big\lvert\widetilde{F}_{N}^{\prime}-\widetilde{F}_{N}(\Pi)\big\rvert\leq\frac{C}{N}\sum_{i<j}\E\Pi^{\prime}_{i,j}\leq\frac{Cc}{N}=\BigO\big(N^{-1}\big),

and therefore,

\big\lvert\widetilde{F}_{N}^{\prime}-\widetilde{F}_{N}^{\circ}\big\rvert\leq\frac{C}{N}\sum_{i<j}\E\big\lvert\Pi_{i,j}-G_{ij}\big\rvert+\BigO\big(N^{-1}\big).

(A.8)

If we write
\smash{\lambda_{i,j}=\frac{c+\Delta\sigma_{i}^{*}\sigma_{j}^{*}}{N}}
, then

\displaystyle\E\big\lvert\Pi_{i,j}-G_{ij}\big\rvert

\displaystyle\leq\E\lvert\Pi_{i,j}-G_{ij}\big\rvert\mathbbm{1}\{\Pi_{i,j}\geq 2\}+\E\lvert\Pi_{i,j}-G_{ij}\big\rvert\mathbbm{1}\{\Pi_{i,j}\leq 2\}\mathbbm{1}\{\Pi_{i,j}\neq G_{ij}\}

\displaystyle\leq\E\Pi_{i,j}\mathbbm{1}\{\Pi_{i,j}\geq 2\}+\mathbb{P}\{\Pi_{i,j}\geq 2\}+3\mathbb{P}\{\Pi_{i,j}\neq G_{ij}\}

\displaystyle\leq 3\mathbb{P}\{\Pi_{i,j}\neq G_{ij}\}+\BigO\big(\lambda_{i,j}^{2}\big),

where we have used the fact that
\lvert G_{ij}\rvert\leq 1
. Taking the infimum over all couplings of
\Pi_{i,j}
and
G_{ij}
, recalling the definition of the total variation distance in (
A.3
) and invoking the binomial-Poisson approximation in
Lemma

A.1
shows that

\E\big\lvert\Pi_{i,j}-G_{ij}\big\rvert\leq 3\mathrm{TV}\big(\Pi_{i,j},G_{ij}\big)+\BigO\big(\lambda_{i,j}^{2}\big)=\BigO\big(\lambda_{i,j}^{2}\big)=\BigO\big(N^{-2}\big).

Substituting this into (
A.8
) and letting
N
tend to infinity establishes (
A.7
). This completes the proof.
∎

Appendix B
Concentration of the free energy

In this appendix we discuss the concentration of the free energy associated with the perturbed Hamiltonian (
4.7
). For simplicity of notation, we will ignore the Hamiltonian (
1.34
) and focus instead on the perturbed Hamiltonian

H_{N}^{\prime}(\sigma)=H_{N}^{t}(\sigma)+H_{N}^{\mathrm{gauss}}(\sigma)+H_{N}^{\mathrm{exp}}(\sigma),

(B.1)

where the randomness of each of the Hamiltonians (
1.26
), (
4.1
) and (
4.5
) is independent of the randomness of the other Hamiltonians. The more general case is treated in an identical fashion but the notation becomes too cumbersome for comfort. We will often need to make the dependence of the perturbed Hamiltonian (
B.1
) on one of its sources of randomness
\smash{\sigma^{*}}
,
\smash{\Pi_{t}}
,
\smash{\mathcal{I}_{1}=(i_{k},j_{k})_{k\leq\Pi_{t}}}
,
\smash{\mathcal{G}=(G_{i_{k},j_{k}}^{k})_{k\leq\Pi_{t}}}
,
\smash{e=(e_{jk})}
,
\smash{\Pi^{\prime}=(\pi_{k})_{k\geq 0}}
,
\smash{\mathcal{I}_{2}=(i_{jk})_{j\leq\Pi^{\prime}}}
and
\smash{Z=(Z_{0,i})_{i\leq N}}
explicit. To do so, we will abuse notation and write
\smash{H_{N}^{\prime}(X)}
when we want to study the dependence on the source of randomness
X
. The main objects of study will be the free energy,

F_{N}^{\prime}=\frac{1}{N}\log\int\exp H_{N}^{\prime}(\sigma)\,\mathrm{d}P_{N}^{*}(\sigma),

(B.2)

and its average

\overline{F}_{N}^{\prime}=\frac{1}{N}\E\log\int\exp H_{N}^{\prime}(\sigma)\,\mathrm{d}P_{N}^{*}(\sigma).

(B.3)

For each even
p\geq 2
, we will bound the concentration function,

v_{N,p}=\sup\Big\{\E\big\lvert F_{N}^{\prime}-\overline{F}^{\prime}_{N}\big\rvert^{p}\mid\lambda_{k}\in[2^{-k-1},2^{-k}]\text{ for all }k\geq 0\Big\},

(B.4)

by means of the generalized Efron-Stein inequality (see Theorem 15.5 of
[
17
]
).

Lemma B.1

(Generalized Efron-Stein inequality)
.

Let
X=(X_{1},\ldots,X_{n})
and
X^{\prime}=(X_{1}^{\prime},\ldots,X_{n}^{\prime})
be two independent copies of a vector of independent random variables, and let
f:\mathbb{R}^{n}\to\mathbb{R}
be a measurable function. Introduce the random variable
Z=f(X)
, and for each
1\leq i\leq n
let
Z_{i}^{\prime}=f(X_{1},\ldots,X_{i-1},X_{i}^{\prime},X_{i+1},\ldots,X_{n})
. If
q\geq 2
, then

\E\lvert Z-\E Z\rvert^{q}\leq C\E\Big\lvert\sum_{i\leq n}\E_{X^{\prime}}(Z-Z_{i}^{\prime})^{2}\Big\rvert^{\frac{q}{2}},

(B.5)

where
C>0
is a constant that depends only on
q
.

A key observation that will be used repeatedly without further explanation is the following: given two sources of randomness
X
and
X^{\prime}
, a configuration-independent bound on the difference of the Hamiltonians
H_{N}^{\prime}(X)
and
H_{N}^{\prime}(X^{\prime})
,

\max_{\sigma\in\Sigma_{N}}\big\lvert H_{N}^{\prime}(X)-H_{N}^{\prime}(X^{\prime})\big\rvert\leq Y,

(B.6)

gives a control by the possibly random
Y
on the difference of the free energy functionals
F_{N}^{\prime}(X)
and
F_{N}^{\prime}(X^{\prime})
,

\big\lvert F_{N}^{\prime}(X)-F_{N}^{\prime}(X^{\prime})\big\rvert\leq\frac{Y}{N}.

(B.7)

The following bounds on the moments of Poisson and binomial random variables will also play their part.

Lemma B.2
.

If
\Pi
is a
\Poi(\lambda)
random variable for some
\lambda\geq 1
and
k\geq 2
is an integer, then

\E\Pi^{k}\leq C\lambda^{k}\quad\text{and}\quad\E\big(\Pi-\E\Pi)^{k}\leq C\lambda^{\lfloor k/2\rfloor}

(B.8)

for some constant
C>0
that depends only on
k
.

Proof.

Denote by
\smash{\left\{\genfrac{}{}{0.0pt}{}{k}{j}\right\}}
the number of ways to partition a
k
element set into
j
non-empty subsets. In combinatorics, such numbers are known as Stirling numbers of the second kind, and they have the property that for any integer
m\geq 0
,

m^{k}=\sum_{j\leq k}\left\{\genfrac{}{}{0.0pt}{}{k}{j}\right\}(m)_{j},

(B.9)

where
(m)_{j}=m(m-1)\cdots(m-j+1)
is the falling factorial. The basic properties of the Poisson distribution imply that

\displaystyle\E\Pi^{k}

\displaystyle=\sum_{m\geq 0}\sum_{j\leq k}\left\{\genfrac{}{}{0.0pt}{}{k}{j}\right\}(m)_{j}\frac{\lambda^{m}}{m!}\exp(-\lambda)=\sum_{j\leq k}\left\{\genfrac{}{}{0.0pt}{}{k}{j}\right\}\lambda^{j}\sum_{m\geq j}\frac{\lambda^{m-j}}{(m-j)!}\exp(-\lambda)=\sum_{j\leq k}\left\{\genfrac{}{}{0.0pt}{}{k}{j}\right\}\lambda^{j}

\displaystyle\leq\max(1,\lambda^{k})B_{k},

where
B_{k}
denotes the
k
’th Bell number. This establishes the first bound in (
B.8
). We now prove by induction that for each
k\geq 2
, the function
M_{k}(\lambda)=\E(\Pi-\E\Pi)^{k}
is a polynomial of degree
\lfloor k/2\rfloor
. The base case holds since
M_{2}(\lambda)=\Var\Pi=\lambda
, so assume the result holds for all
2\leq i\leq k
. By the product rule

\displaystyle M_{k}^{\prime}(\lambda)

\displaystyle=-\sum_{m\geq 0}k(m-\lambda)^{k-1}\frac{\lambda^{m}}{m!}\exp(-\lambda)+\sum_{m\geq 0}(m-\lambda)^{k}m\frac{\lambda^{m-1}}{m!}\exp(-\lambda)-M_{k}(\lambda)

\displaystyle=-kM_{k-1}(\lambda)+\sum_{m\geq 0}(m-\lambda)^{k}(m-\lambda+\lambda)\frac{\lambda^{m-1}}{m!}\exp(-\lambda)-M_{k}(\lambda)

\displaystyle=-kM_{k-1}(\lambda)+\frac{1}{\lambda}\big(M_{k+1}(\lambda)+\lambda M_{k}(\lambda)\big)-M_{k}(\lambda)

\displaystyle=-kM_{k-1}(\lambda)+\frac{1}{\lambda}M_{k+1}(\lambda).

Invoking the induction hypothesis shows that
M_{k+1}(\lambda)
has degree
\max(\lfloor k/2\rfloor,1+\lfloor(k-1)/2\rfloor)
. This completes the proof.
∎

Lemma B.3
.

If
X
is a
\Bin(n,p)
random variable with
np\geq 1
and
k\geq 1
is an integer, then

\E X^{k}\leq C(np)^{k}

(B.10)

for some constant
C>0
that depends only on
k
.

Proof.

Using (
B.9
) and identifying the probability density function of a
\Bin(n-j,p)
shows that

\displaystyle\E X^{k}

\displaystyle=\sum_{m\leq n}\sum_{j\leq k}\left\{\genfrac{}{}{0.0pt}{}{k}{j}\right\}(m)_{j}\frac{n!}{(n-m)!m!}p^{m}(1-p)^{n-m}

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{j\leq k}\left\{\genfrac{}{}{0.0pt}{}{k}{j}\right\}(np)^{j}\sum_{j\leq m\leq n}\frac{(n-j)!}{(n-m)!(m-j)!}p^{m-j}(1-p)^{n-m}

\displaystyle\leq\max(1,(np)^{k})B_{k},

where
B_{k}
denotes the
k
’th Bell number. This completes the proof.
∎

Proposition B.4
.

For any two sequences
(\epsilon_{N})
and
(s_{N})
satisfying (
4.3
) and (
4.4
) respectively and every even
p\geq 2
,

v_{N,p}\leq\frac{C(1+t^{p})}{N^{p/2}}

(B.11)

for some constant
C>0
that depends only on
p
,
c
and
\Delta
.

Proof.

To alleviate notation, write
C>0
for a constant that depends only on
p
,
c
and
\Delta
whose value might not be the same at each occurrence. Given a source of randomness
X
, write
\E_{X}
for the average with respect to the randomness of
X
. The proof will rely upon the generalized Efron-Stein inequality in
Lemma

B.1
and the fact that

\E=\E_{\sigma^{*}}\E_{Z}\E_{\Pi^{\prime}}\E_{\mathcal{I}_{2}}\E_{e}\E_{\Pi_{t}}\E_{\mathcal{I}_{1}}\E_{\mathcal{G}|\sigma^{*}}.

Introduce the averaged free energy functionals

\widehat{F}_{N}^{\prime}=\E_{\Pi_{t}}\E_{\mathcal{I}_{1}}\E_{\mathcal{G}|\sigma^{*}}F_{N}^{\prime}\qquad\text{and}\qquad\widetilde{F}_{N}^{\prime}=\E_{Z}\E_{\Pi^{\prime}}\E_{\mathcal{I}_{2}}\E_{e}\widehat{F}_{N}^{\prime}

in such a way that

\E\big(F_{N}^{\prime}-\overline{F}_{N}^{\prime}\big)^{p}\leq C\Big(\E\big(F_{N}^{\prime}-\widehat{F}_{N}^{\prime}\big)^{p}+\E\big(\widehat{F}_{N}^{\prime}-\widetilde{F}_{N}^{\prime}\big)^{p}+\E\big(\widetilde{F}_{N}^{\prime}-\overline{F}_{N}^{\prime}\big)^{p}\Big).

(B.12)

We will now bound each of these terms separately.

Step 1: proving
\E\big(F_{N}^{\prime}-\widehat{F}_{N}^{\prime}\big)^{p}=\BigO\big((t/N)^{p/2}\big)
.

We decompose this further into

\displaystyle\E\big(F_{N}^{\prime}-\widehat{F}_{N}^{\prime}\big)^{p}\leq C\Big(\E\big(F_{N}^{\prime}-\E_{\mathcal{G}|\sigma^{*}}F_{N}^{\prime}\big)^{p}

\displaystyle+\E\big(\E_{\mathcal{G}|\sigma^{*}}F_{N}^{\prime}-\E_{\mathcal{I}_{1}}\E_{\mathcal{G}|\sigma^{*}}F_{N}^{\prime}\big)^{p}

\displaystyle+\E\big(\E_{\mathcal{I}_{1}}\E_{\mathcal{G}|\sigma^{*}}F_{N}^{\prime}-\widehat{F}_{N}^{\prime}\big)^{p}\Big)=C\big(I+II+III\big),

(B.13)

and proceed to bound
I
,
II
and
III
individually. By the generalized Efron-Stein inequality

I\leq C\E\bigg\lvert\sum_{\ell\leq\Pi_{t}}\E_{\mathcal{G}^{(\ell)}|\sigma^{*}}\Big(F_{N}^{\prime}(\mathcal{G})-F_{N}^{\prime}\big(\mathcal{G}^{(\ell)}\big)\Big)^{2}\bigg\rvert^{p/2},

where
\smash{\big(\widetilde{G}^{(\ell)}_{i,j}\big)_{i,j\in\mathbb{N}}}
is an independent copy of
\smash{\big(G^{(\ell)}_{i,j}\big)_{i,j\in\mathbb{N}}}
. Since
\lvert\Delta\rvert<c
and all spin configuration coordinates are bounded by one,

\displaystyle\big|H_{N}^{\prime}(\mathcal{G})-H_{N}^{\prime}\big(\mathcal{G}^{(\ell)}\big)\big\rvert\leq\lvert\log(c+

\displaystyle\Delta\sigma_{i_{\ell}}\sigma_{j_{\ell}})\rvert\big\lvert G^{\ell}_{i_{\ell},j_{\ell}}-\widetilde{G}_{i_{\ell},j_{\ell}}^{\ell}\big\rvert

\displaystyle+\Big\lvert\log\Big(1-\frac{c+\Delta\sigma_{i_{\ell}}\sigma_{j_{\ell}}}{N}\Big)\Big\rvert\big\lvert G_{i_{\ell},j_{\ell}}^{\ell}-\widetilde{G}_{i_{\ell},j_{\ell}}^{\ell}\big\rvert\leq C\big\lvert G^{\ell}_{i_{\ell},j_{\ell}}-\widetilde{G}_{i_{\ell},j_{\ell}}^{\ell}\big\rvert.

It follows that

\displaystyle I

\displaystyle\leq\frac{C}{N^{p}}\E\Big\lvert\sum_{\ell\leq\Pi_{t}}\E_{\mathcal{G}^{(\ell)}|\sigma^{*}}\big\lvert G^{\ell}_{i_{\ell},j_{\ell}}-\widetilde{G}_{i_{\ell},j_{\ell}}^{\ell}\big\rvert^{2}\Big\rvert^{p/2}\leq\frac{C}{N^{p}}\E\Big\lvert\sum_{\ell\leq\Pi_{t}}\Big(\Big(1-\frac{2}{N}\Big)G_{i_{\ell},j_{\ell}}^{\ell}+\frac{1}{N}\Big)\Big\rvert^{p/2}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{C}{N^{p}}\Big(\E\Big\lvert\sum_{\ell\leq\Pi_{t}}G_{i_{\ell},j_{\ell}}^{\ell}\Big\rvert^{p/2}+\frac{1}{N^{p/2}}\E\Pi_{t}^{p/2}\Big).

Notice that
\smash{\sum_{\ell\leq\Pi_{t}}G_{i_{\ell},j_{\ell}}^{\ell}}
follows a
\smash{\Bin\Big(\Pi_{t},\frac{c+\Delta\sigma_{i_{\ell}}^{*}\sigma_{j_{\ell}}^{*}}{N}\Big)}
distribution conditionally on
\Pi_{t}
. Invoking
Lemma

B.2
and
Lemma

B.3
yields

I\leq\frac{C}{N^{p+\frac{p}{2}}}\E\Pi_{t}^{p/2}\leq\frac{Ct^{p/2}}{N^{p/2}}.

(B.14)

Another application of the generalized Efron-Stein inequality gives

II\leq\E\bigg\lvert\sum_{\ell\leq\Pi_{t}}\E_{\mathcal{I}_{1}^{(\ell)}}\Big(\E_{\mathcal{G}|\sigma^{*}}F_{N}^{\prime}(\mathcal{I}_{1})-\E_{\mathcal{G}|\sigma^{*}}F_{N}^{\prime}\big(\mathcal{I}_{1}^{(\ell)}\big)\Big)^{2}\bigg\rvert^{p/2},

where
\mathcal{I}_{1}^{(\ell)}
has an independent copy
\smash{(i_{\ell}^{\prime},j_{\ell}^{\prime})}
of
\smash{(i_{\ell},j_{\ell})}
at the
\ell
’th coordinate but otherwise coincides with
\mathcal{I}_{1}
. Taylor expanding the logarithm and remembering that
\smash{G_{i_{\ell},j_{\ell}}^{\ell}\in\{0,1\}}
, it is readily verified that

\big\lvert H_{N}^{\prime}(\mathcal{I}_{1})\big\rvert\leq\big\lvert G_{i_{\ell},j_{\ell}}^{\ell}\big\rvert\lvert\log(c+\Delta\sigma_{i_{\ell}}\sigma_{j_{l}})\rvert+\big\lvert 1-G_{i_{\ell},j_{\ell}}^{\ell}\big\rvert\Big\lvert\log\Big(1-\frac{c+\Delta\sigma_{i_{\ell}}\sigma_{j_{\ell}}}{N}\Big)\Big\rvert\leq C\Big(\big\lvert G_{i_{\ell},j_{\ell}}^{\ell}\big\rvert+\frac{1}{N}\Big).

This means that
\smash{\big\lvert H_{N}^{\prime}(\mathcal{I}_{1})-H_{N}^{\prime}\big(\mathcal{I}_{1}^{(\ell)})\big\rvert\leq C\big(\lvert G_{i_{\ell},j_{\ell}}^{\ell}\rvert+\lvert G_{i^{\prime}_{\ell},j^{\prime}_{\ell}}^{\ell}\rvert+\frac{1}{N}\big)}
, and therefore

\Big(\E_{\mathcal{G}|\sigma^{*}}F_{N}^{\prime}(\mathcal{I}_{1})-\E_{\mathcal{G}|\sigma^{*}}F_{N}^{\prime}\big(\mathcal{I}_{1}^{(\ell)}\big)\Big)^{2}\leq\frac{C}{N^{2}}\Big(\E_{\mathcal{G}|\sigma^{*}}\big\lvert G_{i_{\ell},j_{\ell}}^{\ell}\big\rvert+\E_{\mathcal{G}|\sigma^{*}}\big\lvert G_{i_{\ell}^{\prime},j_{\ell}^{\prime}}^{\ell}\big\rvert+\frac{1}{N}\Big)^{2}\leq\frac{C}{N^{4}}.

It follows that

II\leq\frac{C}{N^{2p}}\E\Pi_{t}^{p/2}\leq\frac{Ct^{p/2}}{N^{p}}\leq\frac{Ct^{p/2}}{N^{p/2}}.

(B.15)

A final application of the generalized Efron-Stein inequality reveals that

III\leq\E\Big\lvert\E_{\Pi_{t}^{\prime}}\big(\E_{\mathcal{I}_{1}}\E_{\mathcal{G}|\sigma^{*}}F_{N}^{\prime}(\Pi_{t})-\E_{\mathcal{I}_{1}}\E_{\mathcal{G}|\sigma^{*}}F_{N}^{\prime}(\Pi^{\prime}_{t})\big)^{2}\Big\rvert^{p/2},

where
\Pi_{t}^{\prime}
is an independent copy of
\Pi_{t}
. Slightly abusing notation and redefining
\Pi_{t}^{\prime}
to be the maximum between
\Pi_{t}
and
\Pi_{t}^{\prime}
, we see that

\displaystyle\big\lvert H_{N}^{\prime}(\Pi_{t}^{\prime})-H_{N}^{\prime}(\Pi_{t})\big\rvert

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{\Pi_{t}\leq k\leq\Pi_{t}^{\prime}}\bigg(\big\lvert G_{i_{k},j_{k}}^{k}\big\rvert\lvert\log(c+\Delta\sigma_{i_{k}}\sigma_{j_{k}})\rvert+\big\lvert 1-G_{i_{k},j_{k}}^{k}\big\rvert\Big\lvert\log\Big(1-\frac{c+\Delta\sigma_{i_{k}}\sigma_{j_{k}}}{N}\Big)\Big\rvert\bigg)

\displaystyle\leq C\sum_{\Pi_{t}\leq k\leq\Pi_{t}^{\prime}}\Big(\big\lvert G_{i_{k},j_{k}}^{k}\big\rvert+\frac{1}{N}\Big).

It follows that

\big\lvert\E_{\mathcal{I}_{1}}\E_{\mathcal{G}|\sigma^{*}}F_{N}^{\prime}(\Pi_{t})-\E_{\mathcal{I}_{1}}\E_{\mathcal{G}|\sigma^{*}}F_{N}^{\prime}(\Pi^{\prime}_{t})\big\rvert\leq\frac{C}{N}\E_{\mathcal{I}_{1}}\E_{\mathcal{G}|\sigma^{*}}\sum_{\Pi_{t}\leq k\leq\Pi_{t}^{\prime}}\Big(\big\lvert G_{i_{k},j_{k}}^{k}\big\rvert+\frac{1}{N}\Big)\leq\frac{C}{N^{2}}\lvert\Pi_{t}^{\prime}-\Pi_{t}\rvert,

and by Jensen’s inequality and
Lemma

B.2
,

III\leq\frac{C}{N^{2p}}\E\big\lvert\E_{\Pi_{t}^{\prime}}\lvert\Pi_{t}^{\prime}-\Pi_{t}\rvert^{2}\big\rvert^{p/2}\leq\frac{C}{N^{2p}}\E\lvert\Pi_{t}-\E\Pi_{t}\rvert^{p}\leq\frac{Ct^{p/2}}{N^{p}}\leq\frac{Ct^{p/2}}{N^{p/2}}.

(B.16)

Combining (
B
), (
B.14
), (
B.15
) and (
B.16
) reveals that
\E\big(F_{N}^{\prime}-\widehat{F}_{N}^{\prime}\big)^{p}=\BigO\big((t/N)^{p/2}\big)
.

Step 2: proving
\E\big(\widehat{F}_{N}^{\prime}-\widetilde{F}_{N}^{\prime}\big)^{p}=\BigO\big(N^{-p/2}\big).

We decompose this further into

\displaystyle\E\big(\widehat{F}_{N}^{\prime}-\widetilde{F}_{N}^{\prime}\big)^{p}\leq C\Big(\E\big(\widehat{F}_{N}^{\prime}-

\displaystyle\E_{e}\widehat{F}_{N}^{\prime}\big)^{p}+\E\big(\E_{e}\widehat{F}_{N}^{\prime}-\E_{\mathcal{I}_{2}}\E_{e}\widehat{F}_{N}^{\prime}\big)^{p}+\E\big(\E_{\mathcal{I}_{2}}\E_{e}\widehat{F}_{N}^{\prime}-\E_{\Pi^{\prime}}\E_{\mathcal{I}_{2}}\E_{e}\widehat{F}_{N}^{\prime}\big)^{p}

\displaystyle+\E\big(\E_{\Pi^{\prime}}\E_{\mathcal{I}_{2}}\E_{e}\widehat{F}_{N}^{\prime}-\widetilde{F}_{N}^{\prime}\big)^{p}\Big)=C\big(I+II+III+IV\big),

(B.17)

and proceed to bound
I
,
II
,
III
and
IV
individually. By the generalized Efron-Stein inequality

I\leq\E\bigg\lvert\sum_{k\geq 0}\sum_{j\leq\pi_{k}}\E_{e^{(jk)}}\Big(\widehat{F}_{N}^{\prime}(e)-\widehat{F}_{N}^{\prime}\big(e^{(jk)}\big)\Big)^{2}\bigg\rvert^{p/2},

where
e^{(jk)}
has an independent copy
e_{jk}^{\prime}
of
e_{jk}
at the
jk
’th coordinate but otherwise coincides with
e
. Since

\big\lvert H_{N}^{\prime}(e)-H_{N}^{\prime}\big(e^{(jk)}\big)\big\rvert\leq\frac{\lvert\lambda_{k}\sigma_{i_{jk}}\rvert}{\lvert 1+\lambda_{k}\sigma_{i_{jk}}^{*}\rvert}\big\lvert e_{jk}-e^{\prime}_{jk}\big\rvert\leq\frac{\lambda_{k}}{1-\lambda_{k}}\big\lvert e_{jk}-e^{\prime}_{jk}\big\rvert,

and
\lambda_{k}\in[2^{-k-1},2^{-k}]
, we have

I\leq\frac{C}{N^{p}}\E\Big\lvert\sum_{k\geq 0}\frac{1}{2^{k}}\cdot\frac{1}{2^{k}}\sum_{j\leq\pi_{k}}\E_{e_{jk}^{\prime}}\big\lvert e_{jk}-e^{\prime}_{jk}\big\rvert^{2}\Big\rvert^{p/2}.

It follows by two applications of Hölder’s inequality and Jensen’s inequality that

I\leq\frac{C}{N^{p}}\E\sum_{k\geq 0}\Big(\frac{1}{2^{k}}\sum_{j\leq\pi_{k}}\E_{e_{jk}^{\prime}}\big\lvert e_{jk}-e_{jk}^{\prime}\big\rvert^{2}\Big)^{p/2}\leq\frac{C}{N^{p}}\E\sum_{k\geq 0}\frac{1}{2^{\frac{kp}{2}}}\pi_{k}^{\frac{p}{2}-1}\sum_{j\leq\pi_{k}}\E_{e_{jk}^{\prime}}\big\lvert e_{jk}-e_{jk}^{\prime}\big\rvert^{p}.

Recalling that
e_{jk}\sim\Exp(1)
while
\pi_{jk}\sim\Poi(s_{N})
and invoking
Lemma

B.2
gives

I\leq\frac{C}{N^{p}}\sum_{k\geq 0}\frac{1}{2^{\frac{kp}{2}}}\E\pi_{k}^{\frac{p}{2}}\leq C\Big(\frac{s_{N}}{N^{2}}\Big)^{p/2}\leq\frac{C}{N^{p/2}}.

(B.18)

Similarly, by the generalized Efron-Stein inequality,

II\leq C\E\bigg\lvert\sum_{k\geq 0}\sum_{j\leq\pi_{k}}\E_{\mathcal{I}_{2}^{(jk)}}\Big(\E_{e}\widehat{F}_{N}^{\prime}(\mathcal{I}_{2})-\E_{e}\widehat{F}_{N}^{\prime}\big(\mathcal{I}_{2}^{(jk)}\big)\Big)^{2}\bigg\rvert^{p/2},

where
\mathcal{I}_{2}^{(jk)}
has an independent copy
i^{\prime}_{jk}
of
i_{jk}
at the
jk
’th coordinate but otherwise coincides with
\mathcal{I}_{2}
. By the mean value theorem,

\displaystyle\big\lvert H_{N}^{\prime}(\mathcal{I}_{2})-H_{N}^{\prime}\big(\mathcal{I}^{(jk)}_{2}\big)\big\rvert

\displaystyle\leq\lvert\log(1+\lambda_{k}\sigma_{i_{jk}})-\log(1+\lambda_{k}\sigma_{i_{jk}^{\prime}})\rvert+\lambda_{k}e_{jk}\Big\lvert\frac{\sigma_{i_{jk}}}{1+\lambda_{k}\sigma^{*}_{i_{jk}}}-\frac{\sigma_{i_{jk}^{\prime}}}{1+\lambda_{k}\sigma^{*}_{i_{jk}^{\prime}}}\Big\rvert

\displaystyle\leq C\lambda_{k}(1+e_{jk}).

It follows once again by two applications of Hölder’s inequality and
Lemma

B.2
that

II\leq\frac{C}{N^{p}}\E\sum_{k\geq 0}\frac{1}{2^{\frac{kp}{2}}}\pi_{k}^{\frac{p}{2}-1}\sum_{j\leq\pi_{k}}(1+e_{jk})^{p}\leq C\Big(\frac{s_{N}}{N^{2}}\Big)^{p/2}\leq\frac{C}{N^{p/2}}.

(B.19)

Another application of the generalized Efron-Stein inequality yields

III\leq C\E\bigg\lvert\sum_{k\geq 0}\E_{\Pi^{\prime(k)}}\Big(\E_{\mathcal{I}_{2}}\E_{e}\widehat{F}_{N}^{\prime}(\Pi^{\prime})-\E_{\mathcal{I}_{2}}\E_{e}\widehat{F}_{N}^{\prime}\big(\Pi^{\prime(k)}\big)\Big)^{2}\bigg\rvert^{p/2},

where
\smash{\Pi^{\prime(k)}}
has an independent copy
\pi_{k}^{\prime}
of
\pi_{k}
at the
k
’th coordinate but otherwise coincides with
\Pi^{\prime}
. Slightly abusing notation and redefining
\smash{\Pi^{\prime(k)}}
to be the process with the larger
k
’th coordinate, we see that

\displaystyle\big\lvert H_{N}^{\prime}(\Pi^{\prime})-H_{N}^{\prime}(\Pi^{\prime(k)})\big\rvert

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{\pi_{k}\leq j\leq\pi_{k}^{\prime}}\Big\lvert\log(1+\lambda_{k}\sigma_{i_{jk}})-\frac{\lambda_{k}e_{jk}\sigma_{i_{jk}}}{1+\lambda_{k}\sigma^{*}_{i_{jk}}}\Big\rvert

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{\pi_{k}\leq j\leq\pi_{k}^{\prime}}\bigg(\Big\lvert\lambda_{k}\sigma_{i_{jk}}-\frac{\lambda_{k}e_{jk}\sigma_{i_{jk}}}{1+\lambda_{k}\sigma^{*}_{i_{jk}}}\Big\rvert+C\lambda_{k}^{2}\bigg)

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{\pi_{k}\leq j\leq\pi_{k}^{\prime}}\bigg(\lambda_{k}\Big\lvert 1-\frac{e_{jk}}{1+\lambda_{k}\sigma^{*}_{i_{jk}}}\Big\rvert+C\lambda_{k}^{2}\bigg)

\displaystyle\leq\lambda_{k}\sum_{\pi_{k}\leq j\leq\pi_{k}^{\prime}}\bigg(\frac{\lvert 1-e_{jk}\rvert+\lambda_{k}}{1-\lambda_{k}}+C\lambda_{k}\bigg).

It follows by two applications of the Cauchy-Schwarz inequality that

\displaystyle III

\displaystyle\leq\frac{C}{N^{p}}\E\sum_{k\geq 0}\frac{1}{2^{\frac{kp}{2}}}\bigg(\sum_{\pi_{k}\leq j\leq\pi_{k}^{\prime}}\bigg(\frac{\lvert 1-e_{jk}\rvert+\lambda_{k}}{1-\lambda_{k}}+C\lambda_{k}\bigg)\bigg)^{p}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{C}{N^{p}}\E\sum_{k\geq 0}\frac{1}{2^{\frac{kp}{2}}}\lvert\pi_{k}-\pi_{k}^{\prime}\rvert^{p-1}\sum_{\pi_{k}\leq j\leq\pi_{k}^{\prime}}\bigg(\frac{\lvert 1-e_{jk}\rvert+\lambda_{k}}{1-\lambda_{k}}+C\lambda_{k}\bigg)^{p}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{C}{N^{p}}\sum_{k\geq 0}\frac{1}{2^{\frac{kp}{2}}}\E\lvert\pi_{k}-\pi_{k}^{\prime}\rvert^{p}.

Since
\E\lvert\pi_{k}-\pi_{k}^{\prime}\rvert^{p}\leq C\E\lvert\pi_{k}-\E\pi_{k}\rvert^{p}\leq s_{N}^{p/2}
by
Lemma

B.2
, this implies that

III\leq C\Big(\frac{s_{N}}{N^{2}}\Big)^{p/2}\leq\frac{C}{N^{p/2}}.

(B.20)

A final application of the generalized Efron-Stein inequality gives

IV\leq C\E\bigg\lvert\sum_{i\leq N}\E_{Z^{(i)}}\Big(\E_{\Pi^{\prime}}\E_{\mathcal{I}_{2}}\E_{e}\widehat{F}_{N}^{\prime}(Z)-\E_{\Pi^{\prime}}\E_{\mathcal{I}_{2}}\E_{e}\widehat{F}_{N}^{\prime}\big(Z^{(i)}\big)\Big)^{2}\bigg\rvert^{p/2},

where
Z^{(i)}
has an independent copy
Z_{i,0}^{\prime}
of
Z_{i,0}
at the
i
’th coordinate but otherwise coincides with
Z
. Combining Hölder’s inequality with the bound

\big\lvert H_{N}^{\prime}(Z)-H_{N}^{\prime}\big(Z^{(i)}\big)\big\rvert\leq\sqrt{\lambda_{0}\epsilon_{N}}\big\lvert Z_{i,0}-Z_{i,0}^{\prime}\big\rvert

reveals that

IV\leq C\Big(\frac{\lambda_{0}\epsilon_{N}}{N}\Big)^{p}\E\Big\lvert\sum_{i\leq N}\E_{Z^{(i)}}\big\lvert Z_{i,0}-Z_{i,0}^{\prime}\big\rvert^{2}\Big\rvert^{p/2}\leq\frac{C}{N^{p}}N^{\frac{p}{2}-1}\sum_{i\leq N}\E\big\lvert Z_{i,0}-Z_{i,0}^{\prime}\big\rvert^{p}\leq\frac{C}{N^{p/2}}.

(B.21)

Together with (
B
), (
B.18
), (
B.19
) and (
B.20
), this shows that
\E\big(\widehat{F}_{N}^{\prime}-\widetilde{F}_{N}^{\prime}\big)^{p}=\BigO\big(N^{-p/2}\big)
.

Step 3: proving
\E\big(\widetilde{F}_{N}^{\prime}-\overline{F}_{N}^{\prime}\big)^{p}=\BigO\big((t^{2}/N)^{p/2}\big)

Controlling the final term in (
B.12
) requires more care since
\widetilde{F}_{N}^{\prime}
depends on
\sigma^{*}
both through
F_{N}^{\prime}
and through the conditional expectation
\E_{\mathcal{G}|\sigma^{*}}
. To simplify notation, write
\E^{\prime}=\E_{Z}\E_{\Pi^{\prime}}\E_{\mathcal{I}_{2}}\E_{e}\E_{\Pi_{t}}\E_{\mathcal{I}_{1}}
in such a way that by the generalized Efron-Stein inequality

\displaystyle\E\big(\widetilde{F}_{N}^{\prime}-\overline{F}_{N}^{\prime}\big)^{p}

\displaystyle\leq C\E\bigg\lvert\sum_{\ell\leq N}\E_{\sigma^{*,(\ell)}}\Big(\E^{\prime}\E_{\mathcal{G}|\sigma^{*}}F_{N}^{\prime}(\sigma^{*})-\E^{\prime}\E_{\mathcal{G}|\sigma^{*,(\ell)}}F_{N}^{\prime}\big(\sigma^{*,(\ell)}\big)\Big)^{2}\bigg\rvert^{p/2}

\displaystyle\leq C\E\bigg\lvert\sum_{\ell\leq N}\E_{\sigma^{*,(\ell)}}\Big(\E^{\prime}\E_{\mathcal{G}|\sigma^{*}}F_{N}^{\prime}(\sigma^{*})-\E^{\prime}\E_{\mathcal{G}|\sigma^{*}}F_{N}^{\prime}\big(\sigma^{*,(\ell)}\big)\Big)^{2}\bigg\rvert^{p/2}

\displaystyle\quad+C\E\bigg\lvert\sum_{\ell\leq N}\E_{\sigma^{*,(\ell)}}\Big(\E^{\prime}\E_{\mathcal{G}|\sigma^{*}}F_{N}^{\prime}\big(\sigma^{*,(\ell)}\big)-\E^{\prime}\E_{\mathcal{G}|\sigma^{*,(\ell)}}F_{N}^{\prime}\big(\sigma^{*,(\ell)}\big)\Big)^{2}\bigg\rvert^{p/2}

\displaystyle=C(I+II),

(B.22)

where
\sigma^{*,(\ell)}
has an independent copy
\widetilde{\sigma}^{*}_{\ell}
of
\sigma^{*}_{\ell}
at the
\ell
’th coordinate but otherwise coincides with
\sigma^{*}
. Since

\displaystyle\big\lvert H_{N}^{\prime}(\sigma^{*})-H_{N}^{\prime}(\sigma^{*,(l)})\big\rvert

\displaystyle\leq\lambda_{0}\epsilon_{N}\lvert\sigma_{i}\rvert\big\lvert\sigma^{*}_{\ell}-\widetilde{\sigma}_{\ell}^{*}\big\rvert+\sum_{k\geq 1}\sum_{j:i_{jk}=\ell}\bigg\lvert\frac{\lambda_{k}e_{jk}\sigma_{\ell}}{1+\lambda_{k}\sigma^{*}_{\ell}}-\frac{\lambda_{k}e_{jk}\sigma_{\ell}}{1+\lambda_{k}\widetilde{\sigma}^{*}_{\ell}}\bigg\rvert

\displaystyle\leq 2\epsilon_{N}+\sum_{k\geq 1}\sum_{j:i_{jk}=\ell}\frac{2\lambda_{k}^{2}e_{jk}}{(1-\lambda_{k})^{2}},

and
\E e_{jk}=1
, the Fubini-Tonelli theorem and the basic properties of the multinomial distribution imply that

\displaystyle\big\lvert\E^{\prime}\E_{\mathcal{G}|\sigma^{*}}F_{N}^{\prime}(\sigma^{*})-\E^{\prime}\E_{\mathcal{G}|\sigma^{*}}F_{N}^{\prime}\big(\sigma^{*,(\ell)}\big)\big\rvert

\displaystyle\leq\frac{2\epsilon_{N}}{N}+\frac{1}{N}\E_{\Pi^{\prime}}\sum_{k\geq 1}\frac{2\lambda_{k}^{2}}{(1-\lambda_{k})^{2}}\E_{\mathcal{I}_{2}}\lvert\{j:i_{k}=\ell\}\rvert

\displaystyle\leq\frac{2}{N}+\frac{1}{N^{2}}\E_{\Pi^{\prime}}\sum_{k\geq 1}\frac{2\lambda_{k}^{2}}{(1-\lambda_{k})^{2}}\pi_{k}\leq\frac{2}{N}+\frac{s_{N}}{N^{2}}\leq\frac{3}{N}.

It follows that

I\leq C\Big(\frac{N}{N^{2}}\Big)^{p/2}=\frac{C}{N^{p/2}}.

(B.23)

To bound
II
we will use an interpolation argument. Fix
1\leq\ell\leq N
and condition on all sources of randomness other than
\mathcal{G}
. For each
u\in[0,1]
,
G\in\{0,1\}^{\Pi_{t}}
and
k\leq\Pi_{t}
, let

\displaystyle P_{u}^{1,k}(G)

\displaystyle=G_{k}\bigg(\frac{c+\Delta\sigma_{\ell}^{*,u}\sigma^{*}_{j_{k}}}{N}\bigg)+(1-G_{k})\bigg(1-\frac{c+\Delta\sigma_{\ell}^{*,u}\sigma^{*}_{j_{k}}}{N}\bigg),

\displaystyle P_{u}^{2,k}(G)

\displaystyle=G_{k}\bigg(\frac{c+\Delta\sigma_{\ell}^{*,u}\sigma^{*}_{i_{k}}}{N}\bigg)+(1-G_{k})\bigg(1-\frac{c+\Delta\sigma_{\ell}^{*,u}\sigma^{*}_{i_{k}}}{N}\bigg),

\displaystyle P_{u}^{3}(G)

\displaystyle=G_{k}\bigg(\frac{c+\Delta(\sigma_{\ell}^{*,u})^{2}}{N}\bigg)+(1-G_{k})\bigg(1-\frac{c+\Delta(\sigma_{\ell}^{*,u})^{2}}{N}\bigg),

where
\sigma_{\ell}^{*,u}=(1-u)\sigma^{*}_{\ell}+u\widetilde{\sigma}^{*}_{\ell}
.
Write
\sigma^{*,u}
for the configuration with
\ell
’th coordinate
\sigma_{\ell}^{*,u}
which otherwise coincides with
\sigma^{*}
, and introduce the sets

\displaystyle\mathcal{I}_{1}^{1}

\displaystyle=\{k\mid i_{k}=l\text{ and }j_{k}\neq l\}

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\mathcal{I}_{1}^{3}

\displaystyle=\{k\mid i_{k}=j_{k}=l\}

\displaystyle\mathcal{I}_{1}^{2}

\displaystyle=\{k\mid i_{k}\neq l\text{ and }j_{k}=l\}

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\mathcal{I}_{1}^{4}

\displaystyle=\{k\mid i_{k}\neq l\neq j_{k}\}.

Denote
\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}=(\mathcal{G}_{k})_{k\in\mathcal{I}_{1}^{4}}
,
\mathcal{G}^{(k)}=\mathcal{G}\setminus\mathcal{G}_{k}
,
\widetilde{G}=(G_{k})_{k\in\mathcal{I}_{1}^{4}}
and
G^{(k)}=G\setminus G_{k}
. Define the interpolating free energy

\varphi(u)=\sum_{G\in\{0,1\}^{\Pi_{t}}}F_{N}^{\prime}\big(\sigma^{*,(\ell)},G\big)\mathbb{P}\big\{\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}=\widetilde{G}|\sigma^{*}\big\}\cdot\prod_{k\in\mathcal{I}_{1}^{1}}P_{u}^{1,k}(G)\prod_{k\in\mathcal{I}_{1}^{2}}P_{u}^{2,k}(G)\prod_{k\in\mathcal{I}_{1}^{3}}P_{u}^{3,k}(G)

in such a way that
\varphi(1)=\E_{\mathcal{G}|\sigma^{*}}F_{N}^{\prime}\big(\sigma^{*,(\ell)}\big)
and
\varphi(0)=\E_{\mathcal{G}|\sigma^{*,(\ell)}}F_{N}^{\prime}\big(\sigma^{*,(\ell)}\big)
. By the product rule,

\displaystyle\varphi^{\prime}(u)

\displaystyle=\sum_{k\in\cup_{i\leq 3}\mathcal{I}_{1}^{i}}\sum_{G\in\{0,1\}^{\Pi_{t}}}F_{N}^{\prime}\big(\sigma^{*,(\ell)},G\big)\mathbb{P}\big\{\mathcal{G}^{(k)}=G^{(k)}|\sigma^{*,u}\big\}(2G_{k}-1)

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\Bigg(\frac{\Delta(\widetilde{\sigma}_{\ell}^{*}-\sigma^{*}_{\ell})(\sigma_{j_{k}}^{*}\mathbbm{1}_{\{k\in\mathcal{I}_{1}^{1}\}}+\sigma_{i_{k}}^{*}\mathbbm{1}_{\{k\in\mathcal{I}_{1}^{2}\}}+2\sigma_{\ell}^{*,u}\mathbbm{1}_{\{k\in\mathcal{I}_{1}^{3}\}})}{N}\Bigg)

\displaystyle=\sum_{k\in\mathcal{I}_{1}^{1}}D_{k}\frac{\Delta(\widetilde{\sigma}_{\ell}^{*}-\sigma^{*}_{\ell})\sigma_{j_{k}}^{*}}{N}+\sum_{k\in\mathcal{I}_{1}^{2}}D_{k}\frac{\Delta(\widetilde{\sigma}_{\ell}^{*}-\sigma^{*}_{\ell})\sigma_{i_{k}}^{*}}{N}

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad+\sum_{k\in\mathcal{I}_{1}^{3}}D_{k}\frac{2\Delta\big((1-u)\sigma_{\ell}^{*}+u\widetilde{\sigma}_{\ell}^{*}\big)(\widetilde{\sigma}_{\ell}^{*}-\sigma_{\ell}^{*})}{N}

for
D_{k}=\E_{\mathcal{G}^{(k)}|\sigma^{*,u}}F_{N}^{\prime}\big(\sigma^{*,(\ell)},\mathcal{G}^{(k)},\mathcal{G}_{k}=1\big)-\E_{\mathcal{G}^{(k)}|\sigma^{*,u}}F_{N}^{\prime}\big(\sigma^{*,(\ell)},\mathcal{G}^{(k)},\mathcal{G}_{k}=0\big)
. Since

\big\lvert H_{N}^{\prime}\big(\mathcal{G}^{(k)},\mathcal{G}_{k}=1\big)-H_{N}^{\prime}\big(\mathcal{G}^{(k)},\mathcal{G}_{k}=0\big)\big\rvert\leq\Big\rvert\log(c+\Delta\sigma_{i_{k}}\sigma_{j_{k}})-\log\Big(1-\frac{c+\Delta\sigma_{i_{k}}\sigma_{j_{k}}}{N}\Big)\Big\lvert\leq C,

we have
\lvert D_{k}\rvert\leq\frac{C}{N}
, so the fundamental theorem of calculus yields

\big\lvert\E_{\mathcal{G}|\sigma^{*}}F_{N}^{\prime}\big(\sigma^{*,(\ell)}\big)-\E_{\mathcal{G}|\sigma^{*,(\ell)}}F_{N}^{\prime}\big(\sigma^{*,(l)}\big)\big\rvert\leq\sup_{u\in(0,1)}\lvert\varphi^{\prime}(u)\rvert\leq\frac{C}{N^{2}}\big(\lvert\mathcal{I}_{1}^{1}\rvert+\lvert\mathcal{I}_{1}^{2}\rvert+\lvert\mathcal{I}_{1}^{3}\rvert\big).

It follows by the basic properties of the binomial distribution that

\displaystyle\Big(\E^{\prime}\E_{\mathcal{G}|\sigma^{*}}F_{N}^{\prime}\big(\sigma^{*,(\ell)}\big)-\E^{\prime}\E_{\mathcal{G}|\sigma^{*,(\ell)}}F_{N}^{\prime}\big(\sigma^{*,(\ell)}\big)\Big)^{2}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{C}{N^{4}}\sum_{1\leq i\leq 3}\big(\E_{\Pi_{t}}\lvert\mathcal{I}_{1}^{i}\rvert\big)^{2}=\frac{C}{N^{4}}\sum_{1\leq i\leq 3}\Big(\E_{\Pi_{t}}\frac{\Pi_{t}}{N}\Big)^{2}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{Ct^{2}}{N^{2}},

and thus

II\leq C\bigg(\frac{Nt^{2}}{N^{2}}\bigg)^{p/2}=\frac{Ct^{p}}{N^{p/2}}.

(B.24)

Combining (
B
), (
B.23
) and (
B.24
) reveals that
\E\big(\widetilde{F}_{N}^{\prime}-\overline{F}_{N}^{\prime}\big)^{p}=\BigO\big((t^{2}/N)^{p/2}\big)
. Together with (
B.12
), step
1
and step
2
this completes the proof.
∎

Appendix C
Multi-overlap concentration

In this appendix we prove a finitary version of the main result in
[
14
]
regarding the concentration of the multi-overlaps (
2.11
). Instead of focusing on the stochastic block model (
1.19
) or (
1.34
), we will work in the general setting of optimal Bayesian inference; this presents no additional difficulty, and we suspect that our restatement of the multi-overlap concentration result in
[
14
]
will be useful for the analysis of other statistical inference models.

Let us describe a general optimal Bayesian inference model following
[
14
]
. We consider a ground-truth signal
\sigma^{*}\in\Sigma_{N}=\{-1,+1\}^{N}
with independent coordinates generated from a prior distribution
P^{*}
,

\sigma^{*}\sim P^{*}=\prod_{i\leq N}P_{i}^{*}.

(C.1)

We suppose that the data
\mathcal{D}=\mathcal{D}(\sigma^{*})
is sampled conditionally on the unknown signal
\sigma^{*}
from a probability distribution
P_{\mathrm{out}}
,

\mathcal{D}\sim P_{\text{out}}\{\cdot|\sigma^{*}\}.

(C.2)

The inference task is to recover the signal
\sigma^{*}
as accurately as possible given the data
\mathcal{D}
under the assumption that the likelihood
P_{\text{out}}
and the prior
P^{*}
are known to the statistician. In this setting the posterior of the model can be written explicitly. Indeed, if

H_{N}(\sigma)=\log P_{\text{out}}\big\{\mathcal{D}|\sigma^{*}=\sigma\big\}

(C.3)

denotes the Hamiltonian or log-likelihood of the model, then the Gibbs measure or posterior distribution of the model is given by Bayes’ formula,

G_{N}(\,\mathrm{d}\sigma)=\mathbb{P}\{\sigma^{*}\in\,\mathrm{d}\sigma|\mathcal{D}\}=\frac{\exp H_{N}(\sigma)P^{*}(\,\mathrm{d}\sigma)}{\int\exp H_{N}(\tau)P^{*}(\,\mathrm{d}\tau)}.

(C.4)

In addition to working in the context of optimal Bayesian inference, we will assume that the Hamiltonian (
C.3
) satisfies symmetry between sites. This means that for any permutation
\rho
of the spin indices,

\mathbb{P}\{\sigma^{*}\in\,\mathrm{d}\sigma|\mathcal{D}\}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{=}}\mathbb{P}\{\rho(\sigma^{*})\in\,\mathrm{d}\sigma|\mathcal{D}\}.

(C.5)

Notice that both the stochastic block model (
1.19
) and its enriched version (
1.34
) fall into the setting we have just described. For instance, in the enriched stochastic block model we have
P_{i}^{*}=\Ber(p)
and
\smash{\mathcal{D}=\widetilde{\mathcal{D}}^{t,\mu[-1,1],\overline{\mu}}}
, where the data
\smash{\widetilde{\mathcal{D}}^{t,s,\mu}}
was defined in (
1.30
).

The concentration of the multi-overlaps (
2.11
) associated with the Hamiltonian (
C.3
) will be enforced through a small perturbation which will not affect the limit of the associated free energy (
1.20
). Recall the choice of the sequences
(\epsilon_{N})
and
(s_{N})
satisfying (
4.3
) and (
4.4
), respectively. Fix an integer
K_{+}\geq 1
, and for each perturbation parameter
\lambda\in\mathbb{R}^{1+K_{+}}
with
\lambda_{k}\in[2^{-k-1},2^{-k}]
for
0\leq k\leq K_{+}
recall the definition of the perturbation Hamiltonians (
4.1
) and (
4.5
). Introduce the perturbed Hamiltonian

H_{N}(\sigma,\lambda)=H_{N}(\sigma)+H_{N}^{\mathrm{gauss}}(\sigma,\lambda_{0})+H_{N}^{\mathrm{exp}}(\sigma,\lambda),

(C.6)

where the randomness of each Hamiltonian is independent of the randomness of the other Hamiltonians. The multi-overlaps associated with this perturbed Hamiltonian are defined as in (
2.11
),

R_{\ell_{1},\ldots,\ell_{n}}=\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i\leq N}\sigma_{i}^{\ell_{1}}\cdots\sigma_{i}^{\ell_{n}},

(C.7)

where
(\sigma^{\ell})
denotes a sequence of i.i.d. replicas sampled from the Gibbs measure
\langle\cdot\rangle
associated with the perturbed Hamiltonian (
C.6
). It is actually these multi-overlaps that will be shown to concentrate. The proof of
Lemma

4.1
shows that the free energy functionals associated with the Hamiltonians (
C.3
) and (
C.6
) are asymptotically equivalent; for our purposes these two Hamiltonians can therefore be thought to describe the same model.

To establish the concentration of the multi-overlaps (
C.7
) we will closely follow the arguments in
[
14
]
. The authors in
[
14
]
obtain the concentration of the multi-overlaps (
C.7
) for some perturbation parameter
\lambda
by showing that it holds on average over the set of admissible perturbation parameters. In the proof of
Theorem

1.1
we will need to be able to obtain multi-overlap concentration for a specific perturbation parameter. Following the strategy in
[
14
]
, we will propose a verifiable condition on a perturbation parameter
\lambda
which ensures the concentration of its associated multi-overlaps. To be more precise, we will obtain the concentration of the multi-overlaps (
C.7
) up to a small error for any sequence of perturbation parameters
(\lambda^{N})
with

\lim_{N\to\infty}\E\big\langle(\mathcal{L}_{k}-\E\langle\mathcal{L}_{k}\rangle)^{2}\big\rangle=0.

(C.8)

The quantities
\mathcal{L}_{k}
are defined in (
4.26
) and (
4.27
) for
0\leq k\leq K_{+}
, and, through a slight abuse of notation, we have written
\smash{\langle\cdot\rangle}
for the Gibbs average with respect to the perturbed Hamiltonian (
C.6
) associated with the perturbation parameters
\smash{(\lambda^{N})}
. If necessary, we will write
\langle\cdot\rangle_{N}
to emphasize the dependence of this Gibbs measure on
N
.

We begin by showing that (
C.8
) implies the concentration of the magnetization
R_{1}
and of the overlap
R_{1,2}
; the former will be immediate from the Nishimori identity (
2.3
) while the latter will follow from a standard application of the Gaussian integration by parts formula (see Lemma 1.4 in
[
60
]
).

Lemma C.1
.

For any integer
N\geq 1
,

\E\big\langle(R_{1}-\E\langle R_{1}\rangle)^{2}\big\rangle\leq\frac{1}{N}.

(C.9)

Proof.

Applying the Nishimori identity reveals that

\E\big\langle(R_{1}-\E\langle R_{1}\rangle)^{2}\big\rangle=\frac{1}{N^{2}}\sum_{i\leq N}\E\big(\sigma_{i}^{*}-\E\sigma_{i}^{*}\big)^{2}\leq\frac{1}{N}.

This completes the proof.
∎

Lemma C.2
.

For any integer
N\geq 1
,

\E\big\langle(R_{1,2}-\E\langle R_{1,2}\rangle)^{2}\big\rangle\leq 4\E\big\langle(\mathcal{L}_{0}-\E\langle\mathcal{L}_{0}\rangle)^{2}\big\rangle.

(C.10)

Proof.

The proof is taken from the Appendix of
[
14
]
, and it consists in testing the concentration of the overlap
\smash{R_{1,*}=\frac{\sigma\cdot\sigma^{*}}{N}}
against the Hamiltonian
\mathcal{L}_{0}
by means of the Gaussian integration by parts formula. Using (
4.26
) shows that

\displaystyle\E\big\langle(R_{1,*}-\E\langle R_{1,*}\rangle)(\mathcal{L}_{0}-\E\langle\mathcal{L}_{0}\rangle)\big\rangle=\E\big\langle R_{1,*}(

\displaystyle R_{1,*}-\E\langle R_{1,*}\rangle)\big\rangle

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{2N\sqrt{\lambda_{0,N}}}\E\big\langle R_{1,*}(\sigma\cdot Z_{0}-\E\langle\sigma\cdot Z_{0}\rangle)\big\rangle.

(C.11)

The Gaussian integration by parts formula and the Nishimori identity imply that

\E\langle\sigma\cdot Z\rangle=N\sqrt{\lambda_{0,N}}\big(1-\E\langle R_{1,*}\rangle\big)\quad\text{and}\quad\E\big\langle R_{1,*}\sigma\cdot Z\big\rangle=N\sqrt{\lambda_{0,N}}\big(\E\langle R_{1,*}\rangle-\E\langle R_{1,*}\rangle^{2}\big).

Substituting these two equalities into (
C
) reveals that

\E\big\langle(R_{1,*}-\E\langle R_{1,*}\rangle)(\mathcal{L}_{0}-\E\langle\mathcal{L}_{0}\rangle)\big\rangle=\frac{1}{2}\E\big\langle(R_{1,*}-\langle R_{1,*}\rangle)^{2}\big\rangle+\frac{1}{2}\E\big\langle(R_{1,*}-\E\langle R_{1,*}\rangle)^{2}\big\rangle.

(There seems to be a sign error in equation (5.2) of
[
14
]
.)
It follows by the Nishimori identity that

\E\big\langle(R_{1,*}-\E\langle R_{1,*}\rangle)(\mathcal{L}_{0}-\E\langle\mathcal{L}_{0}\rangle)\big\rangle\geq\frac{1}{2}\E\big\langle(R_{1,2}-\E\langle R_{1,2}\rangle)^{2}\big\rangle.

Invoking the Cauchy-Schwarz inequality and the Nishimori identity completes the proof.
∎

The concentration of the multi-overlaps (
C.7
) is considerably more complicated to obtain, and follows from the Franz-de Sanctis identities described in
[
14
]
. The first section of this appendix will be devoted to establishing this implication. In the second section we will prove a finitary version of the multi-overlap concentration result in
[
14
]
which will be uniform over an appropriate class of random probability measures. This uniformity plays its part in the proof
Lemma

4.2
.

C.1
Franz-de Sanctis identities

The Franz-de Sanctis identities may be thought of as the Ghirlanda-Guerra identities of optimal Bayesian inference. A random probability measure which satisfies the Ghirlanda-Guerra identities must have an ultrametric support; a deep insight which leads to the appearance of the intricate Poisson-Dirichlet probability cascades in many spin glass models
[
60
]
. The Franz-de Sanctis identities enforce a much simpler and more rigid structure on a random probability measure which we will describe in due course. To state these identities, it will be convenient to fix a uniform index
i\in\{1,\ldots,N\}
and an exponential random variable
e\sim\Exp(1)
independent of all other sources of randomness, and introduce the random variables

y_{ik}=\frac{e}{1+\lambda_{k}\sigma_{i}^{*}},\quad\theta_{ik}^{\ell}=\log(1+\lambda_{k}\sigma_{i}^{\ell})-\lambda_{k}y_{ik}\sigma_{i}^{\ell},\quad\text{and}\quad d_{ik}^{\ell}=\frac{y_{ik}\sigma_{i}^{\ell}}{1+\lambda_{k}\sigma_{i}^{*}}

(C.12)

for
1\leq k\leq K_{+}
.

Proposition C.3

(Franz-de Sanctis identities in inference)
.

For any
1\leq k\leq K_{+}
and any function
f_{n}
of finitely many spins on
n
replicas and of the signal
\sigma^{*}
with
\lVert f_{n}\rVert_{L^{\infty}}\leq 1
,

\bigg\lvert\E\frac{\langle f_{n}d_{ik}^{1}\exp\big(\sum_{\ell\leq n}\theta_{ik}^{\ell}\big)\rangle}{\langle\exp(\theta_{ik})}\rangle^{n}-\E\langle f_{n}\rangle\E\frac{\langle d_{ik}\exp(\theta_{ik})\rangle}{\langle\exp(\theta_{ik})\rangle}\bigg\rvert\leq\bigg(2\E\big\langle(\mathcal{L}_{k}-\E\langle\mathcal{L}_{k}\rangle)^{2}\big\rangle+\frac{16}{s_{N}}\bigg)^{1/2}.

(C.13)

The Franz-de Sanctis identities are proved in a similar fashion to the Ghirlanda-Guerra identities by testing the concentration of the quantities

\widetilde{\mathcal{L}}_{k}=\frac{1}{s_{N}}\sum_{j\leq\pi_{k}}\frac{\sigma_{i_{jk}}e_{jk}}{(1+\lambda_{k}\sigma^{*}_{i_{jk}})^{2}}

(C.14)

defined for
1\leq k\leq K_{+}
against an arbitrary function of finitely many spins and of the signal
\sigma^{*}
. Notice that
\smash{\widetilde{\mathcal{L}}_{k}}
is none other than the second term in the sum defining each
\mathcal{L}_{k}
in (
4.27
). The reason for focusing only on the second term is that the first term concentrates automatically by the Nishimori identity. This is the content of Proposition 3.4 in
[
14
]
which we reproduce here for completeness. We present a slightly simpler proof than that in
[
14
]
which was kindly shared with us by Dmitry Panchenko.

Lemma C.4
.

For any
1\leq k\leq K_{+}
and every large enough
N\geq 1
,

\E\Big\langle\big(\widetilde{\mathcal{L}}_{k}-\E\langle\widetilde{\mathcal{L}}_{k}\rangle\big)^{2}\Big\rangle\leq 2\E\big(\langle\mathcal{L}_{k}-\E\langle\mathcal{L}_{k}\rangle)^{2}\big\rangle+\frac{16}{s_{N}}.

(C.15)

Proof.

Introduce the quantity

g(\sigma,\pi_{k})=\sum_{j\leq\pi_{k}}\frac{\sigma_{i_{jk}}}{1+\lambda_{k}\sigma_{i_{jk}}}

in such a way that
\smash{\widetilde{\mathcal{L}}_{k}=s_{N}^{-1}g(\sigma,\pi_{k})}-\mathcal{L}_{k}
. Write
\Var
for the variance with respect to the measure
\E\langle\cdot\rangle
. Since the variance of a sum of two random variables is bounded by twice the sum of the variance of each of the random variables,

\Var\big(\widetilde{\mathcal{L}}_{k}\big)\leq 2\Big(\Var(\mathcal{L}_{k})+\frac{1}{s_{N}^{2}}\Var(g)\Big).

(C.16)

By the Nishimori identity and a direct computation,

\Var\big(g(\sigma,\pi_{k})\big)=\E\bigg(\sum_{j\leq\pi_{k}}\frac{\sigma^{*}_{i_{jk}}}{1+\lambda_{k}\sigma^{*}_{i_{jk}}}\bigg)^{2}-\bigg(\E\sum_{j\leq\pi_{k}}\frac{\sigma^{*}_{i_{jk}}}{1+\lambda_{k}\sigma^{*}_{i_{jk}}}\bigg)^{2}.

(C.17)

Recalling that the coordinates of the signal
\sigma^{*}
are i.i.d. and averaging with respect to the randomness of the indices
(i_{jk})
reveals that

\displaystyle\E\bigg(\sum_{j\leq\pi_{k}}\frac{\sigma^{*}_{i_{jk}}}{1+\lambda_{k}\sigma^{*}_{i_{jk}}}\bigg)^{2}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{N}\E\sum_{j,j^{\prime}\leq\pi_{k}}\frac{1}{(1+\lambda_{k}\sigma_{1}^{*})^{2}}+\frac{N^{2}-N}{N}\E\sum_{j,j^{\prime}\leq\pi_{k}}\frac{\sigma_{1}^{*}\sigma_{2}^{*}}{(1+\lambda_{k}\sigma_{1}^{*})(1+\lambda_{k}\sigma_{2}^{*})}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{4\E\pi_{k}^{2}}{N}+\E\pi_{k}^{2}\Big(\E\frac{\sigma_{1}^{*}}{1+\lambda_{k}\sigma_{1}^{*}}\Big)^{2},

where we have used that
\sigma_{1}^{2}=1
and
\lambda_{k}\leq 1/2
. Similarly,

\bigg(\E\sum_{j\leq\pi_{k}}\frac{\sigma^{*}_{i_{jk}}}{1+\lambda_{k}\sigma^{*}_{i_{jk}}}\bigg)^{2}=\big(\E\pi_{k}\big)^{2}\Big(\E\frac{\sigma_{1}^{*}}{1+\lambda_{k}\sigma_{1}^{*}}\Big)^{2}.

Substituting these two bounds into (
C.17
), recalling (
4.4
) and choosing
N
large enough yields

\Var\big(g(\sigma,\pi_{k})\big)\leq\frac{4\E\pi_{k}^{2}}{N}+\Var(\pi_{k})\Big(\E\frac{\sigma_{1}^{*}}{1+\lambda_{k}\sigma_{1}^{*}}\Big)^{2}\leq 8s_{N}.

Plugging this into (
C.16
) completes the proof.
∎

Proof of
Proposition

C.3
.

We follow the proof of Theorem 3.3 in
[
14
]
; we will not give full details, and instead encourage the interested reader to consult
[
14
]
. The Cauchy-Schwarz inequality and the fact that
\lVert f_{n}\rVert_{L^{\infty}}\leq 1
imply that

\displaystyle\big\lvert\E\big\langle f_{n}\widetilde{\mathcal{L}}_{k}(\sigma^{1})\big\rangle-\E\langle f_{n}\rangle\E\big\langle\widetilde{\mathcal{L}}_{k}(\sigma)\big\rangle\big\rvert

\displaystyle\leq\E\big\langle(f_{n}-\langle f_{n}\rangle)^{2}\big\rangle^{1/2}\E\Big\langle\big(\widetilde{\mathcal{L}}_{k}-\E\langle\widetilde{\mathcal{L}}_{k}\rangle\big)^{2}\Big\rangle^{1/2}

\displaystyle\leq\E\Big\langle\big(\widetilde{\mathcal{L}}_{k}-\E\langle\widetilde{\mathcal{L}}_{k}\rangle\big)^{2}\Big\rangle^{1/2}.

By
Lemma

C.4
it is therefore sufficient to prove that

\E\big\langle f_{n}\widetilde{\mathcal{L}}_{k}(\sigma^{1})\big\rangle=\E\frac{\langle f_{n}d_{ik}^{1}\exp\big(\sum_{\ell\leq n}\theta_{ik}^{\ell}\big)\rangle}{\langle\exp(\theta_{ik})\rangle^{n}}\quad\text{and}\quad\E\big\langle\widetilde{\mathcal{L}}_{k}(\sigma)\big\rangle=\E\frac{\langle d_{ik}\exp(\theta_{ik})\rangle}{\langle\exp(\theta_{ik})\rangle}.

(C.18)

Since
\pi_{k}
is independent of all other sources of randomness, taking the expectation with respect to this random variable first shows that

\E\big\langle f_{n}\widetilde{\mathcal{L}}_{k}(\sigma^{1})\big\rangle=\sum_{r\geq 1}\frac{s_{N}^{r-1}}{(r-1)!}\exp(-s_{N})\E\big\langle f_{n}D_{1k}^{1}\big\rangle_{\pi_{k}=r},

(C.19)

where
D_{1k}^{1}=\sigma_{i_{1k}}^{1}e_{1k}/(1+\lambda_{k}\sigma^{*}_{i_{1k}})^{2}
.
To simplify this expression, we will isolate the first replica
\sigma^{1}
appearing in each of the averages. It will be convenient to introduce the quantities

\Theta_{jk}^{\ell}=\log(1+\lambda_{k}\sigma_{i_{jk}}^{\ell})-\frac{\lambda_{k}e_{jk}\sigma_{i_{jk}}^{\ell}}{1+\lambda_{k}\sigma^{*}_{i_{jk}}}\quad\text{and}\quad\mathcal{H}_{k}^{r-1}(\sigma^{\ell})=\sum_{2\leq j\leq r}\Theta_{jk}^{\ell}

as well as the partially perturbed Hamiltonian

H_{N}^{\prime}(\sigma)=H_{N}(\sigma)+H_{N}^{\mathrm{gauss}}(\sigma)+\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}1\leq k^{\prime}\leq K_{+}\\
k^{\prime}\neq k\end{subarray}}\mathcal{H}_{k^{\prime}},

(C.20)

where
\mathcal{H}_{k}
is defined in (
4.5
). Denoting by
\langle\cdot\rangle_{\pi_{k}=r}^{\prime}
the Gibbs measure corresponding to the Hamiltonian
H_{N}^{\prime}(\sigma)+\mathcal{H}_{k}^{r-1}(\sigma^{\ell})
, we have

\E\big\langle f_{n}D_{1k}^{1}\big\rangle_{\pi_{k}=r}=\E\E_{i_{1k}}\E_{e_{1k}}\frac{\langle f_{n}D_{1k}^{1}\exp\big(\sum_{\ell\leq n}\Theta^{\ell}_{1k}\big)\rangle^{\prime}_{\pi_{k}=r}}{(\langle\exp(\Theta_{1k})\rangle^{\prime}_{\pi_{k}=r})^{n}}

(C.21)

for each
r\geq 1
.
Since the uniform random variable
i_{1k}
and the exponential random variable
e_{1k}
no longer appear in the Gibbs average
\langle\cdot\rangle_{\pi_{k}=r}^{\prime}
, we may replace them by a uniform random variable
i\in\{1,\ldots,N\}
and an exponential random variable
e\sim\Exp(1)
independent of all other sources of randomness as in the statement of the theorem. To emphasize this change, we also replace
D_{1k}^{1}
and
\Theta_{1k}^{\ell}
by
d_{ik}^{1}=\sigma_{i}^{1}e/(1+\lambda_{k}\sigma_{i}^{*})^{2}
and
\theta_{ik}^{\ell}=\log(1+\lambda_{k}\sigma_{i}^{\ell})-\lambda_{k}\sigma_{i}^{\ell}e/(1+\lambda_{k}\sigma_{i}^{*})
, respectively. Notice that this matches the definitions in (
C.12
). In this new notation (
C.21
) reads

\E\big\langle f_{n}D_{1k}^{1}\big\rangle_{\pi_{k}=r}=\E\frac{\langle f_{n}d_{ik}^{1}\exp\big(\sum_{\ell\leq n}\theta_{ik}^{\ell}\big)\rangle^{\prime}_{\pi_{k}=r}}{(\langle\exp(\theta_{ik})\rangle^{\prime}_{\pi_{k}=r})^{n}}.

Substituting this into (
C.19
) and making the change of variables
m=r-1
reveals that

\E\big\langle f_{n}\widetilde{\mathcal{L}}_{k}(\sigma^{1})\big\rangle=\sum_{m\geq 0}\frac{s_{N}^{m}}{m!}\exp(-s_{N})\E\frac{\langle f_{n}d_{ik}^{1}\exp\big(\sum_{\ell\leq n}\theta_{ik}^{\ell}\big)\rangle^{\prime}_{\pi_{k}=m+1}}{(\langle\exp(\theta_{ik})\rangle^{\prime}_{\pi_{k}=m+1})^{n}}.

Notice that whenever
\pi_{k}=m+1
, the Hamiltonian defining the Gibbs average
\smash{\langle\cdot\rangle^{\prime}_{\pi_{k}=m+1}}
is given by
\smash{H_{N}^{\prime}(\sigma)+\mathcal{H}_{k}^{m}(\sigma)}
. This Hamiltonian has the same distribution as the Hamiltonian in (
B.1
) defining the original Gibbs average
\langle\cdot\rangle_{\pi_{k}=m}
. It follows that

\E\big\langle f_{n}\widetilde{\mathcal{L}}_{k}(\sigma^{1})\big\rangle=\sum_{m\geq 0}\frac{s_{N}^{m}}{m!}\exp(-s_{N})\E\frac{\langle f_{n}d_{ik}^{1}\exp\big(\sum_{\ell\leq n}\theta_{ik}^{\ell}\big)\rangle_{\pi_{k}=m}}{(\langle\exp(\theta_{ik})\rangle_{\pi_{k}=m})^{n}}=\E\frac{\langle f_{n}d_{ik}^{1}\exp\big(\sum_{\ell\leq n}\theta_{ik}^{\ell}\big)\rangle}{\langle\exp(\theta_{ik})\rangle^{n}}.

This is the first equality in (
C.18
). The second equality in (
C.18
) is obtained by taking
n=1
and
f_{1}=1
in the first equality. This completes the proof.
∎

Applying this result along a sequence of perturbation parameters
\smash{(\lambda^{N})}
satisfying (
C.8
) reveals that

\lim_{N\to\infty}\Big\lvert\E\frac{\langle f_{n}d_{ik}^{1}\exp\big(\sum_{\ell\leq n}\theta_{ik}^{\ell}\big)\rangle_{N}}{\langle\exp(\theta_{ik})\rangle_{N}^{n}}-\E\langle f_{n}\rangle_{N}\E\frac{\langle d_{ik}\exp(\theta_{ik})\rangle_{N}}{\langle\exp(\theta_{ik})\rangle_{N}}\Big\rvert=0

(C.22)

for any
1\leq k\leq K_{+}
and any function
f_{n}
of finitely many spins on
n
replicas and of the signal
\sigma^{*}
with
\lVert f_{n}\rVert_{L^{\infty}}\leq 1
. Observing that the denominators
\langle\exp(\theta_{ik})\rangle_{N}
do not depend on the signal
\sigma^{*}
, it is possible to use the Nishimori identity (
2.3
) to replace all occurrences of the signal
\sigma^{*}
in (
C.22
) by another replica. For convenience of notation we will denote this new replica by
\sigma^{\diamond}
to distinguish it from the signal
\sigma^{*}
and at the same time not occupy any specific index. The equations in (
C.22
) now read

\lim_{N\to\infty}\Big\lvert\E\E_{\diamond}\frac{\langle f_{n}d_{ik}^{1}\exp\big(\sum_{\ell\leq n}\theta_{ik}^{\ell}\big)\rangle_{N}}{\langle\exp(\theta_{ik})\rangle_{N}^{n}}-\E\E_{\diamond}\langle f_{n}\rangle_{N}\E\E_{\diamond}\frac{\langle d_{ik}\exp(\theta_{ik})\rangle_{N}}{\langle\exp(\theta_{ik})\rangle_{N}}\Big\rvert=0,

(C.23)

where
\E_{\diamond}
denotes the Gibbs average with respect to the replica
\sigma^{\diamond}
only, the bracket
\langle\cdot\rangle_{N}
denotes the Gibbs average with respect to all other standard replicas, the function
f_{n}
depends on finitely many spins on the
n
standard replicas and on
\sigma^{\diamond}
, and, with some abuse of notation,

y_{ik}=\frac{e}{1+\lambda_{k}\sigma_{i}^{\diamond}},\quad\theta_{ik}^{\ell}=\log(1+\lambda_{k}\sigma_{i}^{\ell})-\lambda_{k}y_{ik}\sigma_{i}^{\ell},\quad\text{and}\quad d_{ik}^{\ell}=\frac{y_{ik}\sigma_{i}^{\ell}}{1+\lambda_{k}\sigma_{i}^{\diamond}}

(C.24)

with
\lambda_{k}=\lambda_{k}^{N}
for
1\leq k\leq K_{+}
. We now simplify (
C.23
) for functions
f_{n}
that do not depend on the spin coordinate indexed by
1
. Introduce the collection of functions

\displaystyle\mathsf{F}_{n}=\big\{\text{f}

\displaystyle\text{unctions }f_{n}\text{ of }\text{ finitely many spins }\sigma_{i}^{\ell},\sigma_{i}^{\diamond}\text{ with }2\leq i\leq N

\displaystyle\text{ of the }n\text{ standard replicas }(\sigma^{\ell})_{\ell\leq n}\text{ and the special replica }\sigma^{\diamond}\text{ with }\lVert f_{n}\rVert_{L^{\infty}}\leq 1\big\}

(C.25)

and the quantities

y_{k}=\frac{e}{1+\lambda_{k}\sigma_{1}^{\diamond}},\quad\theta_{k}^{\ell}=\log(1+\lambda_{k}\sigma_{1}^{\ell})-\lambda_{k}y_{k}\sigma_{1}^{\ell},\quad\text{and}\quad d_{k}^{\ell}=\frac{y_{k}\sigma_{1}^{\ell}}{1+\lambda_{k}\sigma_{1}^{\diamond}}.

(C.26)

For functions
f_{n}\in\mathsf{F}_{n}
, the symmetry between sites (
C.5
) and the fact that
i\in\{2,\ldots,N\}
with overwhelming probability in the limit, allow us to replace the uniform random index
i\in\{1,\ldots,N\}
by the index
1
. The Franz de-Sanctis identities together with assumption (
C.8
) therefore have the following important implication.

Corollary C.5

(Asymptotic Franz-de Sanctis identities in inference)
.

If (
C.8
) holds, then for every
1\leq k\leq K_{+}
and all functions
f_{n}\in\mathsf{F}_{n}
,

\lim_{N\to\infty}\Big\lvert\E\E_{\diamond}\frac{\langle f_{n}d_{k}^{1}\exp\big(\sum_{\ell\leq n}\theta_{k}^{\ell}\big)\rangle_{N}}{\langle\exp(\theta_{k})\rangle_{N}^{n}}-\E\E_{\diamond}\langle f_{n}\rangle_{N}\E\E_{\diamond}\frac{\langle d_{k}\exp(\theta_{k})\rangle_{N}}{\langle\exp(\theta_{k})\rangle_{N}}\Big\rvert=0.

(C.27)

C.2
Finitary multi-overlap concentration

The finitary version of the multi-overlap concentration result in
[
14
]
will be uniform over an appropriate class of random probability measures which we now describe. For each integer
N\geq 1
consider the set of random probability measures on
\Sigma_{N}
thought of as a subset of
\{-1,0,1\}^{\mathbb{N}}
,

\mathcal{G}_{N}=\Big\{G\mid G\text{ is a random probability measure on }\Sigma_{N}\times\{0\}^{\mathbb{N}}\Big\},

(C.28)

and say that a measure
G\in\mathcal{G}_{N}
satisfies symmetry between sites if, for any sequence of i.i.d. replicas
(\sigma^{\ell})_{\ell\geq 1}
sampled from
G
,

\big(\sigma_{i}^{\ell})_{i,\ell\geq 1}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{=}}\big(\sigma_{\rho_{1}(i)}^{\rho_{2}(\ell)}\big)_{i,\ell\geq 1}

(C.29)

for any permutation
\rho_{1}
on the finite set
\{1,\ldots,N\}
and any permutation
\rho_{2}
of finitely many indices. Denote by
\mathcal{G}_{N}^{s}
the subset of
\mathcal{G}_{N}
which satisfies symmetry between sites. Notice that each Gibbs measure
G_{N}
defined by (
C.4
) can be thought of as an element of
\mathcal{G}_{N}
by setting
\sigma_{i}=0
when
i>N
for any replica
\sigma\in\Sigma_{N}
sampled from
G_{N}
. In this way, the symmetry between sites in (
C.29
) and (
C.5
) coincide, so in fact
G_{N}\in\mathcal{G}_{N}^{s}
. This identification also suggests that the appropriate notion of the multi-overlap (
C.7
) for a random probability measure
G\in\mathcal{G}_{N}
should be

R_{\ell_{1},\ldots,\ell_{n}}=\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i\leq N}\sigma_{i}^{\ell_{1}}\cdots\sigma_{i}^{\ell_{n}},

(C.30)

where
(\sigma^{\ell})
denotes a sequence of i.i.d. replicas sampled from the Gibbs measure
G
. Denoting by
\langle\cdot\rangle_{G}
the average with respect to the random probability measure
G
we may now sate the main result of this appendix.

Proposition C.6
.

For every
\epsilon>0
there exists
\delta>0
such that the following holds. Let
N\geq\lfloor\delta^{-1}\rfloor
and
G\in\mathcal{G}_{N}
be a random probability measure such that for all
1\leq k\leq K_{+}=\lfloor\delta^{-1}\rfloor
and any function
f_{n}\in\mathsf{F}_{n}
,

\displaystyle\E\big\langle(R_{1}-

\displaystyle\E\langle R_{1}\rangle_{G})^{2}\big\rangle_{G}\leq\delta,\qquad\qquad\E\big\langle(R_{1,2}-\E\langle R_{1,2}\rangle_{G})^{2}\big\rangle_{G}\leq\delta,

(C.31)

\displaystyle\Big\lvert\E\E_{\diamond}\frac{\langle f_{n}d_{k}^{1}\exp\big(\sum_{\ell\leq n}\theta_{k}^{\ell}\big)\rangle_{G}}{\langle\exp(\theta_{k})\rangle^{n}_{G}}-\E\E_{\diamond}\langle f_{n}\rangle_{G}\E\E_{\diamond}\frac{\langle d_{k}\exp(\theta_{k})\rangle_{G}}{\langle\exp(\theta_{k})\rangle_{G}}\Big\rvert\leq\delta.

(C.32)

Then for any
1\leq m\leq\lfloor\epsilon^{-1}\rfloor
, we have

\E\big\langle(R_{1,\ldots,m}-\E\langle R_{1,\ldots,m}\rangle_{G})^{2}\big\rangle_{G}\leq\epsilon.

(C.33)

The proof proceeds by contradiction and closely follows Section 3.5 and Section 3.7 of
[
14
]
. Suppose there exists some
\epsilon>0
such that no matter the choice of
\delta>0
, it is always possible to find some integer
N=N(\delta)\geq\lfloor\delta^{-1}\rfloor
and some random probability measure
G=G(\delta)\in\mathcal{G}_{N}
with

\displaystyle\E\big\langle(R_{1}-

\displaystyle\E\langle R_{1}\rangle_{G})^{2}\big\rangle_{G}\leq\delta,\qquad\qquad\E\big\langle(R_{1,2}-\E\langle R_{1,2}\rangle_{G})^{2}\big\rangle_{G}\leq\delta,

(C.34)

\displaystyle\Big\lvert\E\E_{\diamond}\frac{\langle f_{n}d_{k}^{1}\exp\big(\sum_{\ell\leq n}\theta_{k}^{\ell}\big)\rangle_{G}}{\langle\exp(\theta_{k})\rangle^{n}_{G}}-\E\E_{\diamond}\langle f_{n}\rangle_{G}\E\E_{\diamond}\frac{\langle d_{k}\exp(\theta_{k})\rangle_{G}}{\langle\exp(\theta_{k})\rangle_{G}}\Big\rvert\leq\delta

(C.35)

for any
1\leq k\leq K_{+}=\lfloor\delta^{-1}\rfloor
and any
f_{n}\in\mathsf{F}_{n}
for which there exists some
1\leq m=m(\delta)\leq\lfloor\epsilon^{-1}\rfloor
with

\E\big\langle(R_{1,\ldots,m}-\E\langle R_{1,\ldots,m}\rangle_{G})^{2}\big\rangle_{G}>\epsilon.

(C.36)

Applying the Prokhorov theorem on the compact metric space
\{-1,0,+1\}^{\mathbb{N}^{2}}
and noticing that there are only finitely many choices for
m=m(\delta)
, it is possible to find a subsequence with
\delta\to 0
along which the distribution of the array
\smash{\big(\sigma_{i}^{\ell}\big)_{i,\ell\geq 1}}
under the averaged Gibbs measure
\smash{\E\langle\cdot\rangle_{G(\delta)}}
converges in the sense of finite dimensional distributions and along which (
C.34
), (
C.35
) and (
C.36
) hold for every
k\geq 1
and a fixed
1\leq m\leq\lfloor\epsilon^{-1}\rfloor
. Since
\smash{N(\delta)\to\infty}
and
\smash{G(\delta)\in\mathcal{G}_{N}}
, in the limit, the distribution of spins will be a measure on
\{-1,+1\}^{\mathbb{N}^{2}}
which will inherit the symmetry between sites (
C.29
). By the Aldous-Hoover representation (see Theorem 1.4 in
[
60
]
), this symmetry implies the existence of some function
\sigma:[0,1]^{4}\to\{-1,+1\}
with

\big(\sigma_{i}^{\ell})_{i,\ell\geq 1}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{=}}\big(\sigma(w,u_{\ell},v_{i},x_{i,\ell})\big)_{i,\ell\geq 1},

(C.37)

where
w
,
(u_{\ell})
,
(v_{i})
and
(x_{i,\ell})
are i.i.d. uniform random variables on
[0,1]
. Since
\sigma
takes values in
\{-1,+1\}
, the distribution of the array
(\sigma_{i}^{\ell})
is encoded by the function

\overline{\sigma}(w,u,v)=\E_{x_{i,\ell}}\sigma(w,u,v,x_{i,\ell})=\int_{0}^{1}\sigma(w,u,v,x)\,\mathrm{d}x.

(C.38)

Indeed, the last coordinate
x_{i,\ell}
is a dummy variable that
corresponds to flipping a biased coin to generate a Bernoulli random variable with expected value
\overline{\sigma}(w,u,v)
. To clarify this further, let
\,\mathrm{d}u
and
\,\mathrm{d}v
denote Lebesgue measure on
[0,1]
, and define the random probability measure

G=G_{w}=\,\mathrm{d}u\circ\big(u\mapsto\overline{\sigma}(w,u,\cdot)\big)^{-1}

(C.39)

on the space of functions of
v\in[0,1]
,

H=L^{2}\big([0,1],\,\mathrm{d}v\big)\cap\big\{\lVert\overline{\sigma}\rVert_{L^{\infty}}\leq 1\big\},

(C.40)

equipped with the topology of
L^{2}([0,1],\,\mathrm{d}v)
. As described in Section 2 of
[
64
]
, the whole process of generating spins can be broken into the following steps:

(i)

generate the asymptotic Gibbs measure
G=G_{w}
using the uniform random variable
w
;

(ii)

consider an i.i.d. sequence
\overline{\sigma}^{\ell}=\overline{\sigma}(w,u_{\ell},\cdot)
of replicas from
G
, which are functions in
H
;

(iii)

plug in i.i.d. uniform random variables
(v_{i})_{i\geq 1}
to obtain the array
\overline{\sigma}^{\ell}(v_{i})=\overline{\sigma}(w,u_{\ell},v_{i})
;

(iv)

finally, use the random variables
(x_{i,\ell})
to generate
(\sigma_{i}^{\ell})
by flipping a coin with expected value
\overline{\sigma}^{\ell}(v_{i})
,

\sigma_{i}^{\ell}=2\mathbbm{1}\Big\{x_{i,\ell}\leq\frac{1+\overline{\sigma}^{\ell}(v_{i})}{2}\Big\}-1.

(C.41)

This suggests that the asymptotic Gibbs average should be the average with respect to the random variables
(u_{\ell})
and
(x_{i,\ell})
that depend on the replica indices, which we will denote by

\langle\cdot\rangle=\E_{(u_{\ell}),(x_{i,\ell})}.

(C.42)

We can also expect the asymptotic multi-overlap to be given by

R^{\infty}_{\ell_{1},\ldots,\ell_{n}}(w,(u_{\ell_{j}})_{j\leq n})=\E_{v}\prod_{j\leq n}\overline{\sigma}(w,u_{\ell_{j}},v)=\int_{0}^{1}\prod_{j\leq n}\overline{\sigma}(w,u_{\ell_{j}},v)\,\mathrm{d}v.

(C.43)

This intuition is confirmed by the two following results adapted from Section 3.5 and the Appendix in
[
14
]
.

Lemma C.7
.

For any finite set of
n
replicas and every collection
\{\mathcal{C}_{\ell}\}_{\ell\leq n}
of finite indices,

\lim_{\delta\to 0}\E\prod_{\ell\leq n}\Big\langle\prod_{i\in\mathcal{C}_{\ell}}\sigma_{i}^{\ell}\Big\rangle_{G(\delta)}=\E_{w,(v_{i})}\prod_{\ell\leq n}\Big\langle\prod_{i\in\mathcal{C}_{\ell}}\sigma_{i}^{\ell}\Big\rangle.

(C.44)

Proof.

Let
\mathcal{C}=\{(i,\ell)\mid\ell\leq n\text{ and }i\in\mathcal{C}_{\ell}\}
. By definition of weak convergence in the sense of finite-dimensional marginal distributions,

\lim_{\delta\to 0}\E\prod_{\ell\leq n}\Big\langle\prod_{i\in\mathcal{C}_{\ell}}\sigma_{i}^{\ell}\Big\rangle_{G(\delta)}=\lim_{\delta\to 0}\E\Big\langle\prod_{(i,\ell)\in\mathcal{C}}\sigma_{i}^{\ell}\Big\rangle_{G(\delta)}=\E\prod_{(i,\ell)\in\mathcal{C}}\sigma(w,u_{\ell},v_{i},x_{i,\ell}).

Recalling the notation (
C.42
),

\E\prod_{(i,\ell)\in\mathcal{C}}\sigma(w,u_{\ell},v_{i},x_{i,\ell})=\E_{w,(v_{i})}\prod_{\ell\leq n}\E_{(u_{\ell})}\prod_{i\in\mathcal{C}_{\ell}}\E_{(x_{i,\ell})}\sigma(w,u_{\ell},v_{i},x_{i,\ell})=\E_{w,(v_{i})}\prod_{\ell\leq n}\Big\langle\prod_{i\in\mathcal{C}_{\ell}}\sigma_{i}^{\ell}\Big\rangle.

as required.
∎

Lemma C.8
.

For any collection of sets
\{\mathcal{L}_{i}\}_{i\geq 1}
only finitely many of which are non-empty,

\lim_{\delta\to 0}\E\Big\langle\prod_{i\geq 1}R_{\mathcal{L}_{i}}\Big\rangle_{G(\delta)}=\E_{w}\Big\langle\prod_{i\geq 1}R_{\mathcal{L}_{i}}^{\infty}\Big\rangle.

(C.45)

Proof.

Write
N\geq\lfloor\delta^{-1}\rfloor
for the unique integer with
G(\delta)\in\mathcal{G}_{N}
and suppose without loss of generality that the sets
\mathcal{L}_{i}
for
i\leq j
are non-empty while the sets
\mathcal{L}_{i}
for
i>j
are empty. From (
C.30
),

\E\Big\langle\prod_{i\geq 1}R_{\mathcal{L}_{i}}\Big\rangle_{G(\delta)}=\frac{1}{N^{j}}\sum_{i_{1},\ldots,i_{j}}\E\Big\langle\prod_{\ell_{1}\in\mathcal{L}_{1}}\cdots\prod_{\ell_{j}\in\mathcal{L}_{j}}\sigma_{i_{1}}^{\ell_{1}}\cdots\sigma_{i_{j}}^{\ell_{j}}\Big\rangle_{G(\delta)}.

The number of terms in this sum for which at least two of the indices
i_{1},\ldots,i_{j}
are equal is of order
N^{j-1}
and is therefore negligible in the limit. Moreover, the symmetry between sites (
C.29
) implies that whenever
i_{1},\ldots,i_{j}
are all distinct

\E\Big\langle\prod_{\ell_{1}\in\mathcal{L}_{1}}\cdots\prod_{\ell_{j}\in\mathcal{L}_{j}}\sigma_{i_{1}}^{\ell_{1}}\cdots\sigma_{i_{j}}^{\ell_{j}}\Big\rangle_{G(\delta)}=\E\Big\langle\prod_{\ell_{1}\in\mathcal{L}_{1}}\cdots\prod_{\ell_{j}\in\mathcal{L}_{j}}\sigma_{1}^{\ell_{1}}\cdots\sigma_{j}^{\ell_{j}}\Big\rangle_{G(\delta)}=\E\Big\langle\prod_{i\geq 1}\prod_{\ell\in\mathcal{L}_{i}}\sigma^{\ell}_{i}\Big\rangle_{G(\delta)}.

(This seems to fix a small typo in the second-to-last display of the Appendix in
[
14
]
). Combining these two observations shows that

\lim_{\delta\to 0}\E\Big\langle\prod_{i\geq 1}R_{\mathcal{L}_{i}}\Big\rangle_{G(\delta)}=\E_{w,(u_{\ell})}\prod_{i\geq 1}\E_{v_{i}}\prod_{\ell\in\mathcal{L}_{i}}\E_{x_{i,\ell}}\sigma(w,u_{\ell},v_{i},x_{i,\ell})=\E_{w,(u_{\ell})}\prod_{i\geq 1}R_{\mathcal{L}_{i}}^{\infty}.

This completes the proof.
∎

In the notation of (
C.42
) and (
C.43
), the asymptotic version of (
C.34
) and (
C.35
) therefore reads

\displaystyle\E\langle(R_{1}

{}^{\infty})^{2}\rangle=\big(\E\langle R_{1}^{\infty}\rangle\big)^{2},\qquad\E\langle(R_{1,2}^{\infty})^{2}\rangle=\big(\E\langle R_{1,2}^{\infty}\rangle\big)^{2}

(C.46)

\displaystyle\E\E_{\diamond}\frac{\langle f_{n}d_{k}^{1}\exp\big(\sum_{\ell\leq n}\theta_{k}^{\ell}\big)\rangle}{\langle\exp(\theta_{k})\rangle^{n}}=\E\E_{\diamond}\langle f_{n}\rangle\E\E_{\diamond}\frac{\langle d_{k}\exp(\theta_{k})\rangle}{\langle\exp(\theta_{k})\rangle}

(C.47)

for any
k\geq 1
and any function
f_{n}\in\mathsf{F}_{n}
, while the asymptotic version of (
C.36
) becomes

\E\big\langle(R_{1,\ldots,m}^{\infty}-\E\langle R_{1,\ldots,m}^{\infty}\rangle)^{2}\big\rangle>\epsilon

(C.48)

for some
1\leq m\leq\lfloor\epsilon^{-1}\rfloor
. We now derive the two most important consequences of the identities in (
C.46
) and (
C.47
) that will allow us to establish multi-overlap concentration. On the one hand, the concentration of the overlap
\E\langle(R_{1,2}^{\infty})^{2}\rangle=(\E\langle R_{1,2}^{\infty}\rangle)^{2}
implies that the system lies in a “thermal pure state” and that the function
\overline{\sigma}(w,u,v)
is therefore almost surely independent of
u
. The proof of this fact is taken from Theorem 3.1 in
[
62
]
.

Lemma C.9
.

If
\E\langle(R_{1,2}^{\infty})^{2}\rangle=(\E\langle R_{1,2}^{\infty}\rangle)^{2}
, then for almost all
u,v,w\in[0,1]
,

\overline{\sigma}(w,u,v)=\E_{u}\overline{\sigma}(w,u,v).

(C.49)

Proof.

Denote by
\cdot
the inner product on the Hilbert space (
C.40
),

\overline{\sigma}^{1}\cdot\overline{\sigma}^{2}=\E_{v}\overline{\sigma}^{1}(w,u_{1},v)\overline{\sigma}^{2}(w,u_{2},v)=R_{1,2}^{\infty},

and observe that

\displaystyle 0

\displaystyle=\E_{w,(u_{\ell})}\big(R_{1,2}^{\infty}\big)^{2}-\E_{w,(u_{\ell})}R_{1,2}^{\infty}R_{3,4}^{\infty}=\E_{w}\Var_{(u_{\ell})}\overline{\sigma}^{1}\cdot\overline{\sigma}^{2}.

It follows that for almost all
w\in[0,1]
, the inner product
\overline{\sigma}^{1}\cdot\overline{\sigma}^{2}
of any two replicas sampled from the Gibbs measure
G_{w}
is constant almost surely. In other words, the measure
G_{w}
is concentrated on a single function which may depend on
w
. This completes the proof.
∎

The second identity in (
C.46
) therefore implies that, instead of the equality in distribution (
C.37
), we actually have

\big(\sigma_{i}^{\ell}\big)_{i,\ell\geq 1}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{=}}\big(\sigma(w,v_{i},x_{i,\ell})\big)_{i,\ell\geq 1}

(C.50)

for any function
\sigma:[0,1]^{3}\to\{-1,+1\}
such that
\int_{0}^{1}\sigma(w,v,x)\,\mathrm{d}x=\overline{\sigma}(w,v)
. In particular, the Gibbs average (
C.42
) simplifies to

\langle\cdot\rangle=\E_{(x_{i,\ell})}

(C.51)

while the multi-overlap (
C.43
) becomes

R_{\ell_{1},\ldots,\ell_{n}}^{\infty}(w)=\E_{v}\prod_{j\leq n}\overline{\sigma}(w,v)=\E_{v}\big(\overline{\sigma}(w,v)^{n}\big)=\int_{0}^{1}\overline{\sigma}(w,v)^{n}\,\mathrm{d}v.

(C.52)

On the other hand, the asymptotic Franz-de Sanctis identities in (
C.47
) imply the following decoupling property of the asymptotic Gibbs measure. This is lemma 3.5 in
[
14
]
.

Lemma C.10

(A decoupling lemma)
.

Fix
\lambda\in\{\lambda_{k}\mid k\geq 1\}
. If
e_{1},e_{2}
are independent
\Exp(1)
random variables and, for
j=1,2
,

y_{j}=\frac{e_{j}}{1+\lambda\sigma_{j}^{\diamond}},\quad\theta_{j}=\log(1+\lambda\sigma_{j})-\lambda y_{j}\sigma_{j},\quad\text{and}\quad d_{j}=\frac{y_{j}\sigma_{j}}{1+\lambda\sigma_{j}^{\diamond}},

(C.53)

then

\E\E_{\diamond}\frac{\langle d_{1}\exp(\theta_{1})d_{2}\exp(\theta_{2})\rangle}{\langle\exp(\theta_{1})\exp(\theta_{2})\rangle}=\E\E_{\diamond}\frac{\langle d_{1}\exp(\theta_{1})\rangle}{\langle\exp(\theta_{1})\rangle}\E\E_{\diamond}\frac{\langle d_{2}\exp(\theta_{2})\rangle}{\langle\exp(\theta_{2})\rangle}.

(C.54)

Proof.

The idea is to combine a technical truncation argument with the Weierstrass approximation theorem and an application of (
C.47
) to the function
f_{n+1}=d_{2}^{1}\exp\sum_{\ell\leq n+1}\theta_{2}^{\ell}
; we encourage the interested reader to consult
[
14
]
.
∎

We are finally in a position to contradict (
C.48
) and prove
Proposition

C.6
. The calculations are very similar in spirit to those in
[
63
,
64
]
, and are taken from Theorem 2.2 in
[
14
]
.

Proof of
Proposition

C.6
.

We follow the proof of Theorem 2.2 in
[
14
]
; we will not give full details, and instead encourage the interested reader to consult
[
14
]
. Recall from (
C.50
) that
\smash{\sigma_{j}=\sigma(w,v_{j},x_{j})}
and
\smash{\sigma_{j}^{\diamond}=\sigma(w,v_{j},x_{j}^{\diamond})}
. Since all random variables indexed by
j=1,2
are independent, if we denote by
\smash{\E_{|w}=\E_{(e_{j}),(v_{j}),x_{j},x_{j}^{\diamond}}}
the conditional expectation given
w
and we introduce the random variable

Y(w)=\E_{|w}\frac{\langle d_{1}\exp(\theta_{1})\rangle}{\langle\exp(\theta_{1})\rangle}=\E_{|w}\frac{y_{1}}{1+\lambda\sigma_{1}^{\diamond}}\frac{\langle\sigma_{1}\exp(\theta_{1})\rangle}{\langle\exp(\theta_{1})\rangle},

which depends implicitly on
\lambda
through
y_{1}
and
\theta_{1}
, then (
C.54
) reads

\displaystyle\E\Big(\E_{|w}\frac{\langle d_{1}\exp(\theta_{1})\rangle}{\langle\exp(\theta_{1})\rangle}\Big)\Big(\E_{|w}\frac{\langle d_{2}\exp(\theta_{2})\rangle}{\langle\exp(\theta_{2})\rangle}\Big)-\Big(\E\frac{\langle d_{1}\exp(\theta_{1})\rangle}{\langle\exp(\theta_{1})\rangle}\Big)\Big(\E\frac{\langle d_{2}\exp(\theta_{2})\rangle}{\langle\exp(\theta_{2})\rangle}\Big)

\displaystyle=\E\Var_{|w}Y(w)

\displaystyle=0.

This means that
Y=\E Y
almost surely. To exploit this fact, through a slight abuse of notation, write
\sigma
for
\sigma_{1}
and observe that conditionally on
\sigma_{1}^{\diamond}
,

Y(w)=\E_{|w}\int_{0}^{\infty}\langle\exp(-\lambda\sigma y)\rangle\frac{\langle\sigma(1+\lambda\sigma)\exp(-\lambda y\sigma)\rangle}{\langle(1+\lambda\sigma)\exp(-\lambda y\sigma)\rangle}y\exp(-y)\,\mathrm{d}y.

Using the analyticity of both

g_{w}:\gamma\mapsto g_{w}(\gamma)=\E_{|w}\int_{0}^{\infty}\langle\exp(-\gamma\sigma y)\rangle\frac{\langle\sigma(1+\gamma\sigma)\exp(-\gamma y\sigma)\rangle}{\langle(1+\gamma\sigma)\exp(-\gamma y\sigma)\rangle}y\exp(-y)\,\mathrm{d}y

for a fixed
w
as well as its
w
-expectation
\E g_{w}(\gamma)
, it is possible to deduce that
Y(w)=\E Y
for all
\lambda
in a small neighbourhood of the origin. With this in mind, introduce the random variable

Z(w)=\E_{|w}\int_{0}^{\infty}\langle\sigma(1+\lambda\sigma)\exp(-\lambda\sigma)\rangle y\exp(-y)\,\mathrm{d}y

which is deterministic by the first identity in (
C.46
). This implies that the random variable

X(w)=\frac{Z(w)-Y(w)}{\lambda}=\E_{|w}\int_{0}^{\infty}\langle\sigma\exp(-\lambda y\sigma)\rangle\frac{\langle\sigma(1+\lambda\sigma)\exp(-\lambda y\sigma)\rangle}{\langle(1+\lambda\sigma)\exp(-\lambda y\sigma)\rangle}y\exp(-y)\,\mathrm{d}y

is deterministic for all
\lambda
in a small neighbourhood of the origin. In particular, all its
\lambda
-derivatives are also independent of
w
. We will now deduce from this observation that all multi-overlaps concentrate. Given
n\geq 1
, applying
\frac{\partial^{n}}{\partial\lambda^{n}}
to the denominator in the expression inside the integral defining
X
and evaluating at
\lambda=0
yields the term

n!R_{1,\ldots,n+2}\E e(e-1)^{n},

where
e
is an
\Exp(1)
random variable. Since
\E e(e-1)^{n}=\E(e-1)^{n+1}+\E(e-1)^{n}>0
for all
n\geq 1
, the term obtained by applying all derivatives to the denominator in the expression inside the integral defining
X
produces the multi-overlap
\smash{R_{1,\ldots,n+2}^{\infty}}
. If along the way we apply a derivative of
\lambda
to any factor other than the denominator, this will not create a new replica, so all those terms will produce a linear combination of multi-overlaps on strictly less than
n+2
replicas which by induction we assume to be independent of
w
. This establishes the concentration of all multi-overlaps and contradicts (
C.48
) thus completing the proof.
∎
